### Note on Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities are required to submit all annexes and supporting documents in detail provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the next submission.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 15 pages. Any page exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service time frame.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

### Underwriting and Funding Proposals

* Policy: project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. # A2.1 Executive Summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Cooling Facility is proposed as an innovative, multi-sector and multi-country programmatic financing mechanism that addresses the key sustainability challenges of providing access to cooling while minimizing negative climate impacts.

In a warming world, access to cooling — from cold chains and refrigeration to ensure the safety of foods, medicines, vaccines, and other goods needed to ensure comfortable, healthy and productive homes, institutions and workplaces — is not a luxury, as over 1 billion people globally are deemed at risk from the lack of it. The new global reality of climate change, characterized by more frequent extreme weather events, urbanization, and rising disposable income levels in developing countries — are driving the exponentially growing demand for cooling services in many developing countries, which is associated with a projected rise in energy consumption and greenhouse gas emissions.

The Cooling Facility will provide an innovative financing mechanism that can help to create opportunities of access to cooling services while avoiding maladaptation and limiting GHG emissions. The facility will support the deployment of climate-friendly cooling solutions, thereby creating an opportunity for driving scale, reducing costs and ultimately creating sustainable models for scaling up cooling in developing countries, contributing significantly towards the mitigation of climate change, meeting Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), plans to disaster risk reduction (DRR) from heatwaves) and supporting the goals of the Kigali Amendment.

Developing countries have many competing demands for capital — it is estimated that only 0.04 percent of total global investment in cooling infrastructure is directed towards developing countries — and the COVID-19 pandemic has further compounded these constraints. The onset of the global pandemic due to COVID-19 and its impact as a result of climate change, have highlighted the need for more resilient systems and infrastructure that can play in support of recovery efforts. This is reflected, for example, in the need for proper cooling and ventilation in healthcare facilities, food safety and security, and access to vaccines and medicines.

In this context, a dedicated Facility for cooling would target existing barriers that have so far limited access to cooling services in developing countries, and will place the support for a broader paradigm shift by working across key cooling target areas and with a range of countries from different parts of the developing world. The facility is designed to provide financing for the deployment of climate-friendly solutions at scale and mainstream sustainable cooling applications in a number of key sectors (agriculture, health, buildings), thereby accelerating the deployment of climate-friendly solutions. It will help countries face risks of heatwaves and other extreme weather events by providing access to affordable, reliable, efficient, safe and clean equipment, thereby contribute to adaptation efforts. At the same time, the Facility also aims to help improve energy efficiency in cooling systems, as well as reduce the carbon footprint associated with a lower carbon footprint. The Facility is designed to promote passive and low-carbon cooling in a more resilient way by providing access to affordable, reliable, efficient, safe and clean equipment that enhances access to climate-friendly and heat-resistant cooling solution, as well help strengthen enabling environments for the scale-up of climate-friendly cooling solutions. It is expected that the Cooling Facility will be able to provide financing for up to 10 million people in developing countries with strained energy systems, lowering local pollution, as well as helping lower losses of food and medicine.

In so doing, the Facility takes into account the cross-sectoral nature of cooling, the differing cooling needs per sector, and the need to address both demand-side and supply-side constraints. The facility will pilot and demonstrate implementation and business models and approaches that can be fit-for-purpose in more countries with similar circumstances, and thus provide a foundation to further scale-up access to cooling.

1 Kigali Cooling Efficiency Program (https://www.k-cop.org/why-cooling/)

**Page 11**

services and a shift to sustainable cooling investments, supporting mitigation objectives as well as goals of building resilience to increased temperatures, even beyond the scope of this Facility.

The Cooling Facility will support projects1 that meet the Facility’s criteria in the following cooling target areas (i) energy efficiency and demand-side management; (ii) adaptation to climate change through cooling strategies in the built environment, cooling equipment, building automation and controls, as well as solar and vegetative roofs and walls); and (iii) refrigeration, cold chains and logistics (including refrigeration, storage, and transportation of perishable goods).

### Components

The Cooling Facility consists of three main components which support activities to be adapted to the needs and contexts of each country that is part of the Facility:

#### Component 1 - Policy, regulatory and enabling environment support ($US17.7 million of GCF grant)

This component will support activities that aim to strengthen institutional, policy and regulatory frameworks; raise awareness and stimulate behavior changes, as well as build the capacity of key stakeholders to plan, finance and implement cooling projects in line with national policies and strategies for adaptation to climate change heatwaves and other extreme events. Enabling activities include cooling-specific inputs (related to both climate change and energy efficiency) that are included within the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) processes under the Paris Agreement such as Cooling Action Plans, as well as inputs on national policies and regulations that encourage sustainable cooling. It will also support activities linked to the Facility’s Gender Action Plan.

#### Component 2 - Financing for cooling investments ($US143.3 million of GCF funds, of which $125 million in loan and $16.3 million in grant)

The Facility will provide financing to eligible projects that invest in energy-efficient cooling technologies and systems, including solar-powered cooling solutions, as well as paths for cooling. concessional financing will foster the adoption or scale-up of the deployment of sustainable cooling solutions in line with national policies and strategies for adaptation to climate change heatwaves and other extreme events. The Facility will support eligible projects that meet the needs of the projects through suitable financing modalities for each project’s context, for example credit lines, loans, grants, guarantees, or loans to households, municipalities and small, medium and large enterprises, or subsidies.

#### Component 3 - Project management ($US50 million of GCF funds)

This component will support the Enhanced Cooling Facility in providing technical assistance during the preparation, implementation and implementation of their project activities, in compliance with the EEs and PIEs’ contractual obligations included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into with the Accredited Entities (AE).

### Facility’s impact

The Facility will provide critical support to the transition to sustainable cooling through the mobilization of approximately US$37.9 million in financing (including both GCF and IDA/IDA). It is expected to avoid or reduce up to 10,524 tons of CO2 emissions per year, equivalent to the reduction achieved by the Facility over 16.2 Mio°C over the 27-year lifespan of the Facility’s underlying assets), strengthen local capacities, and policy and regulatory frameworks in line with national policies and strategies for adaptation to climate change heatwaves and other extreme events. The Facility will support projects that meet NDCs, and importantly reach approximately 4.2 million direct beneficiaries across the 4 countries most vulnerable to extreme heat hazards.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### i. Rising global temperatures, cooling needs and GHG emissions

##### a. Global perspective

The world is off-track to meet the 1.5°C objective of the Paris agreement. The global demand for cooling resulting from rising temperatures is projected to increase by more than three times in the next two decades due to population increases. The consequent increases in GHG emissions as a result of increased energy use and refrigerants will be significant, especially in developing countries – particularly in the world’s hotter regions that are increasingly susceptible to extreme heatwaves and related weather shocks - further contribute to the projected exponential increase in the demand for cooling and its associated energy consumption and GHGs.

There is currently an estimated 3.6 billion cooling appliances in use; a figure projected to increase to 9.5 billion by 2050 (Dufres et al., 2018). The cost of cooling appliances will continue to decline, making them more affordable than those who can afford it), which could require in the order of 14 billion cooling appliances (Dufres et al., 2018).

Greater and rapid access to affordable cooling has become a development necessity. In 2019, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) reported that at 1.5°C of warming, 2.3 billion people could be exposed to heatwaves by 2040; this number is projected to increase to 6.7 billion in 2080 (IPCC, 2021). SEforAll (2020) estimates over 1 billion people in developing countries are already facing immediate risks from lack of access to cooling, which includes 699 million slum dwellers living in hotter-climate urban areas where they have no access to air conditioning or other forms of cooling. This is particularly problematic for rural areas without access to cold chains for storage and preservation of food and medicine. This situation will only worsen as the global population increases, with an additional 2 billion people expected by 2050 (IPCC, 2021). The World Bank estimates that 38% of the world’s population lives in low- or middle-income countries – figures that are likely under-estimates as projections assume that the increase in fertility rates will be similar to those observed between 1960 and 2015. This is not a realistic assumption given the rapid decline in fertility rates across most of Africa, Asia, and Latin America (UNFPA, 2021). The World Bank estimates that by 2030 the worst affected regions of South Asia and West Africa. The worse affected sectors will be construction and agriculture – which in many countries will disproportionately affect women given their predominant roles.

The global demand for cooling resulting from this temperature rise – which is trending towards higher than the 1.5°C target set under the Paris agreement according to multiple reports - is generating a vicious cycle as temperatures increase, leading to further increases in energy use and refrigerants, leading to further increases in GHG emissions because of increased fossil-fuel driven energy use and high pollulant refrigerants such as HFCs. This will be particularly problematic for developing countries – especially in the world’s hotter regions that are increasingly susceptible to extreme heatwaves and related weather shocks - further contribute to the projected exponential increase in the demand for cooling and its associated energy consumption and GHGs.

##### b. Sector context

The scale of the cooling challenge is significant across many sectors as illustrated below.


---
**Page 13**
---

Agriculture and rural development. Higher temperatures contribute to droughts that can increase undernourishment through impact on crop yields resulting in malnutrition and affecting food security in many developing countries where agriculture is a major source of income and employment for millions of people along with other climate change impacts. The GHG emissions associated with food loss and waste contribute to about 4.4 GtCO2eq per year, representing 8-10 percent of total GHG emissions from 2010 to 2016 (Mbow, C., et al. 2017). This is a major concern for the world’s urban poor who are increasingly vulnerable as they move into urban centers struggling to access fresh fruits, vegetables, dairy meat and fish, making the existing gaps felt even more acutely. The food sector is also one of the largest emitters of methane (CH4), which is 28 times more technologically important would have serious adverse effects on climate change as a result of increased GHG emissions from agriculture and other land use sectors. This includes areas where livestock production systems stand-out, and are also areas where integrating efficient, clean cooling (from efficient building construction, improved cooling and refrigeration systems powered with renewable energy) could support low-carbon development.

Health. The health sector is a priority. Hospitals and many health facilities are often open 24 hours a day and require heating, ventilation and air conditioning (HVAC) systems to control room temperature, pressurization, air quality, humidity levels, and other factors that can impact patient care and the ability of healthcare workers for patients while also providing a high level of protection for healthcare personnel. The increased electricity load from these HVAC systems is often met by diesel generators or other fossil fuel-based power generation, increasing costs for health facilities so they can dedicate scarce resource for treatments. Rural clinics may need to be built with solar PV and battery storage to provide reliable power supply. This is especially important when needs for cooling (and other uses) can be met with distributed renewable energy. The availability of sustainable, affordable, and reliable electricity in the health sector is critical as climate change impacts become more urgent. The global healthcare sector, with its need for temperature control facilities and services for vaccines, medicines, and other medical supplies, is particularly vulnerable to climate change (WHO, 2021). A well-functioning cold chain and health system infrastructure is essential; it is also an opportunity to build national capacity and sustainability to put countries’ health systems on a more sustainable and climate-resilient path.

The COVID-19 pandemic has shed light on – and compounded - the pre-pandemic inequities in the health cold chain and the challenges in achieving universal immunization coverage, especially in Africa. It has been estimated that the global vaccine supply will be insufficient to meet demand for 2021 (WHO, 2021). The Gavi Alliance estimates that $34 billion annually – a figure that does not include the physical and financial cost associated with achieving universal immunization coverage - is needed to ensure equitable access to vaccines. This is happening around the world, causing hardship practically everywhere, but with particularly severe risks for the poorest countries in Africa and South Asia. The pandemic has also highlighted the need for more resilient health systems globally, especially in developing countries where health facilities are already lacking in capacity and reach, but also in other sectors and across the economy. It is clear that priorities of countries are shifting. A multi-sectoral approach to climate change adaptation will be needed to ensure that health systems, water, food, energy, housing, and other systems plan a recovery. The World Bank Group has been responding accordingly by supporting countries with $10 billion in 2020 and $6 billion in the first half of 2021, including an envelope of $12 billion for vaccine purchase and deployments, which could help cover vaccination costs as well as support health systems to adapt to climate change impacts such as heat stress and cold infrastructure. This overall $16 billion support is tailored to the health, economic and social shocks countries are facing and includes over $50 billion of risk resources on grant and highly concessional terms (Global COVID-19 MPA) (P173789).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 12 OF 83

## B

### Refrigerants
About 20 percent of the climate impact comes from the direct emissions of hydrofluorocarbons (HFC) refrigerants. Further, this category of refrigerants is the most responsible for 15-20 percent of global warming potential (GWP). The largest HFC impacts are in the growing dimension of cooling across sectors, as efforts to only address the climate impact of refrigerants (under the Montreal Protocol) have not been sufficient to reduce the overall GWP. In fact, if HFCs continue to be used gas widely instead of refrigerants, by more than 80 percent over 30 years), would leave out the largest share of the warming potential from cooling. The use of HFCs in air conditioning and refrigeration could increase to 1.5°C or greater of global warming by 2100. If combined with efforts to maximize energy efficiency in cooling equipment, close to 1°C of global warming can be avoided by the end of the century (J. Dryfus et al., 2020).

### Buildings
Cooling is the fastest growing end-use in building. Forty percent of the world’s population resides within buildings and this number will increase to over half by 2050, and this figure is projected to grow to 50 percent by 2050*. The use of air conditioners and electric fans is becoming increasingly common in developing countries, where cooling demand is expected to rise from about 10-30 percent of total global electricity consumption (IEA, 2018). Globally, the energy consumption of buildings could double by 2050. The building sector accounts for over one-third of all final energy use and almost 40 percent of all global electricity consumption. The global energy use for space cooling is projected to grow 300% between 2016 and 2050, and by then space cooling will account for more than half of the total building sector’s energy demand. This means that additional demand. Improvements are needed for the existing build stock to become less energy and GHG intensive. The new buildings (keeping in mind that half of the buildings that we will standing in 2050 have not yet been built) and the major renovations should be designed considering cooling as a lower carbon energy and GHG footprint.

### Energy
About 80 percent of the climate impact of cooling equipment is associated with energy use (i.e. the electricity used to power the cooling system from fossil-fuel based electricity generation). Much of the use of energy for cooling is in peak demand which is typically the least efficient and carbon intensive and driving the need for more renewable energy sources. The cost of solar PV has fallen by 80% since 2010, and it is expected to fall by about 45 percent in 2050, up from 70 percent today. Globally, space cooling-associated emissions from electricity use are projected to increase by a factor of five between now and 2050. The majority of the world’s middle class in developing countries are on the verge of purchasing the most affordable – and also least efficient – air conditioners available (IEA, 2018). In addition, the number of people living in cities is projected to increase by 9.5 billion by 2050 — and this would not even provide cooling for all who will need it in a warming world (as opposed to only those who can afford it), which could require in the order of 14 billion tonnes of CO2 equivalent emissions from space cooling alone, compared with current global space cooling emissions by 38-60 GtCO2e by 2050 and up to 130-260 GtCO2e by 2050.

### c. Country-specific context
Each of the countries covered by the Facility need to adapt to a warming world as they are exposed to risks of water scarcity, heat stress, and different intensity winds, which translates into a growing demand for cooling. For example, Bangladesh, Malawi, Somalia, and El Salvador are exposed to a high risk, while at risk from lack of cooling.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 14 OF 83

## Malawi
Between 1960 and 2006, mean annual temperature increased by an average of 0.5°C per decade. Over this period the total number of hot days in a year have increased.

Mean annual temperature will rise to 1.58°C for 2030-2059 and 2.47°C at the end of the century. The number of days with mean daily maximum temperatures >35°C is expected to increase by about 22 days by mid-century, and by 62 days by the end of the century.

Projected change in annual cooling degree days (CDD) are projected to rise from 480 CDD between 1980-2005, and to 730 CDD by the end of the century.

## Somalia
Between 1960 and 2006, mean annual temperature increased by an average of 0.5°C per decade in the hottest months (April and May) and coldest months (January and December).

Mean annual temperature is projected to increase by 1.3°C at mid-century and 2.4°C at the end of the century, with a total increase of 70 days.

Annual cooling degree days are expected to rise by 836 CDD between 1980-2005, and to 1182 CDD by the end of the century.


The rationale for climate change adaptation of the countries at high risk extreme heat hazard is presented below.

Bangladesh: Bangladesh is highly exposed to extreme heat, drought, and wildfires. Extreme heat hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates prolonged exposure to extreme heat resulting in health impacts such as heat stroke, dehydration, and heat exhaustion. The number of days with Trmx ≥ 36°C has been an increase since 1960 in most parts of Bangladesh, as highlighted in Table 1. Average temperatures have been increasing more rapidly during the monsoon season from June to August, which is consistent with the global trend. The average temperature for the month of July has increased by mid-century, and by 1.9°C by the end of the century, with warming more pronounced during winter months (December-February). The number of days when mean daily maximum temperature exceeds 35°C is projected to increase from 13.23 days in 2009, 22.77 day to 2040-59, and by 41.1 days by 2099. By mid-century, annual cooling degree days (CDD) are projected to rise from 482 CDD between 1980-2005, and to 736 CDD by the end of the century.

On average, Bangladesh is affected by a major country-wide drought every five years. There have been 19 major droughts between 1960 and 1991 that have affected around 53% of the population and 47% of the country’s total area. The most severe drought occurred in 2008-09, through the northeastern and northwestern region. Climate change is projected to exacerbate drought conditions in Bangladesh, especially

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 15 OF 83

## B

In the northern hemisphere, areas of the country that currently experience moderate droughts will likely turn into severely drought-prone areas in the next 20-30 years.[1] This can put significant stress on the energy system as it is likely to increase energy demand while decreasing energy supply reliability. With higher air temperature, wildfires are expected to become more frequent and severe.

This means that there is greater than 50% chance of encountering weather that could support a significant wildfire event in the next decade. The frequency of extreme heat events will also increase, as well as increases in the frequency of fire weather occurrences, including an increase in temperature and greater variability in precipitation. In addition to this, there may be more frequent occurrence of days with weather that supports fire spread and longer periods of drought during fire seasons.

Projections indicate that there could be an increase in the severity of fire, and even areas that are not currently at risk of wildfires will likely experience increased fire activity.

These changes in climate will only lead to increased energy demand for both space cooling and cold storage. This increase will be on top of energy demand growth driven by increasing urbanization and rise in income levels. In addition, the projected increase in temperature will require more cooling capacity within buildings; efficient air conditioning and ventilation will be needed, especially to protect vulnerable segments of the population, such as children, the elderly and the sick during hot days and heatwaves.

Furthermore, various countries are expected to experience an increase in extreme weather events. These include increased frequency and intensity of heavy precipitation, more frequent occurrence of droughts, and increases in temperature across regions. Therefore, the Facility will support investments in sustainable cooling solutions as essential adaptation measures for climate change.

### El Salvador

El Salvador is highly exposed to extreme heat, hurricanes, droughts, and wildfires. Extreme heat hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates that prolonged exposure to extreme heat, resulting from increased temperature, can cause significant health impacts on vulnerable segments of society.[2] It has been reported that the temperature increase in El Salvador has been more pronounced than in other parts of the world, with the temperature increase since 1950s of 3.1°C compared to 0.8°C globally[3]. El Salvador experienced a sustained period of high temperatures from April to August during 2017 and 2018 (the warmest months/months; i.e., April to August) have witnessed increased increase in temperature. The average temperature recorded for the month of May was 29.5°C, which is an increase of 3.4°C from the long-term mean.

Looking into future, mean annual temperature is projected to increase by 1.2°C by 2050. Most notably, the country will experience a significant increase in extreme heat events with the average number of days per year exceeding 30 increasing from 68 days at the beginning of the 21st century and the probability of heat waves will increase by 30% to 210%. The annual cooling degree days are projected to rise by 46.81 CDD by 2050, and 137.68 CDD by the end of the century.[4]

El Salvador is situated in the dry corridor of Central America, and prolonged dry periods often result in droughts. The country has experienced severe droughts in recent years, with the most recent being in 2015-2016, which was characterized by severe, moderate or extreme drought conditions. The probability of severe drought will grow by 36% by 2059, compared to a current probability of 4%. This is expected to have significant impacts on water supply and energy demand while decreasing energy supply reliability (which have a detrimental impact on key health services and vaccine safety). Additionally, the annual precipitation is predicted to decrease by -65.3mm by 2059.

Similar to other countries in Central America, El Salvador has seen a steady increase in the intensity and frequency of extreme weather events and storms in the last 30 years. Due to its social and economic vulnerability, economic assets and infrastructure in El Salvador are at high risk of damage from hurricanes. An analysis by the World Bank shows that for every one-degree Celsius rise in temperature, there is a maximum loss at US$374million (1.5% of GDP). Adding to that, the frequency and intensity of hurricanes and tropical storms have increased over time. This has led to an increase in the number of extreme weather events across the country. This means that there is greater than 50% chance of encountering weather that could support a significant increase in temperature which can result in lost life and property damage any given year: warming climate, rising sea level rise, combined with the effects of El Niño. The impact of the EL Nino phenomenon on El Salvador will likely put additional stress on the country’s energy system, especially considering that 35% of El Salvador's electricity comes from hydropower (2). This might be impacted by droughts and decreases in annual precipitation.

These changes in climate will only lead to a significant increase in demand for both base load generation and peak load generation driven by increased consumption and rise in income levels. Efficient air conditioning and ventilation will be needed, in particular to protect vulnerable segments of the population, such as children, the elderly and the sick during hot days and heatwaves. Further, it is expected that there will be an increase in demand for cooling systems in buildings and industries which will need to create the ambient temperature ranges for optimal functionality and to safely preserve medicines, as well as other goods. The increased demand for cooling systems will also lead to an increase in peak load demand will be exacerbated by the supply variability from hydropower as a result of droughts and decrease in annual precipitation that may put additional stress on the power system.

Therefore, the investment in energy efficiency projects undertaken by the Facility will not only help adapt to extreme temperature hazard on the demand side (for space cooling and cold chains), but also help reduce system stress due to hydropower variability, which will help safeguard the efficacy of vaccines and medicines, as well as other goods.

**Page 19**
---

Beyond extreme temperatures, droughts and floods are two of the most severe of climate hazards in Malawi, which have only increased in frequency and severity over the past twenty years. On average, 1.5 million people are affected by water scarcity every year, mainly in the central and southern parts of the country[2], and close to half a million people live below the poverty line (the national poverty line is US$0.97 per day)[3]. In Malawi, where about half of the population lives below the national poverty line, as severe droughts (10–15 year droughts) are more likely to occur in the future[4], and extreme heat events will become more frequent and last longer than those experienced today. Drought conditions are a significant risk to the country’s energy supply as 70% of its electricity is generated from hydroelectric power plants, which are highly vulnerable to prolonged droughts and below the poverty line[5]. Drought conditions are also a significant demand for water in the country. Such conditions are worsened by extreme temperatures and wildfires. Even when looking at the next few decades, wildfire hazard is projected to be a high risk throughout the country given that in any given year, 10% of Malawi’s land area is burned[6]. Wildfires can also lead to increased transmission of malaria and other vector-borne diseases as well as increase the spread of invasive species such as wildfires. Extreme heat events will put additional stress on the country’s energy system, especially coal-fired power plants which are already struggling with water scarcity in the region. The agriculture sector is also vulnerable to droughts and decrease in annual precipitation thus putting additional stress on the power system in a number of ways.

These changes in climate will have a negative impact on crop, economic activities, and agriculture in particular – particularly for smallholder farmers who make up 80% of the working population and are vulnerable to extreme shocks. Adaptation measures such as improved irrigation systems, drought-resistant crops, and other agricultural products will be critical for farmers and for avoiding food security problems. This will be another factor contributing to increasing energy demand with rising temperatures and increased extreme heat events. The power sector is also expected to face additional stress from the increase in water scarcity and lower river flow as a result of droughts and decrease in annual precipitation thus putting additional stress on the power system, in a number of ways.

As the agriculture sector is both one of the most vulnerable to climate change and one of the largest contributors of GHG emissions in the country, facilitating the deployment of climate-friendly (including energy efficient) agricultural technologies will be critical for reducing greenhouse gas emissions while also increasing carbon-and climate-resilient economy. The support provided through the Cooling Facility will not only help adapt to extreme temperature hazard on the cold chains for the agriculture sector but also help increase agricultural productivity, reduce power system stress due to hydropower variability and avoid the use of diesel.

**Malawi**

Malawi is generally arid and semi-arid with two seasonal rainfall seasons. The country’s climate is influenced by its location in the tropics and the surrounding large bodies of water such as Lake Malawi, and conditions of the neighboring Indian Ocean and Red Sea. Mean annual temperatures was about 27°C in the period from 1961-2013[8], with June to December being the hottest months in the northern part of the country and July to October being the hottest in the southern part of Malawi in 2020, with maximum temperature above 0.5°C in the hottest months (April and May) and 0.5°C in the coldest months (January and December). By mid-century, mean annual temperature is projected to increase by 1.3°C and by around 1.7°C by the end of the


---
**Page 20**
---

century across areas of Somalia**. In addition, the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) influences Somalia’s variable climate in several ways, bringing more rainfall and flooding during El Niño and droughts in La Niña years*. Majority of Somalia is at risk of several natural hazards, including extreme heat, drought, floods, and vector-borne diseases. The most significant hazard to the population is extreme heat.

For example, extreme heat hazard is considered high, with prolonged exposure to extreme heat that results in heat stress and heat stroke. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), the number of days where maximum temperature exceeds 35°C will increase by about 70 days by 2059 relative to the period 1986-2005, and by no less than 40 days in 2080. The number of days expected to rise to more than 38°C is projected to increase from 1 day per year (in 1986–2005) to 1182 CDD by the end of this century. This increase in temperatures will impact the energy sector as there will be higher power demand for cooling.

Beyond extreme temperatures, droughts and floods pose the most direct hazards to the country. According to the World Bank, Somalia has experienced more frequent and severe drought episodes since 1965. Somalia experienced a severe drought event in 2011, which resulted in 258,000 deaths of livestock and 3 million people were displaced by the end of that year. The country also experienced another severe drought episode from June to October 2012. Continued increases in temperature could have vast impacts on the Somali population, livestock, and crop yields. Heat stress could also alter livestock’s feed intake, health, mortality rates, agricultural production, and animal welfare.

These changes in climate will only lead to a significant increase in demand for both space cooling and cold storage. This increase will be on top of energy demand growth driven by increasing urbanization and rise in income levels. The projected increase in cooling demand is expected to have the most severe impacts on segments of the population, such as children, the elderly and the sick during hot days and heatwaves. Further, in addition to the direct health effects associated with extreme temperatures, there will be indirect effects to create the ambient temperature ranges for optimal functionality, ensure safe and healthy indoor environments, and to preserve medications and vaccines. This will be another factor contributing to increasing energy demand.

In summary, there is an opportunity to set the course for affordable and sustainable cooling pathways now. The proposed co-funded investments in the Facility’s Somalia project will help improve the quality of health care services by ensuring that more people have access to essential medicines and vaccines within the ministry’s depleted budget. The investments in sustainable cooling systems supported by the Cooling Facility will not only help reduce the vulnerability of the population to extreme temperature hazard on the ground (for space cooling and cold chains), but also make it possible for countries to improve health outcomes in the country’s population by making available medicines and vaccines longer to more people.

II. Cooling Facility strategic approach

The wide range of cooling needs and priorities implies an equally diverse set of challenges and solutions. Many of these challenges are interlinked, and there is no one-size-fits-all solution or approach. Given the nascent and still early stage focus on cooling (globally) to address for both development and climate resilience, it is important that the Cooling Facility supports a range of activities that can be implemented within countries’ efforts and priorities to respond to the COVID crisis. In fact, it is hoped that the diversity of the Cooling Facility’s portfolio will allow for lessons learned from these investments to be shared with other related to cooling in different contexts. The Facility aims to support different approaches and generate lessons that can then be adapted and scaled more broadly to enhance access to affordable cooling while at same time

kick start a conversation towards more sustainable cooling, offering examples to be emulated and adapted across countries beyond the scope of the Facility. Operationally, the ratio of cooling needs is such that often investments will consist of projects with larger scale, which can be a key leverage for investments to address cold stress issues in health, food security, and other productive uses; an example would be suitable cold chain investments will typically be part of a broader projects targeting support to a country's health sector.

Where growing cooling demand is creating pressures on country energy systems and upward pressures on GHG emissions, the priority is to support space cooling, both in terms of passive measures (such as building design) and active measures (such as air conditioning). The ESMAP Clean Cooling Facility has supported the case for Bangladesh*, Sri Lanka, Panama, North Macedonia). In agriculture-dominant economies with needs to expand access cooling to rural and off-grid areas, the priority is to support efficient and climate friendly cold chain infrastructure.

In hot climate countries, ensuring health infrastructure is adequate for patients and storage of pharmaceuticals is a priority, as pharmacueticals expire and become ineffective more quickly in hot climatic conditions. Efficient and climate-friendly cold chain infrastructure can also be used to store vaccines, including the transportation of vaccines while making them more resilient to warmer temperatures, in addition to keeping their carbon footprint low.

Overall, the Cooling Facility is aligned and supports countries' climate change goals and ambitions, as reflected in their respective climate change nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

### III. Related initiatives / programs

The Cooling Facility draws on and coordinates with other initiatives and programs related to cooling, which are funded through other financiers but are not part of the Fund Activities.

The ESMAP Efficient, Clean Cooling program (ECCP) is a multi-sector technical assistance (TA) program designed to support countries in implementing their commitments under the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol Union (united with the Bank's Environment, Natural Resources and Blue Economy Global Practice). It was established in 2019 with funding from the World Bank Group. The ECCP is a multi-sector initiative that aims at starting a new business line within the WBG by supporting the design and inclusion of efficient, clean cooling components in projects across all sectors. The ECCP also seeks to develop a technical assistance program to raise awareness and disseminate knowledge. The ECCP also seeks to develop strategic partnerships with and within the WBG and help mobilize funding. Under ESMAP's new business plan for FY21-24, the Efficient Cold Cooling Facility (ECCF) will continue to support countries in implementing their commitments under the program activities and develop a pipeline of projects across the World Bank's Global Practices. The ESMAP Efficient Clean Cooling Facility (ECCF) is a multi-sector technical assistance facility that supports country-level project activities (e.g., case studies) to share experiences and lessons from World Bank projects, including those supported by the facility - both among the beneficiaries of the projects and other key national and international partners.

* Bangladesh also ranks among the top 10 countries with lower-middle income populations on the basis of purchasing the most affordable air conditioner or refrigerator – and least energy efficient – on the market. cooling is a critical component in ensuring access to health care, food security, and other productive uses of the system.

The listed programs and initiatives, while having the potential to strengthen project's impact and producing analytical and knowledge products which will inform project design, are not part of the Cooling Facility nor the program activities.

## B

stakeholders as well as with other countries– to help raise awareness and inform further discussion of sustainable cooling more broadly.

The Climate Investment Funds (CIF). The Cooling Facility builds on synergies with the CIF, a multi-donor fund administered by the World Bank. For example, CIF funding could address the barriers to the deployment of sustainable cooling solutions in developing countries, such as lack of regulatory capacity and access to finance for productive uses. The Cooling Facility will continue to identify and leverage partnerships with the CIF to achieve its goals.

The Bank's Leap Energy proposal for Africa30 seeks to help reach universal energy access by 2030. It specifically aims to promote conditions for increased use of productive efficient refrigeration and cooling equipment in Africa, which can be used to power small-scale enterprises and other productive uses. This will help boost both household income and energy demand – and in doing so help strengthen the business case for investment in sustainable cooling solutions.

The ESMAP Gender and Energy Program is helping to strengthen women’s roles as consumers, employees, and entrepreneurs in the energy sector. Aligned with the WBG Gender Strategy (P1623), the program works with governments and other stakeholders to increase access to energy services and products for women in the sector and improve development outcomes. The ESMAP Gender and Energy Program will provide support to countries in developing gender-sensitive policies, programs, and strategies that promote more informed projects, including in new areas, such as women in energy leadership and productive uses of energy.

## B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change diagram (Figure 1) presents the logical underpinning of the Facility.

There are multiple barriers and risks to the scale-up deployment of climate-friendly cooling solutions. The first barrier, i.e. lack of regulatory/policy framework for sustainable cooling system along with limited institutional capacity for implementation, is a major challenge in many countries. This can be addressed by fostering a transformative change in the cooling sector. However, the extent to which market transformation can occur will depend on how quickly and effectively these barriers are removed. The second barrier relates to consumption patterns or in approaches to meet their cooling needs. Low demand for sustainable cooling using energy efficient appliances is another major challenge. This can be addressed by increasing awareness of the benefits of climate-friendly cooling, and the absence of suitable business models and market mechanisms being a third barrier.

Meeting the demand for climate-friendly cooling solutions requires access to sufficient and affordable sources of financing. Gains in financing for investments in energy efficient buildings and efficient and climate friendly equipment are critical to achieving the goals of the Africa Energy Access Leap (AEAL) and the ESMAP (Collaborative Labelling and Appliance Standards), consumer affordability is the most significant constraint for many consumers, especially those living on low incomes. This can be addressed by providing access of an off-grid appliance without upfront financing. On the supply side, producers and retailers struggle to lower the retail price due to high distribution costs. Access to finance for consumers, product innovators, and other stakeholders is another barrier.

In this context, the Facility will support activities that address and help remove regulatory/policy-related constraints in the cooling sector by (i) providing technical assistance and building the capacity of state and non-state actors (such as commercial banks, private investors and technical companies), end user beneficiaries); (ii) supporting the design of sustainable cooling solutions; (iii) increasing awareness about climate-friendly cooling solutions and their benefits of low-carbon cooling solutions; (iv) facilitating access to affordable sources of financing; and v)

The Africa Energy Access Leap is a program proposing a WBG global effort to help reach universal access to modern energy services in Africa by 2030. The leap will be achieved through three pillars: (i) access to modern energy at all); the proposal is currently under development.

Official Use

In turn, these activities will strengthen the capacity of key stakeholders, stimulate the demand for climate-friendly cooling solutions, foster the penetration of climate-friendly equipment and technologies in the market, and improve access to cooling services.

The Facility seeks to provide projects that will strengthen institutional and regulatory systems, improve access to cooling, improve the adaptive capacity of end user beneficiaries to respond to rising temperatures, as well as the energy efficiency of cooling buildings and appliances. The Facility is designed to be flexible enough to adapt to changing circumstances in order to take advantage of opportunities where the carbon intensity of buildings, cold chains and cooling appliances decreases.

The Facility seeks to provide projects that will strengthen institutional and regulatory systems, improve access to cooling services which is critical to building resilience in a warming world while also contributing to climate change mitigation. At a time when there are many pressing demands on developing country governments with limited resources, the Facility aims to build on synergies between sustainable cooling and other sector and development priorities to trigger the changes needed for long-term sustainability of cooling solutions.

## Results

### Result 1: Strengthened institutional and regulatory capacity
- Improved capacity of local user beneficiaries to adapt to the use of cooling facilities.
- Improved energy efficiency of buildings, leading to reduced electricity consumption.

### Result 2: Increased access to affordable cooling services
- By strengthening the capacity of decision-makers, policies can be designed and adapted to ensure that climate-friendly cooling is provided in a cost-effective manner.
- Access to adequate financing sources (individuals, households, businesses) for the implementation of cooling solutions.
- Improved energy efficiency of buildings, leading to reduced electricity consumption.

### Result 3: Increased capacity of both non-state actors (e.g., banks, energy service providers) and state actors in providing climate-friendly cooling services
- Access to adequate financing sources (individuals, households, businesses) for the implementation of cooling solutions.
- Improved energy efficiency of buildings, leading to reduced electricity consumption.

B.3. Programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

# Objectives and goals

The World Bank’s proposed Cooling Facility is a multi-sector, multi-country financing mechanism as part of its effort to help countries address cooling needs and bring to scale sustainable cooling solutions across key sectors. It aims to support the eligible countries for:

- Mobilize additional financing to support transition and market transformation in support of the most climate-friendly means of meeting cooling needs;
- Enhance access to energy efficient and climate friendly cooling services;
- Strengthen national capacity, including financial resources, for sustainable cooling solutions;
- Raise public awareness of the benefits, financial viabilities and positive impacts that result from investment in cooling infrastructure and services.

Build and strengthen skills and capacity among the range of stakeholders involved in the deployment of cooling technologies and services;

Integrate and mainstream cooling into both long-term climate planning for mitigation and adaptation and Sendai framework disaster risk reduction (DRR) planning, and support the achievement of the global Agenda commitments.

In doing so, the Facility will:

- Contribute to multiple Sustainable Development Goals;
- Mitigate the climate impacts of meeting the projected increased cooling demand; iii) promote interest and support for innovative means of providing cooling services; and also iv) contribute to improving climate resilience in developing countries by supporting their efforts to implement national adaptation plans as National Adaptation Plans and Disaster Risk Reduction Plans, while also delivering a wide range of co-benefits including improved health outcomes, increased energy security, and enhanced economic productivity.

Recognizing the magnitude of financial needs, the proposed Facility is the first phase, which will expand to be fully operational in 2018.

To achieve this goal, the Facility will channel concessional climate finance from the GCF to co-finance IBRD- and IDA-financed operations that:

- Support investments in efficient, affordable and sustainable cooling;
- Facilitate access to financing and business model innovations across cooling value chains and sectors to reduce barriers for private sector investment;
- Help countries integrate cooling into national systems and climate change adaptation plans;
- Support the integration of sustainable cooling into long-term plans to address climate change, DRR and other development priorities.

At country level, the Facility development objectives will be adapted to reflect the specific needs and priorities of countries and thereby to align the objectives and impacts of the investments.

Figure 2 provides an overview of the Cooling Facility’s overall framework, including its goals to contribute to the transformation of the cooling sector through policy, regulatory and enabling environment support that would help countries to achieve their climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) targets in the key cooling target areas, i.e., (a) space cooling*; and green/cool surfaces; as well as (b) refrigeration, cold

The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030 provides Member States with concrete actions to protect development gains from the risk of disaster, including heatwaves.

*Space cooling: The use of air conditioning or other cooling technologies in buildings and spaces where people are provided thermal comfort from heat by maintaining the optimum temperature, humidity, and ventilation within the built environment (See ESMAP. Primer on Space Cooling). See below for more details.

# Overview of the Cooling Facility

## Space Cooling and Coolgreen solutions

*Space cooling* refers to the means by which people are provided thermal comfort from heat by maintaining the optimum temperature, humidity, and ventilation within the built environment.

Coolgreen solutions refer to the use of natural elements (e.g., green roofs, walls, and surfaces) in buildings to build cooling loads (energy efficient building construction and design, including building thermal insulation and shading devices), reduce energy consumption, and provide cooling through passive means such as evaporative cooling systems; and (ii) building automation, controls and good operation and maintenance practices for the use of natural elements.

Space cooling projects may:

* Improve the thermal efficiency of buildings (to reduce or avoid cooling loads).
* Reduce the need for mechanical cooling.
* Contribute to energy savings in buildings by reducing the load on air conditioning systems.
* Be implemented locally, in line with the goals and requirements of the Kigali Amendment; and may also (iv) further contribute to climate change mitigation.

Space cooling projects may target greenfield investments or refurbishments in different sectors, i.e., public (including health facilities, education, and administrative buildings), residential (multi-family and single family), commercial or industrial.


## Examples

* For example, off-grid cooling appliances could be deployed in the context of broader energy access programs, e.g. the deployment of solar-powered technologies for heating, cooking, and cooling.
* In rural areas, farms, rural schools and health facilities. Such deployment may be in the context of products of electricity, which are often used to power refrigerators, freezers, and other appliances that can provide cooling.
* Space cooling projects could also contribute directly or indirectly to climate change mitigation by reducing excessive Urban Heat Island (https://www.esmap.org/primmer-for-space-cooling-report)


---
**Page 27**
---

Refrigeration, cold chain and logistics

Refrigeration and cold chain refer to managing the temperature (at specified low-temperature range) of perishable goods in order to maintain their quality and safety from the point of origin through the distribution chain to the final consumer[1]. Refrigeration has many applications, including household refrigerators, industrial freezers, transport refrigerated containers, mobile cold storage units, and cold rooms for food preparation equipment and logistics. The projects may target energy efficient refrigeration equipment and facilities in residential, public, commercial and industrial sectors.

For the health cold chain, projects may target the different components of cold chains, i.e., national and regional level hospitals, district-level stores and local clinics and vaccination outreach. The projects may include efficient, clean built infrastructure (e.g., walk-in refrigerators and freezers) and cold storage equipment such as mobile cold rooms, transport refrigerated containers, etc.

Similarly, for food cold chains (i.e., in agriculture and fisheries sectors), projects may include efficient, clean cold chain equipment linked efficient, clean build infrastructures (e.g. refrigerated warehouses, hubs, and packhouses) with storage equipment and logistics systems.

Projects will also address the entire life cycle of the cold chain (infrastructure and cold storage equipment). Project may also include digital devices and to monitor and track temperature and performance of the equipment, as well as logistics planning systems to enhance overall efficiency of the cold chain. An eligible project component is the development of a national or regional cold chain strategy that addresses gaps in existing facilities or target new equipment and facilities.

## Program components

The Cooling Facility is composed of three complementary components that will be adapted to each eligible country’s context and cooling area, and will be differentiated according to its financing needs in accordance with the Facility criteria. The components are structured to help address countries the barriers to sustainable cooling by (i) helping address the upfront cost and financing barriers, along with (ii) supporting measures such as policy and regulatory frameworks, reaching out to stakeholders through effective communication strategies, and providing technical assistance and capacity building for project teams (e.g., IFC resources in project management – essential for effective implementation). Subject to receiving World Bank Board approval, the funding will be provided by the Facility and co-financed by IRD/BID/AFD.

The indicative total co-financing amounts to $72.8 million.

### Component 1 - Policy, regulatory and enabling environment support (US$11.7 million in GCF grant)

This component aims at providing policy, regulatory and enabling environment support for the cold chain, support program design and roll-out, raise awareness and stimulate behavior changes, as well as build the capacity of key stakeholders. These are critical to help address some of the key barriers and market failures to sustainable cooling. The objective is to provide a conducive environment that will enable countries to support countries address fundamental market and non-market barriers to sustainable cooling, including lack of policy and regulatory frameworks, weak institutional capacities, inadequate infrastructure for cold chain equipment and logistics, as well as limited access to financing for the acquisition of refrigeration and appliance equipment, as well as nature-based solutions and energy efficient cold chains. The component will also support the development of a national or regional cold chain strategy that will be support for developing an enabling environment that can support the effective implementation of sustainable cooling strategies. This includes providing technical assistance and capacity building to the development of regional, national or local industry and services in the relevant sectors, while ensuring alignment with the Kigali Amendment.

[1] Global Cold Chain Alliance website: https://www.gca.org/about/what-is-cold-chain

[2] IRD/BID/AFD will provide co-financing for the implementation of the policy, regulatory mainstream activities and ultimately help identify potential investment opportunities, including for the Facility.

## Component 2 - Financing for cooling investments (US$141.7 million of GCF funds, which of $125 million in loan and US$16.7 million in grant).

In order to promote and accelerate the uptake of investments in cooling technologies that are affordable, efficient, clean, and accessible to all segments of society, including the poor and unmet cooling demand, the Cooling Facility aims to support increased access and deployment of sustainable cooling solutions. The Cooling Facility will provide concessional financing for projects that meet the level of ambition is appropriate and relevant to each market (i.e., affordable) to expand access to— and deepen penetration in—existing markets, while also targeting and avoiding creating a problematic new niche, which would hinder the growth of scaling-up efforts.

The Facility will provide concessional funds to foster the adoption or scale-up of deployment of sustainable cooling technologies, approaches, and business models. The Cooling Facility will support projects that are implemented by public and private intermediaries. The financial instruments will be calibrated to what is needed to make the cooling project viable and affordable for all segments of society, including the poor and unmet cooling demand.

The adoption of efficient cooling solutions or serve remote off-grid areas, which include higher upfront capital costs compared with conventional technologies, will be supported by concessional financing. The Cooling Facility aims at underrepresentation in many developing country regions and sectors. The deployment of these funds will be targeted toward investment projects where the use of concessional funding is necessary to make the project viable for sovereign or sub-sovereign entities credit lines to commercial banks, loans or subsidies to households, municipalities and small, medium and large enterprises.

High concessional loans and grant from the GCF will largely be targeted toward investment projects where upfront capital costs are high. Concessional financing by the World Bank (IDA/IBRD resources)1 is insufficient to ensure affordability and/or to make the efficient, clean cooling project viable.

Different financing and implementation mechanisms and models are suitable for different cooling sectors and different types of actors involved in the Cooling Facility. The Financing and Implementation Mechanisms and Models conditions. Examples of these financing and implementation mechanisms and models are provided below (Section B-4).

### General information on the Cooling Facility

Integrated and holistic approach: The scale of the cooling challenge and the window of opportunity to get now on a path to affordable, efficient, clean cooling demand an approach that integrates the development and strengthening of an enabling environment. Doing this involves technical assistance, capacity building, and coordination and implementation of projects in compliance with the EEI and PIEI contractual obligations included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into by the Accredited Entities (AE).

## Considerations and adopt a holistic approach to sustainable cooling consisting of one or more of these elements:

- **Minimize**: Reduce and avoid cooling loads, for example, with passive building design, increase of shading on buildings, green roofs, cold storage solutions, and the use of low global warming potential (GWP) refrigerants-based cooling systems.
- **Shift to Cooling Solutions with lowest GWP emissions**, for example solar cooling, district-friendly (low GWP) refrigerants-based cooling systems, or centralized cooling solutions where the right enabling conditions are in place.
- **Integrate**: Integrate energy efficiency technologies to achieve cooling with less energy (and less GHG emissions), for example, super-efficient appliances and equipment and demand management strategies.
- **Optimize**: control cooling loads, for example, smart sensing and behavior change approaches.
- **Protect the most vulnerable in urban and rural areas from extreme heat and consequences of unreliable or insufficient medical and agriculture cold chains**.

### National and regional contexts:
The Cooling Facility recognizes that the technologies, opportunities and modalities to enhance access to cooling are context-specific (the level of application (e.g. space cooling vs agriculture cold chains), as well as specific national – and sub-national (e.g. urban or rural) regions. The Cooling Facility will therefore consider, as relevant, country-specific context and technology benchmarks (including those derived from regional initiatives).

### Link with climate change and cooling policy goals:
Projects supported by the Cooling Facility should be aligned with, and contribute to the respective country’s NDC revisions under the Paris Climate Change Agreement, including the 2018-2030 National Adaptation Plan (NAP) targets, as appropriate. National Adaptation Plans and Disaster Risk Reduction Plans and support the goals and recommendations of the Global Cooling for All Partnership.

### Link with gender equality objectives:
The Cooling Facility will seek to contribute to enhancing the knowledge base on gender issues associated with access to cooling and will seek to contribute to reducing gaps in access to cooling, particularly for women. The Cooling Facility will also address gender gaps relevant to the four pillars of the World Bank Group (WBG) Gender Strategy[1], and/or aiming to address these gaps as part of the project.

### Link with countries’ COVID-19 response and build ‘better effects’:
The Cooling Facility may also support efficient cooling-related interventions in the context of the Baseline overall COVID-19 response and related operational plans. This could include, but is not limited to, strengthening cold chains for COVID-19 and routine immunization vaccines as a component of projects seeking to strengthen the country’s overall health sector.

#### Strengthening the health sector cold chain by financing the gap at service delivery points at national, regional, district and sub-district levels;
- ensuring access to user-friendly built infrastructure, including vaccine stores, walk-in cold rooms, walk-in freezers, etc.;
- efficient and climate friendly cooling equipment (including refrigerators and freezers) with lower GWP refrigerants;
- preventive maintenance and as-needed repairs of walk-in cold/freezer rooms at national and sub-national levels.

[1] This approach is consistent with the approach promoted by the Cool Coalition (https://www.coolcoalition.org/).

## IV. Eligibility criteria for Facility-supported projects

The Cooling Facility will allocate a maximum amount equivalent to an aggregate of 30% of the total GCF proceeds to any given Host Country, regardless of the financial instrument(s) deployed. Supported projects will be assessed by the Bank as meeting the following eligibility criteria. A market assessment may be conducted in order to assist with the design of the project and demonstrate how it meets the criteria to access funds from the Cooling Facility.

### • Cooling investments

Projects selected under the Cooling Facility will support investments in the following cooling target areas: (i) space cooling and cooling solutions, and (ii) refrigeration, cold chain and logistics, as defined in section B3.1 above.

In the case of (i) space cooling and cooling solutions projects, projects should demonstrate a pathway towards energy efficiency improvements and/or the use of renewable energy for public resources over time and eventually leverage commercial and private sector funding.

In the case of Facility-supported projects financing investments in off-grid cold storage capacity additional criteria will apply. In this case, the Entities will need to demonstrate that the main source of energy to power these additional cold storage facilities are renewable energy (e.g., solar).

### • World Bank operation linkage

A Cooling Facility-supported project may: (i) co-finance a new IDA or IBRD operation; or (ii) be an additional financing to an existing IDA or IBRD operation.

The ratio of GCF funds to IDA/IBRD funds for each supported project will be at least 1.2.

### • Performance thresholds

To receive support from the Facility, the proposed project must meet the following performance thresholds:

- At least 20% energy efficiency improvement; and
- At least 50% reduction in HFCs.

To that effect, for each project, an estimate of GHG impacts should be developed based on subsector-specific methodologies and calculation tools and consistent with guidelines outlined in Annex 24<sup>†</sup>.

### • Alignment with the transition to low-emission pathways

Facility-supported projects will be aligned with the phase-out of HFCs under the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, under which HFCs are being phased down and replaced with climate friendly refrigerants that do less damage to the climate. As such, investments supported by the facility will promote the use of climate-friendly cooling equipment/appliances commercially available for the cooling applications<sup>†</sup>.

<sup>†</sup> Together IBRD and IDA form the World Bank. IDA focuses on the world’s poorest countries, while IBRD assists middle-income countries.

<sup>†</sup> Annex 24 provides information of estimated GHG emissions for each Facility project.

## B

Through the Facility's programmatic approach to sustainable cooling, it would not be possible to reach the diverse sets of countries covered by the Facility, especially smaller and more fragile countries. Moreover, the programmatic approach is essential in taking advantage of opportunities for scale and supporting broader policy objectives within the context of different sectoral priorities that may otherwise be overlooked.

The Cooling Facility currently proposes to support nine projects (one project per country) subject to the results of their appraisal. This Funding Proposal includes indicative information for each of the currently proposed projects, including a brief description of the project, its objective and expected impact, and the approved projects included in the Program. Subject to the outcome of such due diligence, the World Bank will approve funding for such projects that meet the eligibility criteria as presented in Section B.3.

### I. Facility-level arrangements

As part of its role as GCF AE and co-financer of the projects to be supported under the Facility, the World Bank will assess and confirm the viability of each such Project to be included in the Facility's pipeline for Bank and GCF funding. To do so, for each project proposed to be included in the Facility, the World Bank team will comprise energy-and climate finance experts to manage and be responsible for the Facility's overall objectives and implementation. The team will also ensure that projects meet the criteria presented in Section B.3. The team will aggregate findings from each project team’s implementation progress reports and facilitate the dissemination of lessons learned across Facility projects and beyond the Facility.

Following the GCF Board approval, the World Bank and the GCF will, based on the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), enter into one Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) for the Facility for the provision of the funds required to implement the project. The FAA will specify the terms and conditions under which the management and administration of GCF resources and will carry out such management and administration in accordance with the provisions set forth in the AMA, including the World Bank’s policies and procedures.

The World Bank will apply its own fiduciary principles and standards relating to any integrity checks, countering of financing of terrorism, fraud, financial sanctions, embargoes and anti-money laundering.

The process of reviewing and approving GCF funding for the Facility-supported projects is embedded in the WB's project cycle (Figure 3) and based on its standard policies and procedures.

## Figure 3: Project review and investment approval process

### a) World Bank project cycle
- **Project concept note**
    - **Concept Note Approval**
        - **Assessment of compliance with existing policy framework**
            - **Recommendation and recommendation for further assessment**

### b) Cooling Facility investment process
- **Project proposal**
    - **Project Proposal Assessment**
        - **Assessment of compliance with existing policy framework**
            - **Recommendation and recommendation for further assessment**

During the project identification stage (stage 1), candidates are screened for formal inclusion in the Facility pipeline. This preliminary review focuses on the proposed use of the GCF funds, the scope of activities, the paradigm shift potential and the financing instruments envisaged. The WB project team then carry out a technical, economic, financial, environmental and social, fiduciary, procurement appraisal of the project, in line with the World Bank's standard policies and procedures. This is done through a Project Appraisal Document or equivalent for a technical review meeting (stage 2). This due diligence is separate from the responsibility of the GCF Secretariat to assess the projects against the eligibility criteria for funding under the Facility.

The scope of the technical review will cover the assessment of the GCF financing against the eligibility criteria for the Facility-funded projects. Upon determination by the Bank that the projects meet the eligibility criteria, the project is then submitted to the Board for approval (stage 3). The Board may also require further information and analysis before approving a project.

The next stages (negotiations, WB institutional appraisal, project implementation through completed and executed Subsidiary Agreements) are carried out in accordance with the World Bank's standard policies. The Bank will sign Subsidiary Agreements with the Executing Entities to make available GCF investment financing in line with the provisions of the Facility.

### References
- Track record and track record of the World Bank: The proposed facility builds on the WB’s decades of experience in supporting client countries across the globe implement clean energy projects. Over the past dozen years (FY10-21), the Bank has built a strong track record in financing clean energy, providing support to over 350 projects with an estimated total investment of US$33.9 billion (approximately US$3 billion per year), helping countries achieve their energy and climate (NDC) targets. The Bank’s portfolio includes 124 renewable energy projects, representing approximately US$8.6 billion per year, and the share of solar PV - the most likely source of RE to be associated with sustainable development goals - has increased from less than 5% in FY10 to over 30% in FY21 (approximately US$2.7 billion per year). The Bank’s EES portfolio alone has saved more than 1,300 TWh to date, associated with 575 Mts of avoided CO2 emissions. Demand-side EE accounted for about a third of the total FY-21 World Bank EE portfolio (approximately US$4.8 billion), up from less than 10% in FY10. The Bank has also strengthened its focus on energy efficiency, particularly through the Bank’s Energy and Extractive Global Practice, but with an increasing share with other WB Global Practices, including Urban, Water, Transport and Agriculture. Demand side energy efficiency interventions have also

## B

has been combined with other energy sector focus, such as energy access, and have been combined with renewable energy investments. This EEIF experience has included interventions related to cooling, such a building energy efficiency projects and energy efficient appliance programs.

The Cooling Fund builds on the successful experience of the EESIF in overcoming these market failures and barriers to scaling-up energy efficiency which include weak policy/regulatory frameworks, higher first cost, lack of access to affordable financing, new technology risk, lack of credit worthiness, transaction/aggregation costs (for financial institutions), and a lack of demand. The Cooling Fund will focus on the following areas: deployment of effective clean energy financing and delivery mechanisms across sectors and across the developing world; inclusion of cooling in national climate change strategies and policies; and development of innovative tools that encompass both public and private financial institutions. The design of these mechanisms has evolved, building upon lessons learned from previous initiatives under different circumstances, and they have achieved considerable success as documented in multiple evaluation reports.

The Cooling Facility builds on this deep operational foundation, combined with the Bank’s traditional energy sector experience, to provide a new financing mechanism for the Implementing Agencies of the Multilateral Fund of the Montreal Protocol, supporting the transition towards climate friendly refrigerants. The Facility will also benefit from the Bank's extensive track record in providing environmental finance and technical assistance. This includes experience in designing and implementing the upstream design of lending operations, setting up the regulatory, policy and institutional foundations to help mobilize private sector investment, and providing a range of financial products and services through the technical assistance program established under ESMAF which is assisting the Bank in developing a new business model for financing energy efficiency projects. The Cooling Facility will also draw on the Bank’s considerable experience with the inclusion of sustainable cooling activities in the urban, agriculture, transportation, and health sectors.
