# Implementation models description

This section describes the implementation models and arrangements that would be followed by the projects supported by the Cooling Facility.

## (i) Public procurement and contracting for cooling infrastructure service delivery and technical assistance*

GCF funds would be used to support investments through a traditional public procurement and contracting model. Under this model, the EE or the Project Implementing Entity (PIE) as designated by the EE would need to follow the World Bank’s procurement guidelines and procedures, including the use of standard work procedures (see below). This may be in the form, for example, of a dedicated unit within a government ministry or department.

The EE (or PIE as applicable), will: (i) prepare the technical specifications and terms of reference of the cooling infrastructure or equipment it wishes to procure; (ii) subject to no objection of the World Bank, launch the procurement process in accordance with the World Bank’s procurement guidelines and procedures; (iii) enter into contractual agreements with the winning bidders; (iv) monitor the delivery of the goods and services to ensure quality control using its own resources or through an owner’s engineer procured by the project; and (v) retain the right to terminate any contract for breach of contract. The EE (or PIE as applicable), will subsequently enter into an operations and maintenance (O&M) contract to ensure that the equipment is properly maintained, and that the cooling activities are carried out in accordance with the agreed terms. All personnel involved should receive adequate training to conduct O&M to ensure continuous operation of the equipment and infrastructure and that the impact is achieved as per estimates.

## Figure 5: Implementation arrangement for public procurement and contracting model

| Facility level | Project level |
| --- | --- |
| GCF | World Bank |
| FAA | Subsidiary agreement(s) |
| Contracts | Executing Entity* |

*If a project has a Project Implementing Entity (PIE), there may be additional arrangements including a project agreement between the World Bank and such PIE.

This implementation model is well-suited where the private sector does not have the capacity or the appetite to implement the cooling activities and, thus, public intervention is critical for service delivery. Such cases include:

- Interventions that strengthen the enabling environment by establishing the conducive policy, legal, regulatory, and economic framework; reduce barriers to investment; improve access to knowledge; and strengthen institutional capacities.

*This model is primarily relevant for both Component 1 and Component 3.*

B

Despite their economic viability, interventions that are not commercially viable to adequately remunerate private investors (for example, dissemination of off-grid appliances in poor and marginalized communities with limited economic opportunities and low capacity to pay), investment in energy efficiency and clean cooling is often constrained by the lack of a robust financial and value chain for vaccines and medicines such as in El Salvador, Togo and Principe and Somalia).

Pilot projects will be implemented under Component 2.1 to demonstrate the viability of policies to improve energy services and energy access and generate lessons for a replication at scale.

CGF concessional funds provided through loan or grant agreements with the Government would help redress this market failure by providing financial incentives to private investors in the sector, thereby establishing conducive investment, legal and policy frameworks. Further, it will promote social equity and protection by ensuring that the projects benefit poor and marginalized communities. To this end, CGF concessional funds will be used for a variety of activities including: (i) reducing upfront financing cost and thereby absorbing the incremental cost to deploy efficient cooling solutions. Final beneficiaries would pay back the loan or grant over time through savings on energy costs.

Through these interventions, CGF concessional funds would play a catalytic role in creating a market for and increasing access to clean, efficient and sustainable cooling.

For the purposes of the FAA, this model may be used for the activities under Component 1, financing of climate-friendly cooling investments under Component 2.1 and project management activities under Component 3.

(ii) Credit lines

A key barrier to investment in energy efficiency in general – and sustainable cooling in particular – is the limited availability of commercial financing for these projects. The Facility will support the provision of credit lines, i.e., for example in Mali, to help financial institutions establish business lines for sustainable cooling investments. This would be done by providing technical assistance to financial institutions on how to assess the financial risk of energy efficient and clean cooling, which may be carried out by energy service companies (ESCOs) or other third parties. The credit line will provide financing to ESCOs or other third parties that implement cooling and energy efficiency measures. The services may also include providing or arranging financing for ESCOs or other third parties to finance the purchase of equipment and/or technology, as well as implementing cooling and energy efficiency measures. The services may also include providing or arranging financing for ESCOs or other third parties to finance the purchase of equipment and/or technology, as well as implementing cooling and energy efficiency measures.

The payments from the user entity to the ESCO are contingent upon achieving certain pre-specified performance levels (e.g., a reduction in electricity consumption). The facility will provide technical assistance on how to design such payment mechanisms. This approach is known as an Energy Performance Contracting (EPC) or Energy Service Performance Contracting (ESPC). The payments from the user entity to the ESCO are contingent upon achieving certain pre-specified performance levels (e.g., a reduction in electricity consumption). The facility will provide technical assistance on how to design such payment mechanisms. This approach is known as an Energy Performance Contracting (EPC) or Energy Service Performance Contracting (ESPC).

The World Bank will channel CGF financial along with World Bank co-financing to the EE (the Host Country as the Borrower) through a grant and loan agreement. The EE shall extend the credit line to one or more participating financial institutions (PFIs), which would typically be commercial/developmental institutions, for co-financing.

At the minimum, investment rates of return should cover the cost of debt including private costs and the cost of equity. While the latter can be easily determined as the cost at which commercial investors are willing to lend to cooling projects, the cost of equity depends on the financial robustness of the prospective investor and its risk profile (e.g., a higher cost for an investor with a lower credit rating). The cost of debt is typically lower than that of equity. As a general rule, it would be higher than the rate that can be earned through a passively managed investment portfolio in government bonds or other low-risk investments. For example, investments with longer payback periods will command and higher maturity premium).

For further information on this topic, please refer to "Financing Sustainable Cooling: A Guide for Policy Makers", by Ashok Sarkar, Jonathan Sinton and Joen de Wit (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/18410)

### GCF concessional funds would be critical to help create markets for clean cooling. For illustrative purposes, the example of Malawi is provided below.

An agriculture-driven economy, Malawi is adversely impacted by climate change impacts such as floods and droughts. The country has a high level of energy poverty (90% of the population uses traditional biomass), which has hitherto promoted the use of energy inefficient technologies and systems as an enabling strategy. As the agriculture sector is one of the largest contributors of GHG emissions in the country, facilitating the development of sustainable cooling solutions for this sector would have a significant impact on climate change mitigation. However, commercial banks in Malawi seldom provide loans for agricultural activities. There is overwhelming evidence that access to finance is critical for scaling up clean cooling technologies and the adoption of cooling solutions is limited. Limited awareness and access to financing ultimately affect the deployment of modern cooling solutions. The GCF concessional funds will be used to support the development and provision of credit lines to participating financial institutions to stimulate the market adoption of cooling solutions.

GCF and WB funds will be used to extend credit lines to commercial banks, micro-finance institutions, and local government entities (such as District Development Strategies (DDS) or Provincial Development Strategies (PDS)), for on-lending to micro-, medium and small-scale enterprises (MSMEs). The GCF funds would support the development of a project proposal that is jointly approved by the World Bank and the Reserve Bank of Malawi (the RBM) for the administration of the funds. In such a case, the World Bank would also sign a project agreement with the RBM setting forth the obligations.

The project, approved in August 2020, aims to increase access to financial services, entrepreneurship and capitalization of MSMEs in Malawi. The provision of concessional financing from the Facility is critical to introduce sustainable cooling among the target development areas.

### Energy Efficiency Financing Facility

Under this model, the GCF processes are channeled through the Executing Entity (the Host Country as the Borrower) according to the terms of the Subsidiary Agreement signed with the World Bank. The EE either directly, or through an entity that designates (the Project Implementing Entity or PIE), on-lends the funds to sub-borrowers (e.g., energy service companies or cooling-as-a-service companies). Where there is a PIE, the EE enters into a Subsidiary Agreement with the PIE. The Subsidiary Agreement would include provisions that require the PIE to execute the funded activities in accordance with the requirements of the Project Agreement. In this manner, the EE has contractual control and oversight responsibility over the PIE. In addition, World Bank policies may apply to the PIE, including but not limited to, safeguarding obligations of the Host Country that are passed down to the PIE with respect to the implementation of the funded activities.

Figure 7. Where there is no PIE involved, the Executing Entity directly provides financing to the sub-borrowers and there is no project agreement or on-lending agreement.

## (iii-a) Public super ESCO model. In this variation of the EE financing facility model, the Host Country designates a public energy service company (the public super ESCO) as the PIF. The public super ESCO would be responsible for implementing energy efficiency projects in public buildings and cooling systems. These projects could include: (a) direct investments in the public sector (public buildings and suitable cooling equipment); and (b) strengthened private sector participation through a public-private partnership model, including but not limited to: (i) procurement of equipment for ESCOs; standardization of ESCPs, M&A templates and capacity building; project development; and (iii) financial support for ESCOs. The public super ESCO would be responsible for the implementation of energy efficiency projects in public buildings and facilities. The functions of public sector ESCO acting as a PIF are similar to those described above under the PIE model. In addition, the public super ESCO could perform energy audits to project design to performance contracting, procurement of cooling and energy efficiency measures, and post-contracting monitoring and evaluation. The public super ESCO would be responsible for operations and maintenance. Thus, this PIF would not only moderate stakeholders' risks but also would help build trust, including between private ESCOs and public sector end users. This variation of the energy efficiency financing facility arrangement is envisaged in Bangladesh.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 40 OF 83

## Under the EERF model, the PRE uses revolving mechanisms to recover its investment and operating costs based on contracts such as Energy Service Agreements (ESAs) or also use more traditional revolving financing mechanisms such as debt financing and budget capture. Under an ESA, the facility finances and undertakes the implementation of energy efficiency investments in public buildings and other infrastructure based on the energy savings generated by the investments to the EERF over an agreed period so that the EERF can recover its investment costs and operating expenses (e.g., maintenance) through a combination of the reduced energy bill and the payment to the EE or PIE; as applicable) is equal or lower to their pre-investment baseline energy bill. The ESA covers an agreed period, typically up to 15 years depending on criteria such as the type of investment, the building’s age, and the expected savings in terms of reduction of energy. As ESAs represent long-term term contractual commitments and are typically not classified as debt, they can be used by the EERF to finance investments that would otherwise be considered too risky for banks or other financial institutions, such as those related to space cooling and pose little risk, thereby addressing key barriers to public investments in efficient space cooling and energy efficiency.

### Risks

The PRE bears the risk associated with the performance of these investments and the associated recovery mechanisms. That is, in any case, the Host Country is obligated to make repayments, as per the terms of the Subsidiary Agreements, in line with the provisions of the FBA between the GCF and the World Bank. The EERF will use a combination of revolving financing mechanisms and other recovery mechanisms to incentivize efficient, clean cooling where there are market failures, where commercial financing for energy efficiency investments is not available or affordable, and where the public sector has limited capacity – and sustainable cooling – as too risky. It helps stimulate the market for sustainable cooling – mostly for space cooling (e.g., improve the thermal efficiency of buildings and the efficiency of cooling equipment) in order to reduce the demand for electricity and other energy sources. The EERF will support a country by building capacity, establishing confidence in the energy savings, introducing new financing approaches, and providing technical assistance through the services of local or international renewable energy experts in this area. The World Bank has experience in various countries with the establishment of EERFs that have been in operation for many years.

### Structure

The EERF funds through the EERF model. The North Macedonia Ministry of Finance will establish the EERF within the Development Bank of North Macedonia (DBNM), a development and export bank wholly owned by the Republic of North Macedonia. GCF funds along with WB WFs will be provided to the DBNM for the establishment, operation, and management of the EERF. The proceeds of the GCF proceeds available to the DBNM to support the initial capitalization of the EERF. In so doing, the Host Country will (i) provide a guarantee on behalf of the EERF to the World Bank and the GCF, particularly ensuring that the GCF consciously of the loan financed out of the GCF proceeds is subject to the same accountability framework for the financing of the GCF funded activities; and (ii) cause the DBNM to carry out the future audit functions in accordance with the applicable agreement between the World Bank and the Republic of North Macedonia.

### Use

The DBNM will use the GCF proceeds to finance and implement efficient cooling and other energy efficiency investments. The DBNM will be responsible for identifying and select subprojects presented by the participating municipalities in response to calls for proposals; (ii) eligible financeable subprojects through pre-qualification process; (iii) prepare project documents, including financial analysis, and manage all required consultants to conduct energy audits and prepare technical designs, and contractors to


---
**Page 43**
---

carry out energy retrofits and installation or upgrade of equipment of central government buildings (i.e. primary healthcare facilities identified in consultation with the Ministry of Health); and (iv) carry out and monitoring and evaluation activities. Investments financed by the GCF through the EERP, would need to meet minimum standards for sustainability and resilience as defined under the GCF criteria. These include: public sector owned buildings (e.g., schools, universities, government administration buildings, health facilities).

B. Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words; approximately 2 pages)

Sustainable cooling will remain a small, boutique niche unless targeted efforts are made to mainstream it and catalyze a paradigm shift. As documented by the IEA, SEFURAD, and others, current trends are neither sustainable or adequate to support development goals. If current trends continue, the rapid growth in production of synthetic refrigerants will lead to an increase in global emissions of HFCs (hydrofluorocarbons), which will make the Paris climate goals unattainable. Current trends are also far from sufficient to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and other international commitments. The opportunities for triggering a sustainability shift in traditional cooling approaches are often found within different sectors (e.g. agriculture, transport, buildings). However, these opportunities are not always recognized or prioritized as high-priority—especially as they respond and navigate the health and economic challenges exacerbated and amplified by climate change.

The GCF loan and grant are required for the development of the anticipated implementation models in the selected countries under the Facility, which involves adapting the models to the specific country context; testing and piloting the models with key stakeholders (e.g., government agencies, private sector, civil society); awareness raising among beneficiaries; development of investment pipelines; and other activities. The GCF loan and grant will provide critical financial support for these activities. This early support to enhance institutional and technical capacity require concessional financing before the models can be scaled-up relying on non-concessional financing and private sector financing. Without the availability of long-term, concessional funding, it is unlikely that the models will be able to scale up in a timely manner for the governments and result in either no, insufficient, or delayed adaptation in the critical area of cooling.

The GCF loan and grant are required because of the high demand to meet cooling needs and consequently, higher cooling-related GHG emissions. GCF funding is critical to catalyze the transition towards more sustainable and resilient ways that buildings and facilities can adapt to rising temperatures in a climate-friendly manner.


| 17 These investments would be done on a grant basis, i.e., there would not be any sub-loan agreement signed with the beneficiaries. |
| --- |


World Bank Group, Strategic Use of Climate Finance to Maximize Climate Action: A Guiding Framework, 2018

**Page 44**
---

The following factors justify the use of concessionality:

- Misused national and global incentives to reduce GHG emissions. This explains the lack of incentives for governments to invest adequately in climate action; more specifically, the fact that national policies are often not sufficient to achieve deep decarbonization due to the underinvestment.
- Decarbonization is considered a GPG, concessionality should support mitigation projects and help align national and global plans with climate action.
- Development banks have historically been able to provide loans at prices required to achieve deep decarbonization. Even though many climate policies bring significant domestic co-benefits, they often do not cover the full cost of achieving deep decarbonization. This is because the associated transaction costs linked to any economic transition (e.g., retraining of workers in declining industries) are often too high for national governments to bear.
- In cases where concessionality is used to support mitigation projects, it can help leverage private sector finance and help reduce the funding gap that hamper mitigation and adaptation efforts.
- Temporary interventions financed on grants or concessional terms can ease policy transitions and create a market for climate-friendly cooling technologies. Without concessionary support, new solutions will not break through early enough and certainly not at the scale needed to avoid a higher than 2°C global temperature rise.

Several factors contribute to cooling technologies adding to the rationale for concessionality described in the Guiding Framework:

- First, is the reality that there are market failures leading consumers to favor less climate-friendly solutions. For example, as highlighted in ESMAP’s Primer for Space Cooling (2020), expenditures to achieve space cooling already account for an estimated 5–10 percent of the median household income in many parts of the world.
- Second, the lack of capital access and large transaction costs that many developing countries face when it comes to scaling up climate-friendly cooling solutions. This is further highlighted in the feasibility study.
- Third, there are significant barriers to public support, risking missing cost-effective opportunities and can lead to substantial avoidable increases in GHG emissions: action is urgently needed to mobilize concessional finance for cooling with funding from the Multilateral Ozzone Fund supporting refrigeration replacement projects. Proving performance and expanding the size of markets for efficient equipment, regulatory and financial barriers need to be overcome.
- Another reason is that there are significant energy efficiency gaps in large urban areas, a category according to that most recent SEforAll estimate includes 680 million people. Another 2.2 billion people live in rural areas where access to electricity is limited or unreliable, and where the least efficient air conditioners (SEforAll 2019). Finally, concessionary financing is necessary to enable a programmatic approach that can scale up cooling solutions at an affordable cost while learning lessons learned, thereby accelerating the transition to sustainable cooling across regions and countries.
- There is also the need to accompany physical cooling-related investments with non-revenue generating finance to build and strengthen the policy, regulatory, institutional and general enabling framework for scaling up climate-friendly cooling technologies that can help reduce the impact of the climate and other co-benefits.
- Providing access to affordable and clean cooling solutions whose beneficiaries are the poorer marginalized communities.

The risks generated by efforts to test and put in place new models and create a new market demand for climate friendly cooling solutions. When a climate initiative involves new approaches, tools, or solutions, it entails a risk for the implementing country, and benefits for all others—a key


---
**Page 45**
---

characteristics of global public goods. The lack of (historical) data, uncertainties regarding future climate conditions, and the need for new strategies and technologies imply a need for capacity and financial resources.

- International transfer of knowledge about factors accounting for success/failure in climate action related to cooling could reduce the cost of a low carbon cooling pathway globally.
However, this is not happening. The lack of international cooperation on climate change creates an incentive to delay action and stick to proven solutions rather than experiment. Knowledge transfer does not happen because it is not in the interest of countries to share their knowledge.

- There is a real risk of negative global spillovers from low access to sustainable cooling which will lead to increased demand for fossil fuels, leading to higher emissions. Climate change impacts often spill over internationally and do not remain within country borders. Examples include health effects due to heat waves, food security issues, and water scarcity. These climate change-related security impacts caused by lower agricultural yields in one region leading to higher food prices worldwide.
Other, more uncertain but potentially very large sources of spillovers are:
- Migration: Climate change will lead to increased migration rates on the population; significant internal displacement and migrations could result with large negative impacts on regional stability and development.
- Conflict: Some evidence suggests that climate change may increase local and subnational conflict. This conflict could extend throughout the region.
- Humanitarian crisis cost.

In summary, the proposed level of concessionality is justified by the significant role of public goods; the presence of a global public good; the non-market nature of the good; the needed transition to sustainability over the life of the project; and the large leverage of investment expected from GCF funds.


B. Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The World Bank will help EEs develop and put in place a plan for how they can sustain the results and benefits beyond project closure, where possible. As provided in the Guiding Framework for the Use of Climate Finance, in its use of concessional funds, the WB is prepared to stay the course to support a change in trajectory up to 10 years after project completion. The World Bank will provide technical assistance and capacity building to ensure that EEs can take over. Projects will signal that concessionality is timebound, defined by a clear exit strategy, and intended to be used as a bridge to market rates of return.

Financial. The financing package (including the GCF portion of the funding) will be designed to ensure that it does not distort the market or displace other sources of funds. Depending on the nature of the project, the design may include measures to stimulate the participation of the private sector and provide economic opportunities for end-user beneficiaries.

Knowledge exchanges will be organized to foster cross-pollination and the dissemination of best practices of EEs in the region. The World Bank will also support knowledge sharing activities that involve experience sharing among different communities, institutions or countries sharing their experience with similar challenges. These activities are expected to lead to increased learning, replication and replication at a larger scale.

Technical and implementation arrangements. The World Bank will help EEs develop and put in place a plan for how they can sustain the results and benefits beyond the life of the respective projects. In order to ensure the sustainability of the equipment, it is important that the EEs have a clear understanding of the technical aspects involved with the operation of the equipment and infrastructure and/or may subsequently enter into an O&M contract. A key area for support will be in terms of capacity building, including training on the technical aspects, needs and context relevant to each project. Further, and importantly, the Facility will provide support to strengthen EE's enabling environments, including, for example, the development and adoption technical standards and regulations.

GCF funds will be used to support activities that aim to strengthen policy, regulatory and enabling environments that support scaling up and sustaining cooling-informed investments and building the capacity of key stakeholders. This includes inputs and learnings relevant for inclusion in cooling action plans as well as in the National Adaptation Plans (NAPs). The proposed facility is expected to contribute to the NAPs process by being highly relevant to inform national adoption and disaster risk strategies and plans. Activities linked to capacity building will be focused on supporting countries in developing their own policies, regulations, standards, and benchmarks for cooling technologies (e.g., different appliances and equipment for space cooling and/or cold chains). These activities are expected to contribute to the development of a global framework for cooling in order to help increase the demand for efficient cooling technologies and investments and ultimately stimulate sustainable cooling solutions. The proposed facility is expected to be documented as contributing to building capacity even beyond the context of countries supported by the Facility.

The Cooling Facility will be technology agnostic and support continued technological innovations, as long as the proposed technologies lead to more energy-efficient and climate-friendly cooling services and are in line with the GCF’s investment criteria. The facility is expected to contribute to the development of sustainable cooling solutions should have been demonstrated through laboratory testing and/or field-based data.

## D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section will focus on the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Proposed Project:

The proposed Facility is designed as an innovative, multi-sector and multi-country programmatic financing mechanism with the goal of transforming the cooling sector in the target countries. With a cross-cutting approach to address both adaptation and mitigation needs, this facility is essential for a warming world and especially critical to help build resilience in countries facing risks of extreme heat hazards, as well as help chart a sustainable path to low carbon development (impact potential).
- Adaptation


---
**Page 49**
---

In the Facilities at high risk of facing extreme heat hazards - Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi, and Somalia - the impact of Facility-supported sustainable cooling investment is expected to reach over 8 million direct (and over 16 million indirect) beneficiaries**. It is estimated that the expected direct benefits will be realized by approximately 250 thousand people in Bangladesh (and almost 3 million beneficiaries), 1.3 million in El Salvador (and almost 4 million indirect beneficiaries), 25 thousand in Malawi (and over 70,000 indirect beneficiaries) and 68 thousand in Somalia (and more than 90,000 indirect beneficiaries). The Facility's projects will increase resilience to climate impacts of buildings in Bangladesh, of agriculture cold chain operations, and of health facilities in El Salvador and Somalia. These combined cooling assets alone (more than 16,000 cooling assets) will also contribute to the Facility’s mitigation impact (along with the cooling investments in the Facility's 5 other countries).

### Mitigation

Overall it is approximately estimated that the Cooling Facility will lead to approximately 1 ton of avoided or reduced GHG emissions (compared to BAU) per year across the sustainable cooling investments made in the 9 countries that are part of the Cooling Facility. This amounts to over 17.0 MCO2e over the 15-year period of the Cooling Facility, which is equivalent to avoiding approximately 38,000 tons CO2 (which represents about 2% of today's global cooling-related emissions). This estimated GHG mitigation potential is based on a combination of different types of projects that are being implemented in each country with the different cooling target areas utilizing best-practice and publicly available tools and methodologies and considering the barriers faced by each country. The Cooling Facility will support the implementation of a variety of different types of projects that engage different business, financing and delivery models based on the barriers faced by each country.

The Cooling Facility will:

- promote passive measures to provide comfort while avoiding the need for mechanical cooling (for example, advanced thermal protection in building envelope, natural ventilation and cool roofs) – especially in buildings with high energy demand
- increase energy efficiency by deploying energy-saving cooling technologies in buildings (for example, supply-side and demand-side solutions)
- promote sustainable refrigeration and cold chain infrastructure – refrigeration and cold chain; or,
- promote the deployment of renewable energy to power cooling such as solar refrigerators and air conditioning units.

Given the diversity in the types of projects/sectors covered under the two cooling target areas, different calculation methodologies are used for each country. The Cooling Facility has developed a set of tools that are based on empirical data to derive assumptions. The main tools used to calculate GHG emissions are consistent with the ones recommended by the Technical Working Group (TWG) Guidelines for Harmonized Approaches to GHG Accounting. These tools include:

- IFC EDEG: "Excellence in Design for Greater Efficiency" software application to estimate (EEff Buildings)
- HOMER Powering Health (vaccines cold chains and health facilities)**

Annex 23 of the funding proposal outlines in more detail the GHG calculations for each of the projects covered by the Facility and Annex 24 describes the methodology and assumptions considered to perform the calculations.

The Facility’s experience and approach in assessing and achieving significant GHG impact through its 2 space cooling targets in different countries and sectors will contribute to building the information and knowledge base for how to scale up and stimulate the expansion of sustainable cooling more systematically in climate change plans and strategies.

Through the Facility's support, space cooling projects are being promoted both higher efficiency and lower GWP refrigerants. By shifting the BAU trajectory of cooling GHG impacts towards sustainable high-efficiency/low-GWP cooling, it is expected that the largest share (more than three quarter, i.e., 17.6MtoeCO2e) will be achieved by 2050 through space cooling projects. The remaining 4.3MtoeCO2e of GHG impacts will be achieved from ESEF space cooling - in line with the large contribution of this cooling target area to global electricity demand growth (17% increase between 2016 and 2050, compared to a total increase of 84% globally by 2050 – representing 18% of the increase in the total CO2 emissions associated with global electricity consumption). This is illustrated on the right-hand side of Figure 3.1 (the “Cooling GHG impact curve”). The GHG impact is further enhanced when adding the lower and lower GWP refrigerant use, considering that refrigerants are powerful GHGs in their own right.

The GHG emissions reductions associated with the promotion of more climate-friendly refrigeration and cold chain through the Facility’s projects are estimated to reach about 2.7 MtCO2e over the lifespan of the program accompanied by important co-benefits (see below).

### Official Use

Enabling the expansion of affordable cooling in off-grid contexts will help reach some of the poorest communities and to contribute to enhancing their resilience to the impact of climate change. Indigenous and non-indigenous populations, particularly women and girls with mostly power [MPP] these critical cooling services.

#### Kigali Amendment and Refrigerants

The Facility will also support the goals and requirements of the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol by supporting the transition from HFCs to more environmentally efficient cooling equipment (air conditioners and refrigeration) in parallel with HFC phase down. The more climate-friendly refrigerant for specific projects would be determined based on the specific market and application of the project, as well as the availability of the technology. The Environmental and Economic Assessment Panel (TEAP) at the Montreal Protocol Report Parties on Cost and Availability of Low-GWP Technologies and Equipment that Maintain/Enhance Energy Efficiency (https://ciee.org/en/capacity-enhancement/) have been examined ex-ante in line with global estimates including those from the IEA, E4Tech, and IRENA. These refrigerant transition supported by the Facility have been estimated ex-ante in line with global estimates including those from the IEA, E4Tech, and IRENA. These refrigerant transitions will contribute to the overall GHG emissions reductions achieved under the Facility’s projects are estimated to reach about 18 MtCO2e over the lifespan of the Facility.

#### Other impacts

The Cooling Facility is expected to generate a number of co-benefits, notably in terms of health. The Facility’s projects in Malawi (in collaboration with the Government of Malawi and the World Bank), El Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe and Somalia) will help them respond to the current COVID-19 pandemic, increasing the capacity to store vaccines and lower the incidence of wasted vaccines and help reach larger shares of the population with access to vaccines. The Facility’s projects in Kenya (in collaboration with the Kenyan government and Gavi) will also contribute to the country’s vaccination efforts. Health co-benefits – albeit of a different nature are also expected from the Facility’s projects in India, which will support the expansion of cold chain infrastructure for perishable goods. These investments will help lower the incidence of heat-related illnesses**, as well as mental stress**.

Other expected co-benefits are linked to improved local air quality through the displacement/avoidance of diesel-powered generators and other fossil fuel-based power generation in favor of solar or cold chains.

The Cooling Facility supports in sustainable cooling in the agriculture sector in Malawi as well as Kenya, with aims also to contribute to lower current high rates of food loss and indirectly to help boost efforts to address malnutrition.

As countries face economic downturns associated from the COVID-19 pandemic investments in sustainable cooling can be a win-win for both climate change mitigation and adaptation. In addition to unemployment and efforts to build back better, in fact, space cooling investments in new buildings and refrigerators have been identified in several countries to help boost local employment opportunities**.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

1) **Volume 4: Decision XXXI Task Force Report on Cost and Availability of Low-GWP Technologies and Equipment that Maintain/Enhance Energy Efficiency** (https://ciee.org/en/capacity-enhancement/) provides a comprehensive review of the cost and availability of low-GWP technologies and equipment for cooling applications. The report highlights the potential to reduce GHG emissions from cooling by up to 70% compared to baseline scenarios, while also maintaining or enhancing energy efficiency.

2) **Heat stress and air quality in Africa: A review of the evidence** (https://ciee.org/en/capacity-enhancement/) provides a comprehensive review of the evidence on heat stress and air quality in Africa. The report highlights the need for sustainable cooling solutions to address these issues, particularly in urban areas where temperatures can reach up to 37°C.

3) **Energy efficiency and climate change mitigation: A review of the evidence** (https://ciee.org/en/capacity-enhancement/) provides a comprehensive review of the evidence on energy efficiency and climate change mitigation. The report highlights the need for sustainable cooling solutions to address these issues, particularly in urban areas where temperatures can reach up to 37°C.

4) **For example, see the IEA December 2020 report on Energy Efficiency 2020 (https://www.iea.org/reports/energy-efficiency-2020#section-energy-efficiency-and-the-economic-recovery) and IRENA (https://www.ren21.org/publications/2020/April/Global-Renewables-Outlook-2020


---
**Page 52**
---

At a time when needs for cooling are intrinsically linked with efforts to meet BDCs and to build resilience to impacts from a dangerously warming planet, and when the global cooling demand is increasing exponentially, but on a trajectory that is costly, inefficient and high in GHGs, there is an opportunity now to set the course of the cooling sector on a new path. The potential is huge: maximizing energy efficiency in the cooling sector (refrigeration and space cooling with their continuously shifting GHG footprint) contained in refrigerators, the world can potentially avoid costs close to 1% by 2100.

The Cooling Facility proposes to catalyze a paradigm shift by working across key cooling target areas and with key stakeholders. The Facility will build on the experiences of other climate funds and programs that have had similar objectives and will draw upon their experiences and learnings. In so doing, the Facility seeks to provide pioneering examples that can be replicated in other countries and regions. It will also seek to identify and support innovative approaches that are fit-for-purpose in many countries with similar circumstances, and thus further stimulate scaling-up of the shift to sustainable cooling. The Facility will do this by engaging in a series of country dialogues and regional dialogue sessions in conjunction with investment support for country-defined national priority sectors, the Facility seeks to build evidence and generate a clear track record, working with local leaders and champions to demonstrate that it is possible to make the shift to sustainable cooling at scale. The Facility will also engage in national-level policy dialogues that will allow governments to see how the policy activities are delivering at sector level, put in place implementation structures and financial mechanisms that can be scaled up and/or see their scope broadened as they become more ambitious. In this way, the Facility seeks to ensure that any country with ambitious objectives at country level is being transferred. In order to shift from one-off investments to financing a sustainable cooling sector, the Facility will support countries in developing and implementing models that aim (a) at revolving-financing that allow financing to continue after the end of the program; and (b) at providing private sector investment opportunities in project activities in the longer term by setting up and/or supporting market signals for shifts towards public procurement (in the case of the health sector in particular) and incentives.

For example, credit lines help local banks develop a business line for co-investments and build their capacity to support these investments beyond the program. Developing ESFs that provide financing as well as technical assistance will allow countries to continue investing in cooling projects after the end of the program. The financing and capacity barriers that prevent co-investments from being implemented EERFs collect enough revenue over time to finance a sustainable sector, and the private sector is incentivized to invest and the EERF can become a sustainable entity. In addition, the EERF sustains its staff over the long term and builds its capacity – in contrast to project management units that are usually dissolved after the program ends. The ESFs will also provide financing for projects beyond the end of the program so as to continue and scale-up and create experience that can be shared with and replicated in other countries after the program has ended.

Moreover, the Facility will help countries mainstream sustainable cooling into development and climate plans, procurement strategies and policy/regulatory frameworks and also help train and build the capacity of relevant institutions. The Facility will support countries in developing and implementing national-level cooling measures and technologies. These efforts are aimed at building an enabling environment for further scale-up of sustainable cooling beyond the Facility’s investments.

The Facility's support to El Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe, is targeting efficient and climate-friendly solutions that can be scaled up across the country. The focus on cooling in efforts to strengthen health systems to leave countries better prepared for current and any future public health emergencies with greater sustainability.


| Official Use |

**Page 53**
---

In the building and cooling appliances sectors (e.g., in Bangladesh, North Macedonia, Panama, Sri Lanka), the Facility also seeks to facilitate and mainstream better access to cooling by supporting enabling environments and sustainable implementation mechanisms adapted to local institutional and policy frameworks. The projects will be designed with a focus on replicability and sustainability. The pioneering examples are designed to be taken to scale, and with positive results, the expectation is that there would be a ripple effect.

In the agriculture and off-grid sectors (Kenya, Malawi), the Facility will support new financing mechanisms and help build capacity and awareness and greater affordability highlighting contribution to productivity. Projects in these sectors will be designed to address climate change impacts on food security across the countries and beyond. As many African countries' economies have high reliance on the agriculture sectors and also vulnerable to climate change, the ground is fertile for a push for sustainable cooling if we can demonstrate that it is possible to provide cooling at scale.

At the same time the Facility's projects are supportive and aligned with each country’s climate change priorities and can support further ambitions.


D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The goal of SDG7 is to ensure access for all affordable and clean energy (SDG 7) and Climate Action (SDG 13); if cooling efficiency remains unchanged, additional power generation capacity of 2,000 GW will be required by 2050. The global electricity demand in 2050 could reach up to 48 terawatts or $1 trillion. Increased access to cold chains is critical to ensuring food safety and reducing food loss and waste. Climate trend changes are necessary to reduce associated CH4 emission (estimated at 4.4 Gt CO2e by 2030). Cooling demand will increase in the coming decades, especially for air conditioning (and space cooling) while simultaneously phasing down HFCs contained in refrigerants, the world can potentially avoid a significant increase of global warming potential (GWP) and associated climate change impacts. In developing countries, especially those located in the tropics and sub-tropics, will also contribute to SDG 13 by increasing greater resilience and improved health and livelihood of vulnerable people, communities, and regions.

Protecting workers and communities from extreme heat is critical in order to achieve Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8) and Sustainable Cities and Communities (SDG 11). The number of days with temperatures above 35°C will increase by up to 20% globally. It is estimated that by 2050, work hours lost due to heat may be as high as 12 percent in the worst affected regions of South Asia and West Africa – representing a loss of $48 billion per year in GDP. The number of days with temperatures above 35°C will increase at twice the rate of global change, largely due to the Urban Heat Island (UHI) effect. Annual mean air temperature is projected to rise by up to 10°C in some regions and the frequency of extreme heat events could be as high as 12%. The need to reduce excess urban heat is one of the key resilience and sustainability challenges.

Cooling and thermal comfort are also key to Zero Hunger (SDG 2), Good health and well-being (SDG 3), and Gender Equality (SDG 5).

As noted in Section B, in developing countries, many with undernourished populations, around 40 percent of food is lost or wasted. In the tropics, up to one-third of crops are lost due to heat stress. For every 1°C rise in temperature, harvest and market.

• Most studies predicted global natural disaster in an average year, posing the greatest risk for vulnerable populations. High temperatures increase the risk of heatstroke, breathing issues, heart attacks, asthma and other respiratory problems. Unbearable medical conditions put at risk millions of lives of women and children without supplies

Gender is also a key consideration to understand the gender gap in access to cooling services; gender risks are evident (GEWALL 2019). Women and girls often disproportionately impacted by a lack of access to sustainable cooling and enhanced access could lead to lower vulnerability to mortality

# Bangladesh

Bangladesh is among the World’s most vulnerable countries to the effects of climate change.

The country is also facing both serious energy security challenges and severe heat stress, equally across its high-income residents, low-income neighborhoods and informal settlements. Solutions must address these multiple challenges including scaling solutions that are financially affordable, overcoming consumer resistance to the higher first cost of more efficient equipment, and enabling greater consideration of the environmental impacts of energy use in buildings. The rapid increase in demand for cooling in buildings in particular, is rising rapidly due to increased urbanization and growing income and increased reliance on air-conditioning in both residential and high-end restaurants.

Pressures from growing demands for sustainable cooling, could result in significant increases in coal-fired generation by 2030 if nothing changes. Greater use of renewable energy and faster progress to scale-up energy efficiency will be needed to help develop environments in a more climate-friendly way. Buildings and appliances EEE are an important part of the EES efforts under the Environment Conservation Act and

The Bangladesh Energy Efficient & Conservation Master Plan. Integrating EE&F and passive building design features and energy efficient cooling at scale now key for building a more sustainable infrastructure at lower costs and lower pressures on the electricity grid.

El Salvador is the smallest country in Central America with a poverty rate (based on a US$5.5 per person per day poverty line) of 29% and extreme poverty (US$3.2 per person per day) of 8.5%. As of 2017, its given its tropical geographic location, biodiversity, and topography, El Salvador is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts and dry spells by 2050 (and rising sea levels (a rise up to 8m by 2060) is predicted).

The challenges to El Salvador’s health system and the Government's ability to respond are magnified. These impacts will be felt most acutely in vulnerable populations, who will experience amplified impacts of climate change. These impacts are expected to lead to more frequent natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and tropical storms. The impact on the population is likely to be exacerbated by the fact that many people do not have access to these services at times when their services are most required. A large proportion of El Salvador’s vulnerable population are the elderly (11.7%) and the adult population with underlying risk factors such as obesity, diabetes, and hypertension.

To ensure an effective COVID-19 response and generate long-lasting resilience and be prepared to handle future health crisis, the Government of El Salvador seeks to strengthen the overall national immunization and related health delivery systems. This includes a variety of issues, including safe and appropriate cold chain management for vaccines and other medicines, as well as ensuring that the country is in a position of El Salvador to take advantage of the window of opportunity of revamping its cold chain to ensure it is safe, reliable, and sustainable.

Kenya: Demand for cooling is projected to grow significantly in the Nairobi and Mombasa markets – 1.8 million cumulative unit sales between 2020 and 2030 and for off-grid households alone. In addition, there are an increasing number of businesses that are looking at energy efficiency as a way to reduce their costs while also owning a refrigerator. Energy efficiency will help lower the projected energy consumption to meet Kenya’s cooling demand, and in doing so reduce utility bills for households and businesses, decrease the need for new power generation capacity, and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. This is consistent with Kenya’s country climate mitigation goals. In the off-grid context, furthering access to cooling solutions will contribute to both economic development and improved health outcomes. The government of Kenya has made significant efforts (with renewable energy). In the context of nearly 40% of food perishing before it reaches a consumer in Kenya, there is an urgent need for cooling infrastructure that can be scaled up to meet the needs of the population by improving food security through reduced post-harvest losses, create employment opportunities and increase income generation. The government has identified several opportunities for innovative business models making cold chains available to small farmers as well as financing for the higher cost of more efficient AC systems for residents in resource-limited areas.

Mozambique: Most of Mozambique’s rural landlocked country is South Africa, with most of its people living below the poverty line. The country is subject to frequent macroeconomic and climatic shocks. Mozambique's private sector is mostly informally with a few large-scale companies capturing most economic opportunities. The country has limited access to electricity and water supply, low energy generation capacity, and are primarily concentrated in the service sector, such as wholesale, retail and trade. A key consideration for EE&F in Mozambique is the need to ensure that any EE&F interventions are cost-effective and innovative business models within the financial sector. MSMEs can potentially drive employment, yet they face a number of challenges including access to finance, lack of skills and knowledge related to cooling, in particular more sustainable cooling solutions with lower operational costs, is hindering

## Economic Opportunities for MSMEs Involved in a Number of Important Sectors, such as Dairy and Horticulture (Fruits and Vegetables)

Many firms, especially smaller ones, face capacity and capability gaps, going from attitude/mindset to skills with respect to financial literacy, management practices, business language, market assessment, and business planning. In addition, 50 percent of MSMEs in the dairy sector are not registered, 48 percent do not keep records, and 45 percent lack innovation and diversification (FinStat survey 2019). Among the horticulture sector, 67 percent of MSMEs have no business plan, 35 percent do not know the process or the importance of formalizing their activity. Promoting investment opportunities in sustainable cooling solutions for dairy and horticulture sectors can help to address these gaps and create new opportunities and strengthening the resilience to climate change of vulnerable and marginalized population groups.

In addition to the dairy and horticulture sectors, other sectors are also being considered as mentioned earlier. Malawi is looking at cold chains as a suitable implementation model that can kick start the transformation in the dairy sector. Cold chains in its agriculture sector targeted at MSMEs by facilitating access to financing among MSMEs.

## North Macedonia

### Background
North Macedonia is a landlocked country at the heart of the Balkans, characterized by its mountainous terrain that is intersected by valleys and lowlands. Many public buildings (including schools, hospitals, etc.) are located in these areas, which are prone to landslides and earthquakes due to structural instability and seismic risks) and dislocated. In fact, buildings, which consume about 39 percent of total energy consumption in North Macedonia, are the most vulnerable infrastructure sector.

### Challenges
The public sector has the greatest potential, with about 35-40 percent savings, mostly in the health and education sectors. The private sector is also a major consumer of energy (about 28 percent). Higher temperatures in Europe are expected to substantially increase the demand and need for cooling, especially in buildings where air conditioning systems are used. Cooling systems can significantly reduce their future cooling loads, which will contribute to the country's adaptation to the changing climate.

### Recommendations
Several challenges need to be addressed for the public sector to invest in sustainable cooling. Central government buildings typically have very limited budgetary provisions for capital improvements. If any, and usually only if there is a major disaster or emergency, such as an earthquake, will they receive funding then; have stringent debt limitations which many have already reached. Few have creditable ratings thus, with limited access to financing.

### Energy Efficiency
The energy efficiency of buildings in North Macedonia has been improving over the years but progress has been difficult to mobilize for the public sector, effectively forming a barrier to EEF renovations in public buildings. The private sector is also not yet ready to invest in EEF measures due to lack of a financing mechanism designed specifically for the public – including municipal – context will be needed to overcome these barriers and encourage investment in energy efficiency improvements.

### Air Conditioning Systems
The cooling systems, especially air conditioning systems, are expected to become higher priorities. Given the specific conditions and barriers described above, it is recommended that the government should consider a financing mechanism for energy consumption and associated GHG emissions – through the establishment of an energy efficiency fund, a sustainable financing mechanism that is well suited for its circumstances and targeted sector.

### Panama
In the past ten years, Panama's cooking appliances market has been growing rapidly for both ACs and refrigerators. The number of air conditioners in households increased from 10 percent to more than 65 percent (in 2018). According to the National Energy Plan of Panama (Plan Energético Nacional 2030), the energy consumption of residential and commercial sectors is expected to increase by 47.9% and 66%, respectively, in 2030 compared with 2015 levels.

The electricity sector is facing serious challenges in keeping up with rising electricity demand from the growing number of air conditioners and refrigerators. The National Energy Commission (CNE) has estimated that energy consumption by residential and commercial sectors will increase by an average 6 percent in the business-as-usual scenario to around 4 percent, which would reduce the need of capacity additions by 38 percent.


---
**Page 57**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 50 OF 83

Several barriers must be addressed to respond to this rapidly growing problem. Though Panama has a sophisticated banking sector, the absence of (i) dedicated EEC lines priced to incentivize investments in EE and (ii) local long-term capital market for mortgages (access of local banks to capital markets is limited to short-term loans), high interest rates on loans for equipment purchases, coupled with low levels of investments, represent a major barrier for EE in Panama. The higher initial cost of EE equipment and lack of financing options are barriers that need to be addressed. In addition, the country’s electricity sector has lock-in large quantities of inefficient, polluting but cheaper (on a first-cost basis) cooling equipment. Law 69-08, which was passed by Congress in 2017, aims to transform Panama into a low-carbon economy. The law establishes the Low Emission and Renewable Energy Fund (LREEF) aimed at catalyzing private investment, reducing the high-risk perception associated with green buildings and energy efficient cooling appliances, and improve market liquidity for renewable energy projects. The LREEF will be financed by contributions from public entities, international development partners, and leverage private sector funds.

## Background

The Republic of São Tomé and Príncipe is a small low-middle-income country comprised of two main islands. It has a total population of approximately 200,000 people, 42.6% of whom are 14 years of age or younger. In addition to having a small population and a remote location, there is a high proportion of young people in the country, which makes it particularly vulnerable to development outcomes.

With 1,054 COVID-19 confirmed cases as of January 8th, 2020, São Tomé and Príncipe showed one of the highest rates of infection (644/100,000 people) in the region and still had limited capacity to handle the COVID-19 outbreak. The Government was unable to quickly establish laboratory capacity to diagnose COVID-19 cases, which resulted in a delay in testing for suspected cases. This led to a backlog of several months without having laboratory capacity to diagnose COVID-19, early efforts to contain transmission were hampered by this lack of diagnostic capacity. In addition, the country has limited case finding capabilities and performs contact tracing and slow transmission. This capacity was finally established in July, alongside with the establishment of a national referral hospital.

Notwithstanding the fact that the country is now better prepared to diagnose, isolate, treat and track COVID-19 contacts, a significant and more comprehensive strategy to strengthen the public health systems will be key for the long-term sustainability of the response. The Government has already started to implement this strategy by investing in laboratory capacity and establishing a national referral hospital. However, the investments and expenditures for COVID-19 should also look at longer-term, wider benefits, in line with national development priorities.

Somalia: Somalia has experienced prolonged conflict beginning in 1991 and is among the poorest countries in the world with a per capita GDP of US$839 in 2015. An estimated 60% of the Somali population live in poverty, and 47% are food insecure. The country’s fragile health system has been severely weakened by conflict, and its struggling health outcomes reflect the country’s insecurity, vulnerability and deep-rooted poverty, limiting opportunities for people to access basic social services, including education and health. Health service delivery is also hampered by a lack of infrastructure, limited human resources, and poor data quality. The recently released 2019 Somalia Health and Demographic Survey (SHDS). While geographically disaggregated data show that the majority of Somalis live in areas with adequate access to health services, geographically disaggregated data show tremendous service delivery gaps. Health service utilization is low, particularly in rural areas where only one in five people report having visited a health facility within the past year or one hundred people per year (SARA, 2016). Clan structures are believed to have major impacts on service utilization and access.

The global COVID-19 pandemic is a pressing problem in Somalia, exacerbated by an extremely weak primary health system, inadequate surveillance and laboratory capacity, and limited hospital services. As of January 8th, 2020, there were 35 confirmed cases of COVID-19 in the country, with 4 deaths. There are also reports of additional, unconfirmed cases and quickly rising deaths. The government and development partners have taken important steps to improve Somalia’s COVID-19 response, including establishing testing capacity at the National Public Health and Reference Laboratory in Mogadishu, constituting rapid response teams, and

## Sri Lanka

Commercial (including government buildings) and industrial buildings consume 90% of the electricity consumption in the country. Driven by economic growth, increasing income, rapid urbanization, and a growing middle class, energy demand has increased significantly over the past decade.

### Energy Efficiency

Despite commendable progress in the last decade, development of the energy sector faces major challenges that need to be addressed. The existing stock of buildings is highly inefficient with a much higher energy consumption typical of existing buildings, there is a large untapped potential for energy efficiency improvement and reduction of GHG emissions through improved building design, better insulation, and efficient cooling and ventilation are main energy uses. A large share of the commercial building stock is old and uses chillers that can be replaced with much more efficient and more climate-friendly equipment.

### Energy Efficiency Action Plan (EEAP)

The Energy Efficiency Action Plan (EEAP) aims to address the commercial buildings sector – where a large stock of existing buildings has old, inefficient chillers for space cooling – as one of the key targets for achieving its EEI improvement goals. SEA also has defined an ambitious Demand Side Management (DSM) target of 10% reduction in energy consumption by 2030 through energy efficient buildings (including the potential of nature-based solutions such as green roofs and green walls). The key challenges to achieving these goals are lack of awareness among stakeholders, limited technical capacity, and insufficient public awareness. Dedicated EE credit lines, capacity building and enabling environment support would help address these concerns.

### Sri Lanka's Selection of Viable Implementation Model

The Government has selected the ESCO model as a viable implementation model for energy efficiency cooling at scale and foster the development and strengthening of the national ESCO (Energy Sector Cooling) market in the country, and thereby establish the foundation for the transformation of space cooling.

The Facility will support the implementation of these strategies by providing financing mechanisms to scale up the adoption of clean and sustainable cold chains in agriculture, which will contribute to the achievement of the MDGS goal.

- **North Macedonia:** As a contracting party of the Energy Community Treaty, the Government has committed itself to the implementation of the National Energy Efficiency Strategy (based on a 2010 baseline), more than nine times required under the EECS's National Energy Efficiency Action Plan. The Facility will support the implementation of this strategy by providing financing mechanisms for the construction and operation of buildings.

- **Panama:** In 2012, the Government made a national commitment to EEF by approving the Law 69-12 "National Energy Efficiency Strategy." This law is aligned with the National Energy Plan 2015–2035, which contemplates the design and implementation of the Rational and Efficient Use of Energy. The country also ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2017, which aims to phase-down hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) by 2040. The Facility will support the implementation of the Hydrofluorocarbons Phasing-out Management Plan (HPMP) under the Montreal Protocol.

- **El Salvador:** The Government is integrating climate-friendly investments in its national programs and its NDC includes health as a sector affected by climate change and the promotion of a larger use of renewable energy. The Facility will support the implementation of these strategies through financing mechanisms for the construction and operation of buildings, including cold chain, which will help develop a more sustainable and resilient health system in the country.

- **Sao Tome and Principe:** The country's NDC includes promoting the greater use of renewable energy and building energy-efficient infrastructure. The Facility will support these strategies by providing financing mechanisms for the construction and operation of buildings, including cold chain, which will contribute to the Government's NDC through supporting space cooling and cold chains in the health sector.

- **Somalia:** Somalia’s NDC related to scaling up the use of solar energy is consistent with the Facility’s focus on more climate-friendly health facilities and cold chain, including through greater use of solar power.

- **Sri Lanka:** Demand Side Management is identified as one of the seven mitigation actions of the National Action Plan (NAP). The country has developed a national framework for DSM. The Energy Efficiency Building Code was first introduced in 2002 and revised in 2008. The code provides guidelines on energy efficiency measures, including building design and construction methods for determining compliance. These efforts are aligned with the Facility's support of space cooling and EE measures in buildings.

The financial structure of the Facility adequately balances the need to support low-carbon cooling investments, leverage additional resources from private and commercial investors, and enhance the enabling environment for cooling. ## Co-financing and leverage


---
**Page 60**
---

The US$137 million from GCF will be complemented by an expected US$ 722.8 million in World Bank co-financing.

### Cost-effectiveness

According to the International Energy Agency's Energy Technology Perspectives 2008 Report, a mitigation of reducing a ton of carbon dioxide-equivalent of $200 is the lower-end estimate of the incentive needed to achieve the objectives of the "BLUE Map Scenario." The Facility provides a cost-effective solution for achieving these goals. It will result in an estimated US$137 million in net benefits (i.e., carbon dioxide equivalent avoided or reduced [accounting for the total investment, i.e. GCF plus World Bank co-financing]).

### III. Economic viability

An economic analysis was prepared for the cooling facility to assess its economic net value. The analysis considered both costs and benefits: (i) investment outlays to support the project; (ii) revenues from the funds provided through the facility; (iii) avoided or reduced greenhouse gas emissions; and (iv) recycling and other environmental remediation activities. It was found that the program would not be financially viable if a large number of people living in underserved areas, or reduced food loss and waste due to inefficient cold chains, have not been included as beneficiaries of the cooling facility. The results of the analysis show that the program yields a financial and an economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 10% and 25%, respectively. This is consistent with the World Bank's definition of its contribution to GHG emission reduction potential of the Funded Activities. Further, the economic value is very sensitive to the estimated cost of recycling (otherwise conducting environmental remediation actions) associated with the cooling facility. The program also provides a significant opportunity for the adoption of appliances in a climate-friendly and economic manner.

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Besides the arrangements laid out in the AMA between the World Bank and the GCF, the implementation of the Cooling Facility involves (i) monitoring of program performance indicators (as per Section E3); (ii) periodic progress reports; and (iii) an end-of-program evaluation. The cost of implementing these arrangements is not included in the Program budget as it will be financed by the World Bank. The scope of the AE will be defined at the time of implementation, based on what is necessary. The AE will report to GCF the implementation status at both Facility and country level by aggregating specific country-level information and data.

#### Monitoring
The implementation of each project under the Cooling Facility will be managed, monitored and reported on at program level by the Executive Entities and the Project Management Units (which may include staff and consultants). The World Bank has dedicated staff in its sectoral teams, operation departments, Environment and Social departments and country offices that will conduct due diligence, monitor compliance and


---
**Page 72**
---

performance of implementation of the Environmental and Social Commitment Plans and the stakeholder engagement plans.

The World Bank will follow its standard monitoring and reporting policies and procedures, which will include internal reports on the implementation status and performance of each project. By the end of each project supported by the Cooling Facility, the Bank will conduct jointly with each respective Executing Entity a final evaluation report to be provided to the GCF. The final evaluation report will review the performance of the project, assess effectiveness and efficiency of project implementation, the achievements of the project development objective, and provide relevant lessons learned. Financial reporting is further described in Section D3.

### Reporting

Reporting of Executing Entities to World Bank: As specified in the subsidiary agreements between the Executing Entities and the World Bank, the Executing Entities are obliged to report on the implementation of projects supported by the Cooling Facility. The reports will include information on project progress and social performance of the project and the achievement of the indicators agreed upon between the WB and the Executing Entity. For each project supported by the Cooling Facility, the respective Executing Entity will be required to submit a mid-term evaluation report at the midpoint of implementation period of the facility.

### Reporting to GCF

Reporting of World Bank to GCF: The World Bank will provide to the GCF (i) annual activity performance reports (APRs) on the status of the Facility throughout the relevant reporting period, (ii) mid-term evaluation report at the midpoint of the implementation period of the Facility and (iii) final evaluation report at the end of the implementation period of the facility as per the IFN provisions.

### Evaluation

The evaluation methods for mid-term and final evaluations will include:

a. Key informant interviews with relevant beneficiary staff and clients, as well as other key stakeholders based on stakeholder mapping; and
b. Detailed review of project documentation, including Project Management Unit reports, based on an agreed list of evidence to be provided by the client.

Please refer to Section D3 for further details.

As described earlier, each country's cooling operation, a PMU will be established with a team expected to include at least a team lead; technical expert; procurement specialist; financial management specialist; environmental specialist and social/gender specialist, and a lawyer. Each project will have its own monitoring and evaluation plan (M&E) as per the GCF guidelines.

The Bank's evaluation of projects included in the Cooling Facility is done in the context of the Bank's standard Implementation Status and Issues (ISR) report™ which is the key tool used for internal reporting on the implementation performance and prospective outcomes of projects. It is through this tool that the Bank’s project management team monitors progress, assesses risks, and provides advice to the project team towards reaching its development objective, and evolution of risks. Safeguards, financial management, and procurement specialists advise the team leader on evaluation of project's performance from a fiduciary and safeguards perspective.

[Note: See The Bank Guidance on Investment Project Financing Implementation Support (2017)]


---
**Page 73**
---

The Bank’s internal task team will document progress toward meeting project objectives by tracking and updating the projects cooling or cooling-informed indicators as outlined above through the Bank's periodic Implementation Status and Results reporting (ISR) process®. The Bank will draw on these inputs to report against the project’s development objective, which is a key element of the Bank’s internal reporting system. It should be noted that the end targets of the Facility are best estimates as it is not known upfront exactly the results of each investment that will be financed, and the targets may be revised during AE's due diligence, project preparation and the mid-term review.

At the end of the project, the Bank will conduct jointly with each PMU an implementation completion assessment and prepare and Implementation Completion and Results (ICR) report which will review the performance against the project’s development objective, assess the achievement of the project’s results in line with the development objective, and provide relevant lessons learned. The individual project ICR reports will form the basis of the Facility terminal evaluation report.

The ISR is the Bank's key tool used for internal reporting on the implementation performance and prospective outcomes of projects. It is through this tool that the Bank’s project team leader conducts the evaluation of the project against its implementation status and performance, progress toward reaching its development objective, and evolution of risks.
