# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Identified Mitigation Measures (Box: 3 pages)

### For Facility: High has high probability. Medium has moderate probability. Low has negligible probability.

#### Selected Risk Factor 1: Project readiness risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

There is a risk that some of the early stage projects do not mature and thus, are not able to mobilize funding from the Facility. Developing the relatively new concepts for cooling for some projects may take significant time and resources. The Facility will be designed with a phased approach to project preparation where beneficiaries have limited experience and/or data and information on technology and related cost savings are missing.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

To mitigate this risk, the Facility is designed to leverage the ESMAP multi-sectoral Efficient Clean Cooling Technical Assistance Program. The TA is intended to help beneficiaries and Bank operational teams in the design of projects. It will provide technical assistance for project preparation including market analysis and strategies, knowledge sharing, stakeholder consultations, business models identification, etc., as well as capacity building on project development. This will be complemented by a series of pre-prepared projects, designed to meet the eligibility criteria of the Facility. Continued dialog with national stakeholders will support the preparation of cooling projects aligned with the goals and criteria of the Facility.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Political Risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Political | Medium | High |

**Description**

If project investments result in direct or indirect increases in tariffs, fees, consumer product prices, or other cost increases where constituents might see the cost burden (in order to ensure the financial sustainability of project investments), this may prove politically unattractive for client governments to adopt these projects.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

VIB mitigates this risk by ensuring that project beneficiaries can afford the affordability constraints of the end user beneficiaries. For example, VIB will ensure that low income groups are provided with financial support if affordability thresholds are surpassed and, whenever possible, combine tariff/product investment with other interventions (e.g., subsidies, rebates, etc.) to be applied in order to ensure a positive impact on costs and reduce the cost passed-on to consumers. These effects will be sustained to lower risk level (Low) as much as possible for all projects under the Facility.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: COVID-19 risk

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational | High | Medium |

**Description**

Given the broad scope of the economic impact of the pandemic, there is a growing concern that developing countries will be hit the most which will cause dislocations in various economic and social sectors. There is a risk that client countries will shift their priorities to focus on immediate emergency response and recovery interventions for health care systems and investment cooling could be deferred.

(Mission Measure 6)

The World Bank teams will closely monitor the current COVID-19 crisis and its potential impacts on the progress of the projects supported by the Fund. In addition, the WB operational teams take part of the solutions to help client countries in the immediate response as well as in their economic recovery efforts. The EES will ensure that the beneficiaries receive adequate training to continue with the operation of the equipment and infrastructure and/or may subsequently enter into an O&M framework.

Selected Risk Factor 4: Limited uptake of EE/Eff Clean Cooling technologies

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational | Medium | Medium |

Description

The penetration of energy efficient and clean cooling solutions may be very low particularly in sectors such as health care, education, and social services. This is due to the lack of information and awareness about the energy savings, reluctance to pay for a higher initial cost, etc. Therefore, the implementation of some of the projects could be exposed to the risk of a lack of market demand for implementing additional solutions.

(Mission Measure 6)

This risk can be mitigated through dedicated capacity building activities targeting relevant stakeholders, their awareness raising, and communication with the private sector. Studies that would identify and recommend policy options and business models adapted to the country circumstances to facilitate the rapid uptake of EE/Eff clean cooling technologies will also be developed in close collaboration with the client countries developing countries on EEF, and the teams will learn on the lessons learnt from these experiences as well.

Selected Risk Factor 5: Lack of technical capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Operational | Medium | Medium |

Description

The lack of technical capacity, in some countries, could negatively affect the implementation of the projects, continuous operation of the equipment and infrastructure, and thus the expected outcomes. A proper training and maintenance service is key to ensure the projects are not underperforming, hence the results are not compromised.

(Mission Measure 6)

The risk will be mitigated through the M&E framework that will be developed during the preparation phase and is an integral part of the project documentation and specific to each country. M&E arrangements will be designed in a way that ensures the continuous operation of the equipment and infrastructure, and the sustainability of the investments. The EES will ensure that the beneficiaries receive adequate training to continue with the operation of the equipment and infrastructure and/or may subsequently enter into an O&M framework.

Risk of IBRD/IDA co-financing not materializing or being lower than specified in this Funding Proposal and that the projects proceed without co-financing.

About 54 percent, for $396.84 million, of the total amount of the co-financing amount has already materialized, with WB and WB managed trust funds approved and the corresponding legal agreements signed with the recipient countries for six of the nine projects envisaged. Should the balance not be materialized by the end of the project implementation period (the minimum level set in the eligibility criteria), the Facility would achieve 71% of its mitigation target (as measured against the baseline scenario). If the co-financing amount is not materialized, it will be very unlikely that none of the remaining projects take place and (if this GCF counterpart financing is not provided) the facility we only achieve 33% of its mitigation impact but reach 90% of the desired avoided emissions. The reasons for the lack of co-financing are: (i) alignment issues with the Cooling Facility, with the country needs as expressed in national strategies, policies and plans; (ii) the complexity of the projects through which the GCF will finance, and (iii) the robust sector-level dialog led to the World Bank in the supported countries.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The main aspects considered from an E&S perspective are summarized below. However, each project under the Cooling Facility has its own assessment completed at concept/prototypical stage. The ESF will include a list of expected environmental and social impacts and suggest mitigation and monitoring measures for these impacts.

#### ESS1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts)

The facility's cooling-informed projects are expected to generate substantial direct social and environmental benefits by increasing energy efficiency in buildings and cold chain infrastructure and deploying climate-smart technologies. The main expected environmental and social risks and impacts associated with these benefits include, inter alia: (i) reduced greenhouse gas emissions due to energy savings and reduction or avoidance of fossil fuel use; (ii) improved air quality through the reduction of local air pollutants (the particulates, SOX, NOx); and (iii) reduced emissions of ozone-depleting substances and other harmful chemicals.

The projects also generate significant positive social impacts through job creation and market development for energy efficiency products and services; reduction in consumer electricity bills thus improving affordability and access to energy; increased access to clean water and sanitation facilities which improve well-being including a better overall working environment in buildings. By improving and expanding access to cooling infrastructure, the projects will also contribute to improved health outcomes through increased access to vaccines and medicines as well as to nutritional food; hence generating positive impacts on human health and nutrition.

Some of the projects may cover geographically remote, politically sensitive and socially marginalized areas and communities. The projects therefore benefit not only mainstream communities, but also those that are often excluded from development opportunities. For example, increased access to cooling infrastructure through energy efficient cooling products and increased employment opportunities are likely to also provide benefits for women and girls in these communities.

The implementation of the projects will have impacts on gender and disadvantaged or vulnerable individuals or groups. Differentiated measures will be adopted so that adverse impacts do not fall disproportionately on the disadvantaged or vulnerable, including women and girls. The project will ensure that all employment opportunities resulting from the project, which would enhance project development outcomes by promoting inclusive growth, are open to all genders and other disadvantaged or vulnerable groups.

The implementation of the projects will also have an impact on gender equality and non-discrimination (GENPs) in: (i) Gender; (ii) Non-Discrimination and Disability; (iii) Non-Discrimination: Sexual Orientation and Gender Identity.

If not properly planned, construction of new buildings and cold chain facilities may result in potential adverse environmental and social impacts associated with land acquisition or land conversion. During the construction or retrofitting of buildings, common risks are safety and health hazards on workers and the community due to inadequate site security, handling and disposal of old parts and equipment which may contain hazardous waste and materials.

However, these potential impacts can vary in terms of intensity/significance and are localized in nature. For each project, an assessment of environmental and social risks and impacts will be carried out and measures to address them will be identified in accordance with the mitigation hierarchy as described in the previous section. The Bank will ensure that all projects align with its own policies and self-imposed requirements and mitigated by sound operational practice and consistency with ESS3 principles during operations.

- **ESS2 (Labor and Working Conditions)** Project workers are expected to be mobilized under most projects. This will include direct workers and contracted workers. The Government will have to comply with the ESS2 requirements as specified in Labour Code 1998. Occupational Health and Safety (OHS) plans will need to be prepared as per the World Bank Group OHS guidelines. The labor inflow will be managed by the project team, taking into account the potential impacts on local employment among the project workers but also upon local communities. These risks need to be assessed as part of the overall risk assessment and mitigation plan for each project. Appropriate mitigation measures will be reflected in the bidding and contractual documents. A dedicated grievance redress mechanism will be established and maintained under each project. This mechanism is separate from the overall project Grievance Redress Mechanism.

- **ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention and Management)** Construction of new buildings or retrofitting existing buildings are expected to generate typical construction impacts such as noise, dust, and air pollution. These impacts can be mitigated through additional cost for demolition and handling, and disposal of asbestos waste, resulting in negative impacts on air quality and occupational safety and health. The potential impacts are low to moderate in intensity/significance and are reversible and localized in nature, and therefore can be managed.

- **ESS4 (Community Health and Safety)** ESS4 requirements will be considered in the implementation of projects. During the construction phase, there may be a risk that hazardous substances may arise from noise, dust, labor safety, and disposal of old parts and equipment which may contain hazardous waste. In the case where the operation is carried out by contractors, the contractor must ensure that all risks are managed. There may be a temporary transitional impact for these users. These impacts require appropriate mitigation assessment and management measures to contain them.

- **ESS5 (Land Acquisitions, Restrictions on Land Use and Involutionary Settlem** Land acquisitions will be required in some cases where land is needed for the project. This does not consist of ESS3 requirements - to avoid, minimize, reduce/mitigate and compensate at replacement cost for loss or damage caused by the project. The Bank has a policy on resettlement that sets out an outline of a resettlement policy framework (is presented in Annex 5 (ESS3 Annex). The Executing Entities will be responsible for preparing, in a manner and substance satisfactory to the bank, the project-specific resettlement policy frameworks or/and resettlement action plans in line with ESS3 requirements.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 77 OF 83

* **E58 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources):** Impacts, such as out of the silviculture and selection of land for future cold storage facilities, may include loss of habitats or other adverse encompassment impacts, depending on the scale of the projects. Care should be taken by these future projects to ensure that their impacts on natural habitats, terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems are minimized.

* **E57 (Indigenous Peoples/Sub-Saharan African Historically Underserved Traditional Local communities):** The presence of indigenous people in the areas identified for the project will be screened and confirmed by the World Bank consultation with the national authorities. Once it is confirmed, consistent with ESSST objectives and requirements will be integrated into the design of the project and E&IS documents, including the development of appropriate measures to ensure that indigenous peoples plan or other types of plans depending on the country and project context, and Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (in the circumstances specified in E58).

* **E56 (Cultural Heritage):** The project will screen the project area and its surroundings for potential cultural heritage, both tangible and intangible. If any such heritage is identified, it will be mindful of cultural heritage and the application of ES58 will be further assessed under each project. In case of no apparent cultural heritage in civil works sites, a chance find procedure will be a part of the E&IS Instrument.

* **E59 (Financial Intermediaries):** The decision on how to use the Facility will be tailored to the needs of the projects. Some projects are expected to channel financing to Financial Intermediaries (FIs). For such projects, ES58 will be applied to the FIs going to receive financial support under the Cooling Fund. In this respect, the FI is required to assess and manage the environmental and social risks associated with its activities, including the identification, management, and monitoring of the environmental and social risks and impacts of FIs subprojects on an ongoing basis.

* **E58 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources):** The project will screen the project area and its surroundings for potential biodiversity conservation and sustainable management. If any such biodiversity is identified, it will be mindful of biodiversity conservation and the application of ES58 will be further assessed under each project. In case of no apparent biodiversity in civil works sites, a chance find procedure will be a part of the E&IS Instrument.

* **E60 (Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure):** The Project affected parties include implementing agencies at national level, regional level, local authorities of participating provinces, private sector, and other interested communities. In addition, all interested parties including government representatives, international NGO and development partners working in the same region will be informed about the project. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) will be developed for each project to ensure transparency and inclusiveness throughout the project cycle. The SEP will include a description of how stakeholder engagement will be conducted throughout the project cycle. This will include discussions of project design and impacts as well as other environmental instruments, such as ES58. The SEP will also describe how the project interacts with other social and environmental instruments, will be subject to public consultation and disclosure per requirements in ES59, and can be treated as a live document to be regularly updated during the phase of project implementation.

### G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Facility aims to strengthen women’s roles as employees, entrepreneurs and consumers in cooling-related sectors in project countries, including enhanced female economic participation in cooling-related activities, increased access to sustainable cooling services, improved health outcomes (e.g., financial) and enhanced productivity and income generating opportunities for female-led businesses and women. The Gender Strategy is aligned with the World Bank’s Gender Strategy 2016-2025, the Group Gender Strategy (2018-2021): (i) advancing equality between men and women through climate change mitigation and adaptation actions, and (ii) minimizing gender-related risks in climate change actions.

While more data and analysis are needed to better understand the gender aspect of access to sustainable cooling services, it is clear that greater female participation in the cooling sector could be achieved by limited access to sustainable cooling and enhanced access could ensure gains in terms of entrepreneurship opportunities and enhanced livelihoods.

Taking into account the projects supported by the Facility, in terms of sectors (e.g., agriculture, building materials and energy) and also in terms of countries and in terms of urban versus rural contexts), the Facility’s gender approach will be adapted to each project’s specific design, local barriers and opportunities. The gender strategy will ensure that women are effectively included as service users, economic participation, access to finance, as well as opportunities to promote gender equality and inclusion will be effectively identified and embedded throughout each project cycle.

109 These will be incorporated into the ESMS, the environmental and social procedures and/or set out in the legal agreement.
110 These are: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts (PSI), Labor and Working Conditions (PSII), Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention (PSIII), Community Health, Safety and Security (PSIV), Indigenous Peoples (PSV), Cultural Heritage (PSVI), and the Gender Strategy (PSG), Management of Living Natural Resources (PS6), Indigenous People (PS7), and Cultural Heritage (PS8).


---
**Page 81**
---

These issues are tackled in a sustainable manner, raising awareness, improving gender policies, building capacities and generating knowledge on the program’s differentiated impact on men and women. Preliminary gender assessments for each of the identified countries under the Facility highlighted the following gender gaps and their potential mitigation actions:

- **Entrepreneurship**: Women entrepreneurs often lack information about access to finance options in the country. In addition, developing a business requires technical assistance support tailored to women’s needs. Partnering with business networks, such as female exporters or associations under chambers of commerce, can be an effective way to reach out to women entrepreneurs. From a consumer perspective they will also have different needs and wants in terms of technology products and services and the sectors they operate in (e.g. agriculture or transport).

- **Financial services**—not only accounts, but payments, savings, and credit—give women some basic tools for economic empowerment. In many countries, women’s access to financial services is limited as bank accounts are key to ensure payments for cooling technologies, and strategies need to be developed to ensure that women can open these accounts. The use of mobile money or the introduction of digital payments can further reduce access for women. Affordability is a key part of this focus.

- **Jobs and Skills Development**: Job creation and livelihood opportunities will be mapped out as part as part of the gender analysis. In addition, the role of informal sector employment in supporting agents and local suppliers will create opportunities in terms of jobs or looking at labor force participation rates for women.

- **Consumer Engagement**: Growing evidence points to significant differences in the needs and wants of women versus men as e.g. consumers and entrepreneurs, as well as caregivers. For example, different groups may have different preferences when it comes to cooling technologies, how they are adopting cooling technology and measures (e.g. agriculture sector), and how they respond to campaigns and communication strategies. Women also play a significant role in the distribution of caregiving responsibilities in communities and families. It is therefore essential to tailor any efforts towards women specifically and ensure that they have a need to consider communication strategies in these countries to look beyond written material or communication methods (e.g. radio, television) as they transmit information related to benefits of cooling-related services, consumption patterns, etc.

- **COVID-19 response and health measures**: Women often lack empowerment and autonomy to access services, sometimes related to health and decision making. Women must not be excluded from accessing services or being empowered in their communities. The Facility will support the collection of data on women’s access to services and how they are affected by COVID-19. Data collected through this process can also help to assess the program's results and impacts on gender equality and also help to scale-up/refine activities for future interventions.

The Facility will adopt a systematic approach and provide support to projects interventions with a focus on data collection, policy engagement, project level actions and capacity-building activities. The Facility will leverage support for the gender-related activities from the ESMAP Gender and Energy Program (GEP) and other relevant WBG programs. The Facility will also identify key gender focal points for relevant WB Global Practices. The Facility will encourage the recruitment of a balanced female-to-male employment ratio recruitment of women within the project management unit (PMU) teams.

Obtaining stakeholders buy-in will be key in ensuring that interventions to promote women’s employment and enhance their livelihood are effectively implemented with measurable outcomes. Institutional and behavioral change is a long-term process that will require sustained support of all relevant actors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 80 OF 83

## Stakeholders (public and private) throughout the program cycle. Countries adherence to international agreements and national normative frameworks on gender equality, will be a good starting point to strengthen government's support for gender interventions.

At the project and program level, the following approach will be followed:

- **Data collection** to document the baseline situation and identify gaps. Baseline data will be collected to set targets and measure progress during implementation. It will also support existing efforts where baseline sex-disaggregated data are weak. Research will build upon existing country gender equality frameworks and national gender equality strategies, as well as other relevant assessments considered.
- **Support project design and implementation**: Stakeholders' consultations will involve female and male stakeholders from the outset of the project. Gender equality considerations in all assessments will include an analysis of the differentiated impact on men and women, through the involvement of gender and energy experts (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, Resettlement Action Plan, etc.). The assessment should also consider the potential for or inadequate access to finance for the cooling-related sectors, and design mitigation measures that minimize gender gaps such as enhanced productive uses of energy, enhanced knowledge and skills of small-scale women entrepreneurs.
- **Identifying and implementing relevant actions**: Based on the initial diagnosis and inclusive consultations with stakeholders, targeted activities will be identified in addition key gender gaps. These may include (i) adoption of policies and strategies (including public awareness campaigns for women to gain hands-on experience in the sector such as support for school-to-work transition), (ii) financing for women's participation in the cooling-related sectors (e.g., through microfinance projects [such as activities generating revenues]), (iii) implementation of prevention measures to mitigate associated incidents or risks in the areas of implementation of the projects and/or facilitating access to energy services, and (iv) capacity building for women entrepreneurs (including that develop energy services).
- **M&E and Knowledge management**: In 2017, the WBSC started implementing the "gendering mechanisms" approach on projects that effectively promote equality between men and women and are aligned with the WB's gender strategy. The Cooling Facility will adopt a gender perspective and aim to close the knowledge gap in this area by collecting data on gender gaps at project level and used for real-time adjustments during implementation. To expand the knowledge base, projects will build on lessons learned from prior projects as well as regional and country-based knowledge production and exchange.
- **Engaging stakeholders with specific needs and risks of gender inequality**: Meaningful consultation, information-disclosure and responsive grievance redress throughout the project life (as set out in the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment). Focus groups will be used to ensure women's participation and voice during consultations. Consultations and public meetings with stakeholders will be conducted at least once a year, including specific sessions for women and other groups of engaging women. This will help gaining knowledge about community concerns during project preparation and help monitor impact and undertake real-time correction during implementation.
- **Special attention** will be paid to using local data, customizing concerns when relevant and including women from minority ethnic groups and other socially vulnerable groups.

Official Use


---
**Page 83**
---

G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Each project financed through the Cooling Fund will be required to conduct financial management and procurement according to the World Bank policies and procedures.

* **Procurement**

Each project supported by the Cooling Fund will follow the World Bank's Procurement Framework and New Framework (https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/operations-and-services/procurement/new-framework#framework). World Bank procurement staff support borrowers throughout the procurement process, including in the preparation of procurement documents. The goal is to use a competitive approach to get the best development results. Each project is designed, developed and monitored by the Executing Entity (or PMU) with support and supervision by a World Bank task team that includes a procurement specialist.

For each project, a PMU will be established by the relevant Executing Entity (host countries or governments) of the project. The PMU will lead the team responsible for the procurement function.

Each project, with the exception of the guarantee operation (see below), is also be subject to the 'World Bank's Anti-Corruption Guidelines', dated July 1, 2016.

* **Financial Management**

Each project supported by the Facility is required to maintain financial management arrangements (including planning and budgeting, accounting, internal control, funds flow, financial reporting, and auditing arrangements) that are consistent with World Bank policies and procedures and in accordance with the World Bank. A financial management specialist is expected to be part of the team forming the PMU established by each relevant Executing Entity (or PME as applicable) for its respective project.

Moreover, for each project, the World Bank task team includes an acronym Financial Management (FM) specialist responsible for advising on the design and implementation of the FM arrangements (setup, supervision and implementation) in accordance with the World Bank's operational policies and procedures (to work with partner countries to develop a sound financial management system that is consistent with the World Bank's policies and procedures and the performance of World Bank-financed operations). The team's FM specialist closely monitors the performance of the Executing Entity’s (or PME as applicable) FM arrangements and their continued capacity to provide resources in accordance with the World Bank's operational policies.

Financial reporting is also required for each project which is specified in each project's Disbursement and Financial Information Letter (DFIL). Unless otherwise agreed by the World Bank, the Executing Entity must submit a quarterly financial report. The DFIL will specify the frequency of submission and the format of the unaudited interim financial (IFR) reports periodically. Audits are carried out by auditors with independence and capacity acceptable to the World Bank, under terms of reference acceptable to the World Bank.

G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

The Access to Information Policy (2015) governs the public accessibility of information of operational development activities financed through the World Bank. The policy can be accessed through the following link: http://pubdocs.worldbank.org/en/383051436585910261/World-Bank-Policy-on-Access-to-information-V7.pdf

* **No confidential information:** The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
* **With confidential information:** The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence.


---
**Page 84**
---

Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

- full copy for internal use at the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality as per the Green Climate Fund disclosure policy; and
- redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# ANNEXES

## H.1 Mandatory annexes

- **Annex 1**: NDA no-objection letter(s) (template provided)
- **Annex 2**: Feasibility study – and a market study, if applicable
- **Annex 3**: Economic or financial analyses in spreadsheet format
- **Annex 4**: Detailed budget plan (template provided)
- **Annex 5**: Implementation timeline including key project/programme milestones (template provided)
- **Annex 6**: E&S document corresponding to the EAS category (A, B or C; or I1, I2 or I3):
    - Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or
    - Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) or
    - Environmental and Social Mitigation Action Plan (ESMAP) or
    - Environmental and Social Sustainability Framework (ESSF)
- **Annex 7**: Summary of consultations and stakeholder engagement plan
- **Annex 8**: Gender assessment and project/programme-level action plan (template provided)
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Procurement framework
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 12**: AE fee request (template provided)
- **Annex 13**: Co-financing commitment letter, if applicable (template provided)
- **Annex 14**: Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule (template provided)

## H.2 Optional annexes

- **Annex 15**: Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- **Annex 16**: Maps indicating the location of proposed interventions
- **Annex 17**: Multi-country project/programme information (template provided)
- **Annex 18**: Appraisal, due diligence or evaluation report for proposals based on up-scaling or replicating a pilot project
- **Annex 19**: Procedures for controlling procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects
- **Annex 20**: First level AMUCLIFT (KYC) assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- **Annex 22**: Projects information (confidential)
- **Annex 23**: Greenhouse gas calculations
- **Annex 24**: Annualized cost of carbon for greenhouse gas and beneficiaries calculations

*Please note that a funding proposal will be considered complete only upon receipt of all the applicable supporting documents.*

Архивски број: 09-12 4741

**Зелен климатски фонд**

Док. 06.03.2021

Предмет:

Писмо без приложение за Предлог проект за Зелениот климатски фонд, поднесен од Светска банка за енергетска ефикасност во јавниот сектор во Република Северна Македонија како што е наведено во предлог проект поднесен од Светската банка до Националната комисија за финансиски прашања, фонд на 12.05.2020 (броj: БВ-36/98П) и 08.01.2021 (броj: бр. 26-204УЛ) не изискуваате за следните:

ДОЛОЖНОСТНАНИЦИТЕ претставени во заседанието претпоставено на Национално калинешко седиште, орган на Република Северна Македонија, наредниот ден за контакт (Focal Point) за Реформи Северна Македонија, град Фетиче Петков. Заменик на претседателот на Владата на Република Северна Македонија задолжен за економски прашања, координирање со економските ресори и иновациите.

Во соразмерност со одлука за Зелениот климатски фонд (броj: БВ-86П), со чија содржина сме запознаени, го доставувааме овој Писмо без приложение за проектната како што е назначено во предлог проектот.

Доставувачкото на овој Писмо без приложно не изискуваате дека:

• Владата на Република Северна Македонија нема земењки по однос на програмата како што е наведено во предлог проектот;

• Програмата како што е наведена во предлог проектот, во соразмерност со намените и цели на Зелениот климатски фонд,

• Во согласност со насоките за залагање на природата и одговорните решенија на Зелениот климатски фонд, програмата како што е наведена во предлог проектот се согласуваат со соодветни закони и регулации.

Исто така, потврдуваме дека националниот працес за издавање на нисмо без приговор за програмата како што е намедено во предлог-проектот е сprovedена.

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by World Bank regarding Cooling Facility Programme

Dear Sir,

We refer to the multi country programme "Cooling Facility" also executing in Sri Lanka as included in the funding proposal submitted by the World Bank to us on the 20th of April 2020.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Environment, the National Designated Authority of Sri Lanka.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government has given its prior written direction to the programme as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Sri Lanka; and
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

## Proposal name: Cooling Facility

### Accredited entity: World Bank

### Country/ies): Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, Panama, São Tomé and Príncipe, Somalia, and Sri Lanka

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Cooling Facility is a multi-sector, multi-country, cross-cutting proposal that is designed to address key barriers to scale by providing technical and financial assistance to create enabling environment and develop innovative financing models. It will have a significant impact on adoption, scaling up, and replication of cooling solutions in the countries involved. This makes a difference, particularly when countries are prioritizing scarce resources available for an emergency response such as COVID-19, focusing on healthcare. | The Cooling Facility and the projects for investment are at an early development stage. Proper assurances, including implementation arrangements, need to be put in place to ensure that the intended impacts that the Facility will deliver as well as the associated risks are managed effectively. It is important that the Accredited Entity (AE) strictly complies with the agreed set of procedures in the terms sheet throughout the process. |

The nine (9) countries are representative of different regions, climates, and varying needs and challenges for cooling. The data gathered from these countries will inform the larger cooling community of the innovative financing approaches.

The programme has a high level of impact both on climate change mitigation while meeting Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), planning for disaster risk reduction (DRR) focused on supporting the goals of the Kigali Amendment.

5. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in annexure XVIII and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

# Programme background

## 2.1 Programme context

### 2.1.1 Programme description

Cooling is critical for health and well-being, livelihoods, and the environment. Cooling is an essential part of vaccines and food systems to keep vaccines safe and food fresh, and is central to maintaining productive work environments in hot climates. It is also necessary for maintaining "comfort" buildings for both work and leisure to ensure people have comfortable buildings where they can live, work, and learn. However, cooling is a luxury good that is expensive to provide, especially in low-income countries. Cooling has become more important as climate change and lifestyles, rapid urbanization and rising ambient temperatures are leading to an increase in the demand for cooling. The global market for cooling appliances and services currently in use is projected to increase to 9.5 billion by 2030, according to Dreyfus et al., 2020, and the number of people living without access to cooling will rise from 1.6 billion to all people in need of cooling were to be catered for, as opposed to only those who could afford it.

According to SELLERALL (2020), over 1 billion people in developing countries are already facing heat stress and the number is expected to increase by a further 350 million. People living in hotter-climate urban areas where electricity services do not exist, are intermittent or are too expensive for cooling appliances, are particularly vulnerable.

The cooling sector also faces challenges related both to mitigation and adaptation. Firstly, the increase in cooling demand leads to high levels of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from cooling equipment and the use of fossil fuels as energy sources. Secondly, most of the refrigerants used today for cooling are fluorinated gases (F-gases), hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) or hydrofluoroolefins (HFOs)—these F-gases have a very high global warming potential (GWP). The use of these F-gases in cooling equipment can cause substantial emissions. Thirdly, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change in 2019 reported that climate change will increase heat stress and extreme weather events. By 2050, 3.7 billion people could be both exposed and vulnerable to these events, which can cause illnesses such as heat stroke, dehydration, and cardiovascular disease. Food stress will also impact productivity. The International Labour Organization (2019) estimates the productivity loss due to heat by 2030 to amount to the equivalent of 80 million full-time jobs.

As mentioned above, cooling has become a critical part of daily lives, if it is comfort, supply the food and medicine, or provide a safe environment for vaccines. Cooling will be a "part" of the overall sector and never a stand-alone initiative. Therefore, clear focus on cooling has been missing in most policy frameworks. This project aims to address this gap by integrating cooling within the overall agricultural value chain.

The Cooling Facility will be one of the world's first cooling-focused facilities with the aim of providing solutions for cooling from multiple angles, including regulation and policy, technical assistance, and most importantly financing to address multiple barriers inhibiting the broader adoption of cooling. The facility will focus on both efficient and low-GWP refrigerant.

Key barriers to be addressed by the Cooling Facility are:

(a) Lack of regulatory/policy frameworks for sustainable cooling systems along with limited institutional capacity to design, implement, monitor, and enforce cooling standards;

(b) Lack of enforceable standards like Minimum Energy Performance Standards (MEPS) and Standards & labels (S&L), which target a specific sector—in this instance cooling.


---
**Page 106**
---

# Cooling Facility design

The wide range of cooling needs and priorities implies an equally diverse set of challenges to be addressed as outlined above. Therefore, the Cooling Facility is uniquely designed based on the principle of “no one-size-fits-all solution or approach”. Given the nascent and still early stage focus on cooling globally, it addresses both development and climate change purposes. It will support countries in addressing their cooling needs in a sustainable and socio-economic context, and efforts and priorities to respond to the crisis caused by the COVID-19 pandemic.

In this context, the Cooling Facility will support activities that address and help remove regulatory/policy-related, institutional, market and behavioural barriers to the development of sustainable cooling solutions:

* Providing technical assistance and building the capacity of state and non-state actors (including commercial banks, private investors and technical companies, and end-user beneficiaries);
* Supporting the design of sustainable implementation mechanisms and business models;
* Increasing end-user awareness of the benefits of low-carbon cooling solutions;
* Facilitating access to innovative financing; and
* Providing credit lines to financial intermediaries.

In turn, these activities will: (1) strengthen the capacity of key stakeholders; (2) stimulate the demand for climate-friendly cooling solutions; (3) foster the penetration of


---
**Page 107**
---

climate-friendly equipment and infrastructure: and (4) mobilize capital from public and private sources.

12. These interventions will strengthen institutional and regulatory systems, improve access to cooling, improve the adaptive capacity of end-user beneficiaries to respond to rising temperatures, and reduce energy consumption in buildings and other critical infrastructure. Ultimately, the climate change resilience of built infrastructure will be enhanced, while the overall cost-effectiveness of cooling solutions is improved.

13. In fact, the diversity of the selected countries targeted by the Facility is expected to provide pioneering examples for mitigation and adaptation activities related to cooling in different contexts. The interventions will not only be country-specific but also sector-specific, and thus the Cooling Facility aims to support different approaches and generate lessons that can then be scaled up or replicated elsewhere. In this way, the Facility will help countries at the same time kick start a transformation towards more sustainable cooling, particularly by offering financing for energy-efficient solutions and technologies beginning with high priority.

14. Where growing cooling demand is creating pressure on a country’s energy systems and upward pressures on GHG emissions, the priority is to support space cooling, in terms of both passive and active cooling, as well as efficient appliances and equipment. In countries where efficient cooling equipment and systems (this is the case for Bangladesh, North Macedonia, Panama, and Peru) are already available but where access to financing remains a barrier, the priority is to provide access to cooling into rural and off-grid areas; the priority is to support an efficient and climate-friendly cold chain (e.g. through the Facility’s proposed support to Malawi and Kenya).

15. In countries where the demand for cooling is increasing due to population growth and storage of pharmaceuticals is a priority, because pharmaceuticals expire and become ineffective more quickly in warmer temperatures. In these cases, the priority will be to work with facilities that can expand quality health services and ensure safe storage of vaccines while making them more resilient to warmer temperatures, as well keeping their carbon footprint low (e.g. it is already happening in India).

16. There is an urgent need to cut emissions and meet increasing demand for future cooling sustainability. The Cooling Challenge is a global initiative that has been endorsed by over 100 countries, and regional frameworks such as the European Union F-Gas Regulation demonstrate how refrigerant emissions can be reduced, yet further refrigerant phase-downs and phasing-out of hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) are needed. To support this transition to renewable energy build-out up to USD 3.5 billion by 2030 and accelerate the net-zero transition in cooling, the Cooling Challenge will provide strong support on how to contribute to meeting these targets.

### Integrated and holistic approach

17. The scale of the cooling challenge and the work required to get on a path to affordable and sustainable cooling demand are very large; an integrated development and strengthening of an enabling environment. This involves technical assistance, capacity-building, and concessional financing to mainstream sustainable cooling and establish sustainable energy systems. To support countries in achieving their climate change goals to reduce greenhouse gas emissions while meeting growing cooling demand, the Facility will integrate a range of interventions that are designed to provide a comprehensive and holistic approach to sustainable cooling consisting of one or more of the following elements:

- Minimize: reduce and avoid cooling loads, for example with passive building designs; increase energy efficiency in buildings and appliances; use renewable energy sources such as solar thermal systems and nature-based solutions such as green roofs;


---
**Page 108**
---

# Shift to cooling solutions with the lowest GHG emissions, for example solar cooling, climate-friendly (low GWP) refrigerant-based cooling systems, or centralized cooling solutions where the right enabling conditions are in place:

* Improve efficiency: use energy-efficient technologies to achieve cooling with less energy and lower costs.
* Reduce demand: reduce peak demand and demand management to reduce peak demand and the need for new generating power capacity.
* Optimize: control cooling loads, for example with smart sensing and behavioral change approaches; and
* Protect vulnerable urban or rural areas from extreme heat and the consequences of unreliable or insufficient medical and agriculture cold chains.

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Policy, regulatory and enabling environment support (Total cost: USD 93.6 million; GCF cost: USD 15.7 million)

* This component will support activities that aim to strengthen institutional, policy and regulatory frameworks, raise awareness and stimulate behavioral changes, as well as build the capacity of key stakeholders.
* The activities are expected to contribute to the country’s NDCs and NAPs, and to plan for responses to increased heatwaves and other extreme events. Enabling activities will include costing-specific inputs (related to both climate mitigation and adaptation) into the country’s energy sector planning processes, as well as providing technical assistance and support such as Climate Action Plans, as well as inputs on national policies and regulations that encourage sustainable cooling.
* It will also support activities linked to the Facility's Gender Action Plan.

Activities relevant to the eligible cooling areas supported under Component 1 may include the following:

* Capacity-building of key stakeholders, for example, on technologies, business models, cooling project appraisal, implementation and evaluation;
* Technical assistance in developing policies, regulations and standards;
* Designing sustainable market mechanisms and business models; and
* Communications and awareness campaigns.

### Component 2: Financing for cooling investments (Total cost: USD 769 million; GCF cost: USD 193.3 million)

In order to promote and accelerate the uptake of innovations in renewable and climate-friendly cooling technologies and systems, including in markets with low penetration and limited access to finance, this component will provide financial support for the development of sustainable cooling solutions performing better than current market practice and at the same time ensure that these solutions are affordable. The objective is to make them more affordable) to expand access to – and deployment of - more climate-friendly cooling at scale and avoid promoting a prohibitively expensive market niche, which would hinder the goals of scaling up access and deployment.


---
**Page 109**
---

22. The Facility will provide concessional funds to foster the adoption or scale-up of sustainable cooling technologies, appliances' deployment through financing of climate-friendly cooling investment and credit lines to financial intermediaries.

23. The financial instruments will be calibrated to what is needed to make the cooling project financially viable, including but not limited to: (a) Subsidies for end-users, including addressing the incremental cost associated with the adoption of efficient cooling solutions or serve remote off-grid areas; (b) Subsidies for the private sector, including addressing the high transaction costs and market entry barriers arising from the underrepresentation of efficient and clean cooling solutions in mass developed markets; (c) Financial instruments to support the development of a financing pipeline to meet the needs of the projects, through various financing modalities, for example, public financing to sovereign or sub-sovereign entities, blended finance, grants, equity investments, loans or subsidies to households, microenterprises and small, medium and large enterprises.

24. This component will focus on different financing and implementation mechanisms and models that can be used by the Facility to support the deployment of cooling solutions to be deployed by different projects of the Facility based on specific country and sector conditions. Examples of these financing and implementation mechanisms and models are as below.


### 2.3.1 Public procurement and contracting for cooling infrastructure service development

25. GCF funds would be used to support investments through a traditional public procurement and contracting model. Interventions that promote the deployment of a good (for example, air conditioning units) or services (for example, installation of air conditioning in El Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe and Somalia). This model includes the following activities:

(a) Prepare the technical specifications and terms of reference of the cooling infrastructure or equipment to be procured;

(b) Launch the procurement process to invite bids from suitable contractors;

(c) award the procurement contract;

(d) Sign contractual agreements with the winning bidders;

(e) Monitor the delivery of goods and services to ensure quality control using own resources or through an owner's engineer procured by the project; and

(f) Remunerate the contractor according to the agreed terms.

2.2.3 Credit lines

- A key barrier to investing in energy efficiency, in general, and sustainable cooling, in particular, is the limited availability of commercial financing for these projects.
- The Facility will support the provision of credit lines, for example in Malawi, to help financial intermediaries (FIs) provide loans to project developers.
- Credit lines can be provided by FIs or directly from the GCF. Credit lines can be well suited in less mature markets; they help mitigate the perceived high financial risk of energy efficient and clean cooling, which may be carried out by energy service companies (ESCOs).
- A key feature is that ESCOs enter into a performance-based contract with the user for design and implementation of cooling and energy efficiency measures. The services may also include providing or arranging financing to the user.
- The ESCO enters into an agreement with the executing entity under which the payments from the energy user to the ESCO are contingent upon achieving certain pre-specified performance levels. This approach allows energy users to transfer financial risk associated with cooling and energy efficiency measures to the ESCO.
- The concept of energy saving performance contracts (ESPCs) implemented by SSQCs has been recognized as a promising approach to overcome some of the most intractable market barriers.

2.2.3 Energy efficiency financing facility

- Under this model, the GCF proceeds are channelled through the executing entity (EE). The EE either directly or, through an entity that it designates (the project implementing entity (PIE)), on-lends the funds to sub-borrowers (for example, private ESCOs, cooling as a service companies, cooperatives, or small- and medium-sized enterprises) to undertake eligible cooling investments.

## The energy efficiency financing facility implementation arrangement involves two main variations, as shown in figure 3.

### a. Public super ESCO model
In this variation of the energy efficiency financing facility model, the host country designates a public energy service company (the public super ESCO). The public super ESCO is responsible for implementing the following activities: (a) support cooling and energy efficiency investments in the public sector (public buildings and sustainable cooling projects) and (b) strengthen private sector ESCOs through demand aggregation, capacity building, and other technical assistance. This model is envisaged for Bangladesh.

### b. Public super ESCO implementation model


---
**Page 112**
---

### (3-b) Energy efficiency revolving facilities (EERF)
Under the EERF model, the PVE uses contracting mechanisms to recover its investment and operating costs based on contracts such as energy service agreements (ESAs) or also uses more traditional revolving financing instruments. The facility finances the project implementation and/or the facility finances and undertakes the investment on behalf of the beneficiary. In return, the beneficiary makes regular payments to the facility covering energy savings generated by the implemented EERF over an agreed period so that the EERF can recover its investment and operating costs. For the beneficiaries, the recurring payment (consisting of the reduced energy bill and the payment to the EERF) is a fixed cost and not subject to market fluctuations.

### Component 3: Project management (total cost: USD 19.2 million; GCF-cost: USD 0 million)
This component will support the ESAs and project implementing entities in the management of their projects. The project implementing entities are required to comply with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into with the AE. There is no funding request from GCF for project management.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
**Scale: High**

The Cooling Facility has a mitigation impact potential of 16,24 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MCO2eq) avoided for the lifetime of the project. A total of 4.22 million people will be directly and/or indirectly impacted by this project, including 3.75 million direct beneficiaries and 0.47 million indirect beneficiaries. The Cooling Facility envisages enhancing the resilience of 18,057 physical assets, including buildings, agricultural and health cold chains.


---
**Page 113**
---

3 Adaptation - The Cooling Facility will be engaged in countries facing a high risk of extreme heat hazards: Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi and Somalia. The impacts of extreme heat are expected to increase significantly by 2050, with up to 14 million people over four million direct and over sixteen million indirect beneficiaries. It will facilitate more than 300 million hours of work avoided per year.

(b) Mitigation - It is estimated that the Cooling Facility will lead to approximately 1 million tonnes of avoided or reduced GHG emissions per year across the countries supported by the Facility, with up to 250 million kg CO₂e and more than 14 million tonnes of GHG reduced within the lifetime of the project. This amounts to nearly 2 per cent reduction in global cooling-related emissions, which is quite substantial from a sector point of view.

As a multi-sectoral facility, the impacts will be envisioned across these cooling sub-sectors:

(a) Kigali Amendment and refrigerants: The Facility will also support the goals and requirements of the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol by helping countries in the transition away from hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs). This will be achieved through energy efficient cooling equipment (air conditioners and refrigeration) in parallel with HFC phase-down. The estimated avoided emissions for the countries supported by the Facility have been estimated as ante, in line with global estimates indicating that the Kigali Amendment refrigerants represent around 20 per cent of total cooling equipment's overall GHG emissions.

(b) Space cooling: Space cooling projects will promote a) policy measures to provide incentives for energy efficient cooling systems and appliances; b) increased efficiency and lower GWP refrigerants' use, thereby shifting the business-as-usual trajectory of cooling GHG impacts towards higher efficiency/low-GWP cooling;

(c) Refrigeration and cold chains: This will contribute to a more resilient food cold chain by improving the efficiency of refrigeration, storage and distribution infrastructure for agricultural products. The project will also support the transition from direct exposure to health facilities and cold chains to safely store vaccines and medicine, reducing emissions by 27 MtCO₂e under the diagram of the proposed interventions.

(d) GHG emissions: The Cooling Facility will contribute to the expansion of affordable cooling in off-grid contexts which will reach some of the poorest communities and contribute to a reduction in energy poverty. This will be achieved through both grid- and non-GHG-emitting renewable energy (solar) with mostly power (126 kW) these critical cooling services.

(e) Other impacts: Climate-friendly vaccine cold chains in three countries (El Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe, and Somalia) will help them respond to the current COVID-19 pandemic. The project will also contribute to: [1] improved access to medicines and vaccines; [2] investments will lower the incidence of heat-related illnesses, as well as mental stress; and [3] improved local air quality through the displacement/avoidance of diesel generators.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High


---
**Page 114**
---

The Cooling Facility envisions working across key cooling sub-sectors and with a range of countries from different parts of the developing world via a combination of targeted technical assistance and country-specific projects, accompanied by investment support for country-identified national priority sectors to:

* Develop an enabling environment for cooling, via policy/regulations such as MEPS, S&Ls, National Cooling Strategies;
* Build and generate a track record in the cooling sector;
* Amplify the potential to leverage experience gained and lessons learned;
* Provide pioneering examples that can be emulated through different models and approaches, including those developed by private sector companies in countries;
* Allow governments to see how the policy activities are delivered and delivered at sector level via investments; and
* Further accelerate the take-up of the shift towards sustainable cooling investments.

The Cooling Facility will work with countries to mainstream sustainable cooling into their development and climate plans, procurement strategies and policy/regulatory frameworks. The programme will also provide technical assistance for developing countries, while also helping raise awareness of the availability and benefits of sustainable cooling technologies and methodologies.

The Cooling Facility will parallel also focus on different implementing models: a) credit lines that help local banks develop a business line for cooling investments and build their capacity to design and implement cooling and energy efficiency measures; b) private sector investment funds that can be used by governments, development agencies or others to design and implement cooling and energy efficiency measures; c) public-private partnerships (PPPs) with the aim of developing a pipeline of projects; d) capital expenditure for cooling investments so that the funds can be re-invested and the EEFIP can become a self-sustaining model.

The programme will also set up revolving financing that allows financing to continue after the end of the projects; b) enable private sector investment in cooling infrastructure through public-private partnerships (PPPs); c) send strong market signals for shifts towards sustainable cooling through public procurement, thus exemplifying the financial sustainability and scale-up potential within the cooling sector.

The programme has a high potential to generate environmental and health benefits, including reduction in air pollution, increase in access to resources and cold chains for vaccines and health products, and improved food security.

The Cooling Facility will provide benefits over multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). For instance:

* Decrease in the Global Economic Growth (SDG 8): Higher temperatures and lack of cooling negatively impact performance labour productivity. It is estimated that by 2050, work hours lost due to heat may be as high as 12 per cent in the worst affected regions of South Asia and West Africa - representing 4 per cent of annual GDP.

**Page 115**

### Needs of the recipients

#### Scale: High

- **Bangladesh**: The country faces serious energy security challenges and severe heat stress in urban areas. The Cooling Facility will enable Bangladesh to meet its cooling needs in a more climate-resilient manner, contributing to lower GHG emissions (by strengthening their institutions and implementation capacity) while addressing the barriers to access to cooling technologies.
- **El Salvador**: The country is committed to building long-lasting resilience and prepared to handle future health crises. The Government of El Salvador seeks to strengthen the overall national immunization and related health delivery systems, including cold chain infrastructure, by providing a more reliable supply of vaccines and related services.
- **Kenya**: Demand for cooling is projected to grow significantly in the Nairobi and Mombasa markets, with a projected 1.8 million cumulative unit sales between 2020 and 2030 for off-grid households alone. There are an estimated 5.4 million off-grid household refrigerators currently in use, which consume more than 70% of their electricity by running a refrigerator. Energy efficiency will help lower the projected energy consumption to meet Kenya’s climate mitigation goals while also benefiting smallholder farmers and businesses, and reduce GHG emissions in line with the country's climate mitigation goals.


---
**Page 116**
---

### Country ownership

Scale: High

#### Malawi
A lack of access to cooling, particularly more sustainable cooling solutions with lower operational costs, is hindering potential opportunities for micro, small and medium-sized enterprises involved in a number of important sectors such as dairy and horticulture. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will provide co-financing for renewable-based cooling investments, with a focus on women and the youth, which will contribute to providing economic opportunities for these groups while also ensuring access to affordable and reliable energy services for vulnerable and marginalized population groups, while also avoiding expensive and polluting energy consumption.

#### North Macedonia
Buildings consume about 39 per cent of the energy in the country, and represent a major priority. The public sector has the greatest potential, with about 45–60 per cent of total building stock owned by the state. In addition to its high heating demand, increasing summer temperatures in Europe have increased the demand and need for cooling, especially in schools and hospitals. Improving thermal response capacity is a key priority for North Macedonia’s energy sector, which will contribute to the country's adaptation to the changing climate. In the context of the National Energy Strategy 2035, the development of district heating systems, adequate air conditioning systems, is expected to become a higher priority.

#### Panama
According to the National Energy Plan of Panama, cooling systems consume about 14 per cent of total electricity consumption and are projected to increase as electricity consumption increases. The electricity sector is facing serious challenges in keeping up with rising demand coupled with the need for energy efficiency improvements. In 2009, the share of cooling systems was estimated at around 8 per cent; it is expected to reach about 13–15 per cent by 2030 and an estimated 64 per cent by 2050: the need for efficient cooling systems is a priority for Panama.

#### Sao Tome and Principe
The Government was initially unable to quickly establish laboratory capacity to diagnose COVID-19. Notwithstanding the fact that the country is now better equipped, there are still significant challenges in terms of testing capacity, a significant and more comprehensive strategy to strengthen the public health system is needed. The Government has developed a national plan for the fight against COVID-19 in the country and to design and implement the national COVID-19 immunization plan, which includes a robust vaccine cold chain.

#### Somalia
The lack of access to cooling is a pressing problem in Somalia, exacerbated by an extremely weak primary health-care system, inadequate surveillance and laboratory capacity, as well as limited access to medicines and vaccines and cold chain capacity, which prevents expanding the currently extremely low vaccine coverage and will make distribution of COVID-19 vaccines difficult. Likewise pharmacists and medical personnel are not adequately equipped or trained for the treatment of infectious conditions. Moreover, without support, it is expected that keratoac, petrol and diesel fuel prices will continue to rise, making access to cooling even more expensive.

#### Sri Lanka
Commercial buildings (including government buildings) and industrial buildings contribute 60 per cent of the electricity consumption in the country. The Sustainable Cooling for All (SC4All) project aims to provide energy efficient cooling solutions through a Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation Management programme that includes chillers, fans, refrigerators, air conditioning and green roofs. The project will also include the installation of solar panels, photovoltaics, and green walls. But the major barrier to meet the needs of the people for these technologies is the lack of adequate type of financing: The Cooling Facility via the EERF with ESCO model could be a solution.


---
**Page 117**
---

### Bangladesh: The Government has prepared the Energy Efficiency Conservation Master Plan (EECMP) to reduce energy consumption and promote energy efficiency and conservation regulatory measures. In addition, the project will inform the Government's efforts in developing a National Cooling Action Plan.

#### Kenya: The project aims to reduce electricity demand by 30% or 142 GWh by 2030. The National Electrification Strategy presents a real opportunity for Kenya to achieve its energy efficiency and conservation goals, as well as reducing GHG emissions by 30 per cent, or 142 GWh, by 2030.

#### Malawi: Promoting the use of energy-efficient technologies and designs is one of the strategic priorities in the National Electrification Strategy (NES) and the Medium-Term Development Strategy (MTDS III). Since agriculture is one of the major contributors to GHG emissions, improving the efficiency of agricultural cold chains in agriculture will contribute to the achievement of the MDGS II goal.

#### North Macedonia: The Facility will support achieving the Government's energy saving commitments by establishing a financial mechanism and investing into efficient space cooling of buildings.

#### Panama: Panama has ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2017, which aims to phase down HFCs. The project will implement the Second Stage of its National Action Plan under the Montreal Protocol. The Facility support will achieve the Government's commitments by promoting energy efficiency and conservation measures.

#### El Salvador: The Government is integrating climate-friendly investments into its national programmes and its NDC includes health as a sector affected by climate change. The project will contribute to the achievement of the MDGS II goal aligned with the Facility’s support for climate-friendly health facilities and cold chains.

#### Sao Tome and Principe: The country's NDC include promoting the greater use of renewable energy sources, including solar power, which is increasingly threatened by climate change. The project will contribute to the Government's NDC through supporting space cooling and cold chain infrastructure, including through the increased use of solar power.

#### Somalia: Somalia’s NDC related to scaling up the use of solar energy is consistent with the Facility’s support for more efficient cooling systems in agricultural cold chains, including through greater use of solar power.

#### Sri Lanka: Demand Side Management is identified as one of the seven mitigation actions of the National Action Plan. The project will implement the Energy Efficiency Building Code sets standards for energy efficiency in design and retrofits in buildings, at the same time providing a cost-effective solution to reduce GHG emissions. The project will also match the Facility’s support of space cooling and energy efficiency measures in buildings.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Programme background:** The programme aims to support subprojects in the areas of space cooling equipment; refrigeration and cold chains (including cold storage); water, environment, cooling equipment; building automation and control; and solar and vegetative roofs and walls; and refrigeration, cold chains and logistics (including refrigeration, storage, and distribution). The programme consists of three components that focus on: (1) supporting policy and the regulatory and enabling environment for energy efficiency in buildings and appliances, including cooling systems; others; (2) providing finance for eligible cooling investments through financing modalities that will provide access to capital for project developers, investors, and other stakeholders; and business models; and (3) project management to support the AE and PIs/Es perform their project management functions.

**Environmental and social (E&S) risk category and safeguards instrument:** The AE has assessed that the activities under the Cooling Facility will have potential for limited adverse E&S risks. These risks are generally considered as low in terms of severity, largely reversible, and can be readily addressed through mitigation measures. The supported activities are not expected to result in significant environmental or social impacts.

**Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards:** The programme has submitted an ESS that provides a description of how the E&S risks and impacts will be assessed, managed, and mitigated at both the project level and the programme level. The ESS describes the necessary instruments and information related to environmental and social impact assessment or audit and management at the programme level. The ESS also outlines the


---
**Page 119**
---

## ESS2: Labour and working conditions

Construction and implementation of the subprojects will be carried out by either directly employed or contracted/sub-contracted workers. Occupational health and safety plans will be prepared as per the requirements of the host country laws and regulations. All migrant labourers who work on the projects have to comply with these requirements including the preparation of a labour management procedure. Since an influx of migrant labour will also have potential impacts in relation to worker conflicts, it is important that there are measures put in place to prevent or mitigate conflict among the subproject workers as well as with the host local communities; these risks/impacts will be managed through the implementation of appropriate procedures and guidelines related to the labour management procedure and other appropriate plans and procedures. These measures should also include a dedicated grievance redress mechanism for migrant labourers/sub-contractors. A dedicated grievance redress mechanism for subproject workers will also be established.

## ESS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

While the interventions under the programme are generally expected to promote efficiency and resource conservation, construction activities associated with the implementation of the subprojects are likely to generate typical construction impacts such as dust and air pollution from excavations and vehicle operations. The disposal of hazardous waste (e.g., asbestos) will also generate the potential need to handle and dispose of hazardous waste (e.g. asbestos and wastes from dismantling/disposal of old cooling equipment and refrigerant gases). These risks/impacts will be managed through appropriate procedures and guidelines related to resource efficiency and pollution prevention. The impacts are expected to be low to moderate and are reversible if located in nature, appropriate countermeasures can be implemented as per the best available technology (BAT) and international industry practice.

## ESS4: Community health safety and security

Construction facilities and infrastructure development activities will have potentially adverse effects on the health, safety and security of the host communities. Impacts may arise from noise and vibration; dust emission; and disposal of hazardous waste. The construction of new buildings or cold storage facilities may also result in incidents as a result of vehicle transport in and out of construction areas which may also affect the community’s safety can be managed through proper implementation of adequate and appropriate countermeasures as per international industry practice.

## Land acquisition/evacuation requirements

The programme may result to land acquisition for construction of new buildings or cold storage facilities and may have potential adverse environmental and social impacts associated with land acquisition or land
