#### Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESF 7: Indigenous Peoples

The AE will ensure that the presence of indigenous peoples in the areas identified for the projects will be screened and confirmed by the AE in consultation with the national authorities. The AE outlines that, once a project is approved, the AE will work closely with indigenous communities to integrate them into the design of the project and E&S risk documents, including the development of an Indigenous Peoples Action Plan (IPAP) as part of the E&S risk document.

#### Cultural heritage

The ESF indicates that subprojects will be screened and assessed regarding potential impacts on tangible and intangible cultural heritage. Site selection for each subproject should take into account the preservation of cultural heritage, including archaeological sites, sacred places, and other culturally significant areas. The AE should ensure that there is a possibility for presence and/or discovery of items of cultural significance during construction.

#### Implementation arrangements

In accordance with the ESF, the AE will manage the overall implementation of the project and monitor its progress in line with the approved project documents as well as monitor their performance in accordance with the requirements of the legal agreements. The AE will also ensure that the environmental and social performance measures are agreed between the AE and the borrowers. The AE will likewise provide implementation support regarding the environmental and social performance of the subprojects, which will include monitoring and reporting on progress against these measures. The AE will share such information with the GCF through the annual performance reports.

#### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The program has considered stakeholder engagement as a key element in project design. These engagements included meetings and consultation workshops with representatives from implementing agencies, government officials, local communities, indigenous peoples, farmers, investors, private representatives, and concerned communities. In addition, other interested stakeholders such as civil society organizations, non-governmental organizations, and development partners working in the same region.

A detailed stakeholder engagement plan (SEP) will be developed for each of the subprojects to ensure transparency and meaningful consultation with all relevant stakeholders. The SEP will include a process for stakeholder identification, analysis, information dissemination, consultation, and engagement that will be

### Programme-specific execution risks: medium risk

#### Demand risk:
The impact achieved by the project will depend on the financing opportunities for the envisaged projects materializing, i.e., impact depends on continued utilization of GCF funds in such opportunities. Demand risk is partially mitigated by technical assistance under the programme to support countries in developing and implementing their climate change adaptation strategies, including activities such as cooling market studies, sector gap analysis and strategies, knowledge sharing, stakeholder consultation, business model identification. It is advised that the demand for financing be assessed on an annual basis and that a demand assessment shall be invested in sub-projects for the duration of the programme.

#### Sovereign risk:
The GCF will not have any exposure to sovereign loans under the programme. The GCF will not benefit from the preferred credit status of the AE. The credit risk of the countries under the programme varies from lower grade medium (BBB by S&P, Paastran) to substandard (CCC by Fitch). The AE has a high credit rating (A3 by Moody’s and Baa1 by S&P) with a low default probability. The AE is also rated by a major rating agency (e.g., Somalia and Sao Tome and Principe). The AE has included in the terms of conditions that it will not be held liable for any claims or losses arising from the failure to meet its obligations under this agreement.

#### Co-financing risk:
Lower than indicative financing amounts provided by the AE and related entities could affect the impact of the programme. Co-financing presented in the funding proposal is indicative only, as the actual co-financing amount will be determined based on co-financing before funded activity agreement (FAA) execution. A minimum co-financing ratio of 1:2 is recommended for projects with a high climate impact and a minimum co-financing ratio of 1:4 in section E.2 of the funding proposal. However, the AE notes that 54 per cent of the co-financing presented in the funding proposal has been committed by the time this document was approved and signed the corresponding legal agreements with the recipient countries for six of the seven projects included in the funding proposal. The AE will ensure that the minimum co-financing ratio is maintained to reflect the climate impact of the project. It is recommended that a minimum co-financing ratio of 1:4 as a programme basis is included so that the efficiency of GCF funding remains as envisaged.

#### Operational risk:
The operational risks are related to the availability and quality of technical capacity on the ground as well as political factors relating to affordability. The AE will develop a monitoring plan for each project, taking into account the level of technical capacity available, technical capability risk and will take into account impacts on affordability of the interventions.

#### Carbon credits:
The termsheet includes no limitation on the sale of carbon credits. Sale of carbon credits is subject to the terms and conditions set out in the terms and conditions of the programme. The terms of sale on the sale of carbon credits are included in the terms and conditions of the programme

### Compliance risk: medium risk

#### UN Security Council Resolution 1373 (UNSC):
Of the countries involved in this proposal, it is confirmed that Somalia is subject to United Nations Security Council (UNSC) Resolution 1373 (L92). However, the World Bank, as the AE, has determined that the implementation of the proposed project will have no material impact on the proposed project being considered under the Cooling Facility. The World Bank acknowledges that it may be necessary for Somalia to take additional measures in order to facilitate humanitarian assistance to the Somali population and it does not rule out/exclude any particular measure.

#### Accreditation agreement (AMA):
In confirming that no individual or entity that is listed on any UNSC Sanctions List will be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, the World Bank referred to its obligations in this regard as stated in paragraph 4.06(A)(ii) of the accreditation mission agreement (AMA).

#### Project viability and concessionality:

The AE will differentiate the level of concessionality provided by the GCF. If a host country is in external debt distress or receives very low or high concessional loans. If the host country is in external debt distress, it will receive GCF non-reimbursable funds and will only receive co-financing exclusively on grant terms. Any other projects will receive low concessionality.

Within the above parameters, GCF shall rely on the AE to appropriately deploy the concessionality. ### 4.5 Fiduciary

The World Bank will be the Accredited Entity for this Programme. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that the Projects are developed and implemented by the relevant Executive Entities (EEs) or Project Implementation Entities (PIEs) in accordance with its policies and procedures and the relevant provision of the AMA and FAA, and selected according to the criteria set out in the TSP. The AE will also provide oversight of the Projects including necessary monitoring, supervision, and quality assurance in accordance with the terms and conditions of the Agreement between the AE and the FAA.

Each Project will be carried out through different Executive Entities (EEs) and PIEs as further described below. EEs are as listed on FP. For indicative purposes, Table 1 on the TS, section 4.2.3 provides a summary of the TSP for each EE. The AE will also oversee all Projects in specific Host Countries. For the avoidance of doubt, the AE will not act as an EE for the Project and will not be responsible for the financial management or disbursement of funds. Although the PIEs are not included within the definition of EEs for this Programme, as they will carry out part or all of the project implementation in accordance with the due diligence on them in accordance with class 102.4(2)(i) of the AMA as if they were included within the definition of EEs for this Programme and the outcome of such due diligence will be included in the respective TSPs.

In respect of each Project for which a disbursement is requested, AE will deliver to the Fund a copy of the Accredited Entity’s Financial Management Plan (FMP), containing information on the relevant Project. FMP will include the capacity assessment of the EEs.

Each project financed through the Cooling Facility will be required to conduct financial management in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the TSP for each project supported by the Cooling Facility giving effect to the AE’s FMP as applicable.

For any project supported by the Fund is required to maintain financial management arrangements (including planning and budgeting, accounting, internal control, funds flow, financial reporting, and audit) that are consistent with the terms of the Agreement (applicable for project implementation) acceptable to the AE. A financial management specialist is expected to be part of the team forming the FMP established by each relevant Executing Entity (or PIE as applicable).

Moreover, for each project, the AE task team includes an accredited Financial Management Specialist (FMS) who will provide oversight and support in the areas of project preparation, supervision and implementation) in accordance with the AE’s operational policies and procedures (to work with partner countries to design financial management and disbursement arrangements that are consistent with the terms of the Agreement and the TSP for project implementation operations). The team’s FMS specialist closely monitors the performance of the EEs (or PIE as applicable) and ensures that the financial management arrangements are being used in a proper, responsible manner such that loan proceeds are being used for the purposes intended.


---
**Page 128**
---

# Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting initiative, the programme aims at generating both mitigation and adaptation benefits. On the mitigation side, the intervention is expected to result in overall GHG emission reductions of approximately 42 million tonnes CO₂ equivalent (tCO₂e) by 2030, based on metrics of the GCF core indicator for mitigation based on GHG accounting methodology detailed under annexes A and B.

Regarding the programme, the benefits expect to benefit 4.22 million direct and 16.85 million indirect beneficiaries in the four countries at high risk of extreme heat hazard (Bangladesh, India, Nepal, Pakistan). The AE provided a revised methodology for defining the baseline and calculating the mitigation impact.

The theory of change adequately explains how changes is understood to come about in the targeted countries. The goal statement is properly articulated, and the stated programme results are aligned with the overall GCF strategy and the specific goals of the climate project goal.

The logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including the inclusion of solid means of verification and reporting on the appropriate core indicators for both mitigation and adaptation, as well as the respective impact, outcome and programme-level indicators. The logic framework also includes a description of the monitoring and evaluation plan and result measurement frameworks. At the design stage the AE has not been able to provide credible estimates of the baseline emissions or the lifetime mitigation impact. As described in section E.5., the Secretariat has requested through a dedicated letter sheet covenant, that these are updated together with each annual performance report and finalized prior to the interim independent evaluations.

The implementation timeline in Annex 5 of the Funding Proposal should be updated during the design phase of the programme, as the AE is currently unable to provide this and it will not be negotiated and agreed.

The monitoring and evaluation plan (annex 11 of the funding proposal) has been found to follow the minimum requirements because it includes the entire list of the programme logical framework indicators while ensuring alignment of the data/sources with established means of verification.

The Secretariat considers, however, that the programme does not provide sufficient safeguards for the GCF mitigation impact and, therefore, has agreed to discuss further with the AE during the design phase of the programme in order to ensure that no carbon credits by the programme, while maintaining the integrity of the lifetime mitigation impact.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP177

## Proposal name: Cooling Facility

### Accredited entity: World Bank

#### Country/ies:
- Bangladesh
- El Salvador
- Kenya
- Malawi
- North Macedonia
- Panama
- Sao Tome and Principe
- Somalia
- Sri Lanka

#### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to low

This is a large-size funding proposal submitted by the World Bank as the accredited entity (AE). The programme covers nine countries from different regions: four countries in Africa (Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, and Sao Tome and Principe), one country in Asia (Bangladesh), two countries in Latin America (El Salvador and Panama), and two countries in Eastern Europe (North Macedonia and Sri Lanka).

The funding proposal is for the estimation of a cooling programme to be implemented by the World Bank. The estimated cost of this project is US$ 974 million, which includes US$ 157 million GCF contribution.

### 2.0 Programme description

#### 2.1 Programme objective
The programme aims to contribute to shifting from low-efficient cooling systems/technologies to more efficient cooling systems/technologies through a number of subprojects.

#### 2.2 Project components
- 77 per cent of GCF proceeds will be used for the purchase of energy-efficient appliances and equipment.
- The remaining 23 per cent is for adaptation activities in the areas "industrial processes" and "urban cooling".

### 3.0 Programme impact

The programme aims to contribute to shifting from low-efficient cooling systems/technologies to more efficient cooling systems/technologies through a number of subprojects.

#### 3.1 Project components
- 77 per cent of GCF proceeds will be used for the purchase of energy-efficient appliances and equipment.
- The remaining 23 per cent is for adaptation activities in the areas "industrial processes" and "urban cooling".

### 4.0 Financial support

The total cost of funding proposal is US$ 974 million, including USD 157 million GCF contribution, which is 17.8 per cent of total cost. Twenty percent (USD 327 million) will be issued for 20 years with a 6.75 per cent interest rate as concessional loan. The rest, 80 per cent (USD 1,251 million), is for a senior concessional loan. This loan has an interest rate of 4.35 per cent and will be issued over 20 years with a grace period of five years.

### 5.0 Conclusion

According to the definition in the funding proposal, "The Cooling Facility is essentially designed as an umbrella facility for GCF support, aggregating through a programmematic approach different types and sizes of sustainable cooling interventions."


---
**Page 133**
---

The total contribution from the AE is USD 722.84 million of which 15 per cent is grant comprising contributions from the International Development Agency (IDA), the Global Financing Facility, the Health Emergency Preparedness and Response Trust Fund (HEPTF) and the Fund for Climate Change Adaptation (FCCA). The World Bank Loan (WBL) which is not still decided at the stage of submission and review of the proposal document. The WBL will be provided in two tranches with different pricing, grace periods and tenors (for details see section C.1 of the funding proposal).

for cooling infrastructure service delivery and technology transfer. The model will be based on a public procurement and contracting framework. Final beneficiaries, in this financial model, could be households, sole traders, micro, small and medium enterprises (MSMEs) or other entities. ### Cooling investments

Projects selected under the Cooling Facility will support investments in the following target areas: (1) space cooling and cool/cooling solutions, and (2) GWP refrigerants. Projects must be financially sustainable and demonstrate a pathway towards financially sustainable models that reduce the need for public funding. The projects should also have a clear business case and be able to attract private sector funding.

In the case of investing in off-grid cold storage capacity additions under (2) refrigeration, it is expected that the renewable energy sources used to provide energy to power these additional cold storage facilities are renewable energy (e.g., solar).

### World Bank operation linkage

A Cooling Facility-supported project may (1) co-finance with other World Bank/IDA/IBRD projects or (2) be linked to a World Bank/IDA/IBRD operation. The ratio of GCF funds to IDA/IBRD funds for each supported project will be determined by the TAP and will not exceed 50%. The amount of co-financing or linkage funding that can be provided under the GCF processes that are spent for cooling purposes will be monitored when proceeds are added to the project. It is noted, however, that the implementation of energy efficiency measures for heating purposes does not always affect energy for cooling. In some cases, it might be vice-versa and heating energy efficiency measures facilitate the increase of cooling energy consumption (e.g., in the case of North Macedonia, the GCF processes are aligned with the TAP). The TAP notes that cooling efficiency monitoring indicators should be transparently described and reported.

### Performance threshold

The threshold is stated as "at least 20 per cent energy efficiency improvement and/or at least 20 per cent avoided/reduced GHG emissions". According to the definition, this means that a project must achieve an average of 20% for the total project. A project, according to the definition confirmed during the review, is in most cases expected to have a cooling component. Therefore, it is recommended that each project an estimate of GHG reduction impact should be developed based on publicly available information and/or data provided by the project proponents as outlined in annex 24 of the funding proposal. Regarding this criterion, the independent TAP notes that "20 per cent energy savings in some low-income countries will not have any tangible impact on reducing emissions from the world's 'top emitters'. The assessment of performance threshold is not relevant: the 20 per cent GHG reduction should be obligatory for all projects. As mentioned above, the monitoring and verification of the results of this programme will be compulsory and the TAP notes that information related to the cooling process should be developed for each participant country.

### Alignment with the transition to low global warming potential (GWP) refrigerants

The GCF processes are aligned with the TAP. This means that they apply only to the subprojects considering refrigerators in the cooling process (TAP) Projects supported by the Cooling Facility will not use hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs). The HFCs are subject to Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, under which hydrofluorocarbon (HFC) refrigerants are phased out. The TAP notes that the use of HFC refrigerants is harmful to the climate. Investments will support the following activities: (1) providing cooling services using low GWP refrigerants and/or alternative cooling solutions; and/or (2) using refrigerants that are aligned with the Kigali Amendment and relevant safety standards, as commercialized in respective local markets.

*This criterion takes into account that very low GWP refrigerant alternatives may not be readily available in all local markets for all the applications considered in the project.

Adaptation to climate change (Note that this criterion should be applied only for Adaptation projects - "AP"). ### 1.3.3 Expected impact

29 The independent TAP notes that the project management unit, described in figure 4 of the funding proposal, is not appropriate for achieving the climate change related results anticipated by the programme. #### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

25 According to the feasibility study provided by the AE, global energy use for space cooling is projected to grow three-fold between 2016 and 2050, a majority of this growth occurring in developing countries. To ensure that this growth is aligned with the developmental needs of countries, this growth must be addressed with effectively designed strategies and solutions to avoid severe economic, power system and environmental impacts.


---
**Page 139**
---

29. The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol entered into force on 1 January 2019. The phase-down of HFC refrigerants under the amendment has the potential to avoid up to 0.3°C of warming by 2050 and up to 4°F by 2100.

The independent TAP recognizes that the increasing cost in demand could be regarded as a differentiating factor for this process that is particularly important for those countries most vulnerable to the increasing trends of heat indices (maximum daily temperature, CDDs and heat waves). The TAP notes that there are several options available to address these challenges: energy efficiency measures or by replacing conventional fossil fuels sources with renewable sources in the cooling sector. These options can be considered as mitigation actions for the cooling sector consistent with the potential of global impact. The independent TAP shares the AE’s rationale that enabling those countries most vulnerable to heat waves and other extreme weather events to adapt to this demand should be considered as adaptation. Hence, the independent TAP confirms that the concept considered within the funding proposal has paradigm shift potential in each country and globally.

The Cooling Facility considered in the funding proposal plans to demonstrate different optimal approaches for mitigation of GHG emissions from the cooling sector in a variety of countries with different but key cooling target areas and with a range of countries from different continents of the developing world having different barriers and national priorities. Diversion of financial mechanisms towards this sector is not straightforward, as it is not yet clear how to implement the AS if such a diversified approach could amplify the potential of the AE to leverage its experience in other sectors. The independent TAP notes that the funding proposal is demonstrating example models and approaches, results can be scaled up and be fit-for-purpose in more countries and regions.

The independent TAP does not fully agree with the AE on its position that the selected approach of putting under one programme a variety of countries with different national circumstances and barriers to implementation would be an effective way forward. A number of complicated financial schemes for implementation will be a more efficient way to put this subject matter into practice, as it is not yet clear how to implement the AS if such a diversified programmatic approach where countries and measures are concentrated around the narrower concept of cooling demand could amplify the potential of the AE to leverage its experience in other sectors. The independent TAP concludes that the funding proposal is the pilot case for testing the effective approaches for mitigation of GHG emissions from the cooling sector in a way that does not limit itself merely satisfying the climate change objective of reducing GHG emissions and countries.

The overall analysis by the independent TAP suggests that this financial mechanism as a pilot programme could not be rated as having a high paradigm shift impact; rather, more demonstration impact is expected, providing examples of success and failure and reasons for these outcomes. The concept itself has high transformational potential.


### Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium

Space cooling is increasingly recognized as a development priority and an issue of equity in its widespread use, the increasing cost of cooling demand and the increasing gap between energy supply and cooling demand is significantly exacerbated by COVID-19 management requirements.

As mentioned in the feasibility study (annex 2a), meeting cooling demand in a business-as-usual way leads to increased GHG emissions, but providing cooling is unavoidable if countries are to meet their development goals. The independent TAP analysis describes how


---
**Page 140**
---

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) contributes to sustainable development by demonstrating options for slowing the increasing trend of energy consumption through supporting energy efficiency and clean energy development processes in pilot countries, transfer of clean technologies and solutions, and capacity building. The GCF is also committed to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). In particular, it supports projects that contribute directly to the achievement of SDG 3: Good health and well-being; SDG 7: Affordable and clean energy; SDG 11: Sustainable cities and communities; and SDG 12: Responsible consumption and production.

The GCF's efforts in these areas are significant, particularly for countries focusing on energy efficiency in buildings. The programme also aims to reduce the global warming potential of refrigerants by phasing out HFCs contained in refrigerants, which could potentially avoid close to 1°C temperature increase.

Refrigerators and vapour compression-based equipment used in refrigeration and air conditioning account for about 20 per cent of the climate impact associated with cooling. Cooling is expected to grow significantly over the next few decades, and its contribution to global warming potential could rise by up to 65% by 2100.

The independent TAP notes that this first funding proposal on cooling efficiency remains unchallenged in the GCF's current portfolio. The TAP considers the role of this subsector in the sustainable development process as crucial and should have high priority, although the independent TAP is not sure whether it would be considered a climate change project at the country level and therefore scores this criterion as medium to low.

The G20 agreement to reduce greenhouse gases to tackle climate change - UN agency | 11UN News The legal framework for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) was adopted in 2015, beginning its operations in 2019 for developing countries, and most developed countries by late 2020. Its initial phased schedule will achieve a peak level of funding in 2023-24 for developed countries, whereas most developing countries will freeze consumption and production in 2024 and begin the phase down five years later. Developed countries susceptible to high ambient temperatures will freeze at 2028 and begin to phase down in 2032.

1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

The rise in temperature and lack of access to cooling will impact the health, education, productivity, and economic development of nations. In 2019, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change reported that at 1.5°C of warming, 2.3 billion people could be both exposed and vulnerable to heat wave events.

The climate risks faced by the nine countries involved in the programme significantly differ from each other, as described below:

- **Bangladesh**: Bangladesh is highly exposed to extreme heat, droughts, and wildfires. Extreme heat hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates that prolonged exposure to extreme temperatures will be a significant concern for the population.

The seasonal frequency of days with T ≥ 30°C has been on the increase since 1986 in most parts of Bangladesh. The monsoon season, from June to August, according to historical data for the past 30 years (1991–2020), reveals a significant increase in temperature compared to previous decades.

The number of heat waves has increased over time, with an average of 45 days per year between 1986 and 2009. However, this number is expected to rise to 73 days by the end of the century (2071–2099). By mid-century, annual CDDs are projected to rise from 422 CDD (2005–2029) to about 778 CDD by 2050 and 1,210 at the end of the century. Overall, this will result in an increase of 36% in annual CDDs over the period from 2005 to 2099.

- **El Salvador**: El Salvador is highly exposed to extreme heat, hurricanes, droughts and wildfires. Extreme heat hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates that prolonged exposure to extreme temperatures will be a significant concern for the population.

The country is also facing serious energy security challenges and severe stress, with the impact reaching its high-income regions, low-income neighborhoods and informal urban settlements. The Climate Fund will support investments in sustainable cooling solutions for the building sector as essential adaptation measures delivered through low-carbon efficient solutions.

El Salvador has experienced a sustained increase in monthly temperatures since 1986 (from April to August) have witnessed consistent increases in temperature. According to the future trends, the number of heat waves is expected to rise by 34 days per year between 2050 and 2079, an annual CDDs are projected to rise by 47% by 2050, and 1,370.68 CDD by the end of the century (2071–2099). The model is situated in the low corridor of Central America.

### To ensure an effective COVID-19 response and to generate long-lasting resilience and be prepared for future health crises, the Government of El Salvador is seeking to strengthen the overall national immunization and related health delivery systems. This includes a variety of issues, including strengthening the cold chain infrastructure in the country. The project will provide timing and financial means to enable the Government of El Salvador to take advantage of the window of opportunity for reforming its vaccine supply system, as it is well as climate-friendly.

- **El Salvador has asked for help on its health sector via a “Cooling Emergency Response Facility” (CERF) to implement sustainable cooling solutions.** The Cooling Facility will help not only with demand-side adaptation to extreme temperatures (for space cooling and cold chains in primary and secondary-level health-care facilities), but also with supply-side adaptation, which will turn up to safeguard the efficacy of vaccines and medicines, as well as essential medical equipment.

- **Malawi is prone to adverse climate hazards including extreme temperatures, dry spells, seasonal droughts, intense rainfall and flash floods.** Extreme heat hazard is classified as medium to high in 2015–2049, with a projected increase of up to 3°C by the end of this century. This will result in heat stress in likely to occur at least once in the next five years. The southern region of Malawi has experienced extreme heat events since 2008 and is expected to continue experiencing them until 2050. Between 1960 and 2004, mean annual temperature increased by 0.9°C. Climate trends over this period show that the total number of hot days in a year has increased by 30 days. By mid-century, annual CDDs are expected to increase from an average of 58 days per year (1960–2004) to more than 21 days.

- **In 2010, “Mazwi Financial Inclusion and Entrepreneurship Fund” project for agriculture sector, was approved by the World Bank Board. Refrigeration, cold storage and logistics for agriculture and food security are planned to be supported by the Cooling Facility within this project. The Cooling Facility will support the implementation of the cooling strategy that strengthens climate change policy framework of the Government of Malawi to promote the use of low-carbon technologies in the agricultural sector. This will further support choices for rural farmers.

- **Somalia is arid and semi-arid and with arid zones with two rainy seasons.** The country’s climate is influenced by the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone. Between 1960 and 2004, mean temperatures have increased by about 0.5°C in the hottest months (April and May) and 0.3°C in the coldest month (January). Climate trends over this period show that the total number of extreme heat hazards, including extreme heat and droughts. Risk of extreme heat hazard is considered high with projected increase up to 2°C by mid-century. The number of days where the daily maximum temperature exceeds 35°C is expected to increase from an average of 10 days per year (1960–2004) to more than 93 days by the end of the 21st century. Annual CDDs are expected to rise by more than 83% between 2040 and 2050, and by 1182 CDDs by the end of this century. This significant increase in temperature will require a cooling strategy that strengthens climate change policy framework for cooling.

- **The Cooling Facility will support financing and implementing solar power systems for space cooling and cold chains in primary and secondary-level health-care facilities to address**

### Kenya has included enhancement of energy and resource efficiency as one of the priorities for adaptation to climate change under its nationally determined contribution (NDC). Despite the priority given to adaptation in Kenya's NDC, the country recognizes the importance of clean, efficient, and sustainable energy systems. The government is committed to phasing out all non-sustainable biomass fuels. Kenya intends to include no-GC gases such as HFCs and perfluorocarbons (PFCs) under its NDC.

- Off-grid solar access for underserved counties is targeted by the Cooling Facility. Such support would be used for innovative business models to make cold chains available to small farmers, who are currently unable to afford them. The facility will also provide cooling and refrigeration systems (solar powered in off-grid contexts) for residential and business applications.

### In North Macedonia most of public buildings (including schools, health-care centers, etc.) were built between 1950 and 2000 years of age and clearly demonstrate, unsafe (due to structural instability and seismic risks) and dilapidated. In fact, buildings which contain air-conditioning systems are in a very poor condition. The government has given priority with estimates of savings ranging from 20 to 40 per cent. The public sector has the greatest potential for energy efficiency improvements due to its large share of total electricity consumption.

Several challenges need to be addressed for the public sector to invest in sustainable cooling. Central government buildings typically have very limited budgetary provisions for capital improvement projects, and are subject to a complex procurement process that involves going through the fiscal decentralization process and even then, stringent debt limitations which many have already reached. The lack of investment in energy efficiency improvements, rehabilitation of health-care facilities, with adequate air-conditioning systems, is expected to become more acute.

### North Macedonia is seeking to tackle space cooling in the public sector – and lower its energy consumption and associated GHG emissions - by establishing an energy efficiency fund, a sustainable cooling strategy, and a national building code.

### Panama's cooling appliances market has been growing rapidly over the past decade, for both air conditioning and refrigeration technologies. Air-conditioning penetration in the residential sector is estimated to be around 40 per cent of total electricity consumption. The country’s dynamic economic development (e.g. residential household ownership of air-conditioning equipment) is expected to continue driving demand for cooling appliances, with the market projected to grow to an estimated 66 per cent by 2015). According to the National Energy Plan of Panama (Final Energy Consumption Scenario), the cooling sector in the residential and commercial sectors currently represents about 40 per cent of the overall electricity consumption in Panama. The electricity sector is expected to continue growing at a rate of around 8 per cent year on average, but this is growing at an average annual rate of 8.5 per cent year by mainly due to the increased use of cooling equipment in the residential and commercial sectors. In order to align with Panama's NDCs, the country has set a target for energy efficiency improvements in the cooling sector from the business-as-usual scenario to around 4 per cent, which would reduce the need of energy generation capacity by 100 MW. This is expected to be achieved through the introduction and promotion to this rapidly growing problem. Although Panama has a sophisticated banking sector, the absence of (local) long-term capital market for mortgages (access of local banks to capital markets is limited to short-term loans), as well as the lack of awareness about energy efficiency products and investments, represent a major barrier for energy efficiency in Panama. The higher initial cost of energy-efficient equipment and lack of access to affordable financing

## The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

The GCF is a global mechanism for climate change mitigation and adaptation that provides financial resources to developing countries. It aims to support projects and programs that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and enhance resilience to the impacts of climate change.

### San Tome and Principe

San Tome and Principe is an island nation in the Gulf of Guinea, comprising two main islands: São Tomé (the larger) and Príncipe (the smaller). With a population of approximately 200,000 people, 62% of whom are aged 14 years or younger. In addition to having a small population and a remote location, there is a high level of poverty in the country, which has significant implications for health outcomes and human development outcomes. The public health sector is underdeveloped and this is a significant barrier to achieving universal health coverage (UHC). The Government of San Tome and Principe will be crucial to continue implementing mitigation measures to contain the spread of the pandemic in the country and to design and implement its National COVID-19 Recovery Plan.

### Sri Lanka

The demand-side management activities are one of the priority areas of Sri Lanka's NDC. Sri Lanka has recently taken strong initiatives to implement efficient and decentralized renewable energy systems, including solar power generation projects initiated through different modalities such as solar rooftops and solar parks. This will provide approximately 10% of the country’s electricity demand by 2030, with a target of achieving higher levels by 2050. In addition to the interventions concerning power generation, the Government has also taken several initiatives in energy efficiency in buildings in priority areas, and a Presidential Task Force has been appointed for implementing an island-wide demand-side management program.

### Improving Energy Efficiency

Improving energy efficiency in the building sector is a focus of a sample project. The proposed project will finance and promote the implementation of energy-efficient, climate-friendly cooling and other energy efficiency measures in Sri Lanka's commercial and industrial building sector.

The independent TAP considers that the needs of the recipient countries are high but are not fully met by the available funding for the GCF projects. Additional resources are provided for few cases. The information provided above, extracted from the funding proposals, is limited to a small number of projects in each country. The independent TAP scored this criterion as high mainly based on the information provided for three highly vulnerable participant countries identified in the assessment report, "Tracking Sustainable Cooling for All."

1.5 Country ownership

Scale: Medium

All nine countries involved in the programme have provided no objection letters.

In addition:
- All of these countries have submitted their updated NDCs.
- All of these countries have energy efficiency as one of the priorities for implementing their NDCs through their climate action plans.
- Bangladesh is among those nine countries that have transparently claimed the cooling sector as priority for energy efficiency measures; and
- Only Bangladesh has finalized and adopted a GCF country programme. Most of the other participating countries are in various stages of developing their country programmes.

Bangladesh:
The Government has prepared the Energy Efficiency Conservation Master Plan 2015–2030, which describes the adoption and implementation of energy efficiency and conservation measures in the power sector, including cooling appliances and systems. This effort is aligned with the Cooling Facility's support of space cooling and energy efficiency in the health sector.

El Salvador:
The Government is integrating climate-friendly investments in its national programmes, and its NDC includes health as a sector affected by climate change and the promotion of renewable energy sources. The Cooling Facility will provide technical assistance to support for climate-friendly health facilities and cold chain.

Kenya:
Under the Paris Agreement, Kenya has committed to reducing GHG emissions by 30 per cent below business-as-usual levels by 2030. The National Electricity Strategy presents a roadmap for achieving universal access to electricity by 2022. Supporting off-grid cooling appliances is one of the strategies advocated in this area under the strategy, and Kenya is also in the process of developing a National Energy Efficiency and Conservation Strategy, which includes energy efficiency measures in the health sector.

Malawi:
Promoting the use of energy-efficient technologies and designs is one of the strategies advocated in the Government's priority areas under the Malawi Growth and Development Strategy 2016–2035.

North Macedonia:
As a contracting party of the Energy Community Treaty, the Government has committed to reduce energy use by 12 per cent (about 200 kilo tonnes of oil equivalent) in buildings by 2020. This is more than the 7 per cent required under the Treaty's National Energy Efficiency Action Plans. Buildings and cooling appliances are included as a major priority, with estimates of energy savings ranging from 20 to 40 per cent.

Panama:
In 2012, the Government made a national commitment to implement energy efficiency by approving the National Energy Plan 2015–2030. In 2016, the National Secretariat of Energy published the National Energy Plan 2015–2030, which contemplates the design and implementation of energy efficiency measures in buildings and cooling appliances to meet the sector objectives. The country also ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2017. Panama is currently preparing its NDCs under the Paris Agreement and the Kigali Amendment Protocol. The Cooling Facility will support achieving the Government's commitments through its support for efficient space cooling.

See: Green Growth Principle:
The country’s NDC includes promoting the greater use of renewable energy and bits health as one of the sectors negatively impacted by climate change. The Cooling Facility will contribute to the Government’s NDC, through supporting space cooling and cold chains in the health sector, including through the increased use of solar power.

#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

- **Scale:** Medium to low

The share of the GCF total contribution to the total co-financing from the AIIB and other donors is 1:4.8. For the loan, this proportion is 1:4.5 (USD 280 million is reported as TBF) and for the grant it is 1:3.5 (USD 76 million is reported as TBF). The share of the grant to component 2 is considered by the independent TAP as effective distribution of the investment by components. The GCF concessional loan fully supports the most risky investment for component 2.

As already mentioned in section I.1, the independent TAP’s concerns regarding the maximum amount of co-financing that can be provided by the AIIB and other donors to the Secretariat in the term sheet and now all countries have some indicative budget. The independent TAP welcomes this approach, as it considers that all GCF eligible countries issuing non-bidirectional letters of credit (LoC) for the cooling facility will have a similar opportunity to access funding from the AIIB and other donors.

As mentioned above, the projects and measures to be implemented through the Cooling Facility are too diverse and therefore it is not possible, or efficient, to design unique eligibility criteria for each project. The following activities are considered as eligible under the Cooling Facility: (i) installation of solar-powered water pumps; (ii) installation of solar-powered refrigerators; renewable energy (solar) and GHEC-free refrigerators are considered as eligible activities for this Cooling Facility. It is important to note that the cooling facility will not be able to provide any financial support to countries in terms of technical assistance or capacity building, unless it is taken into consideration when designing measures, financial schemes and monitoring mechanisms. The independent TAP notes that the main focus on financial schemes has been put on financial schemes. These are important for GCF but only if balanced with technical assistance and capacity building activities. It would be difficult to confirm this for the independent TAP to confirm that, even in the case of success of the proposed financial models, the mitigation and/or adaptation results will be absolutely successful. Such diversity of the support provided by the Cooling Facility may have a negative impact on the programme’s overall efficiency and effectiveness.


---
**Page 147**
---

### (a) Efficiency in the building sector

- Bangladesh, North Macedonia, Panama and Sri Lanka: Types of buildings are not specified for Bangladesh but appliances are included; only public buildings are the focus for North Macedonia.
- Efficient cooling facilities (HFC-free refrigerators, cold chains, logistics): Kenya (off-grid solar energy and energy access to refrigeration), cold chains and logistics) for cooling purposes in health care and food security.

### (b) Strengthening the health sector within the context of COVID-19 requirements

El Salvador, San Tome and Principe, and Somalia: The World Bank is considering supporting these countries with potential investments from the Cooling Facility. The World Bank will work with each EE country to identify the most appropriate cooling solutions for their specific needs and opportunities.

### (c) Independent TAP regarding efficiency and effectiveness of whole programmes

The independent TAP is of the opinion that, due to the broad diversity of the energy efficiency scope, the primary focus should be on the overall impact of the programme in terms of energy savings and effects in the individual countries asking for support in the health sector or cool chain for food security. Three types of cooling technologies are considered:

- Cooling energy efficiency is a part of energy efficiency but it should be treated separately and not mixed with traditional energy efficiency measures when building energy consumption is calculated.
- The relationship between energy efficiency and energy savings for heating and cooling does not always coincide; indeed, they could contradict each other depending on the type of technology used. Therefore, it would be misleading to automatically add GEF investment to an Energy Efficiency programme (as has been done in the case of Bangladesh) or to a Cool Chain programme (in the case of North Macedonia) which is dedicated to the energy efficiency of the building without considering the impact of the programme on the EE criteria for the cooling sector; the expected effect of the programme could be negative.

### (d) In countries where existing less-efficient appliances (including refrigerators) will be replaced by more efficient ones, the increase in energy consumption should be calculated and deducted from the reduced amount, and vice versa. 

### (e) Allowance for a country-specific TAP approach to the implementation of such programmes for highly vulnerable countries that have limited access to cooling facilities under the prevailing requirements.

The independent TAP is of the opinion that the overall impact of such programmes may be added value compared with individual country-level projects and particularly for highly vulnerable countries with increasing threats of heatwaves (ECDs, heat waves, extreme daily temperatures). The independent TAP would recommend a programmematic approach: the scope of targeted cooling technologies and geography should be determined at the country level. A list of potential technologies and their costs should be provided for each country to ensure the added value of adopting a programmeatic approach.

The independent TAP would particularly like to highlight the role of well-designed monitoring, evaluation, learning (MEL) systems in supporting such programmes. The MEL approach considers various financial schemes, components and subprojects. The monitoring arrangements are expected to be developed by the country authorities with support from the independent TAP. The independent TAP would be interested to see more details of monitoring.

particularly for component 2 directly contributing to the final results. The AI should submit a very transparent monitoring plan for component 2 demonstrating how the final beneficiaries (not always borrowers) - consumers of energy - will be monitored in general and in particular when related to the cooling sector.

71. Based on the available information the independent TAP considers that the efficiency and effectiveness of this programme is expected to be medium to low. The independent TAP would also like to point out that the GCF Board has not yet received any baseline data from the countries implementing this programme having added value to individual project. The independent TAP notes that it is receiving a lot of information on the cooling sector, but most of these are related to studies on good concepts and ideas but not supported by relevant feasibility/baseline studies. Such projects should be considered as pilot projects with a learning approach and can always refer to assess against the six investment criteria of the GCF.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

76. Despite the deficiencies identified by the independent TAP and commented on in the above assessment, the independent TAP fully recognizes the urgent need to regulate the cooling sector, which is not yet relatively targeted as the most important subsector of the energy efficiency and demand side management (DSM) sector. The cooling sector has a significant mitigation-related energy needs for COVID-19 vaccination-related cooling processes. The independent TAP also notes that the GCF Board has already approved the funding proposal to the GCF Board. Although recommending approval, the independent TAP also recognizes that the funding proposal is subject to certain conditions and will be reviewed by the GCF Board prior to the execution of the funded activity agreement. The independent TAP recognizes that the rating for this funding proposal is currently at 3/5. The independent TAP notes that the independent TAP considers that, once the condition for endorsement is met, these ratings will go up. Hence, the independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval under the following conditions:

(a) Conditions to be met prior to the execution of the FAA.

(i) Delivery by the AEF to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of all documentation required either as a revised funding proposal pursuant to these annexes:
- (i) A cooling sector-related baseline study for each host country, which shall demonstrate that the cooling sector is an important component and trendsetter expressed by the independent TAP in this assessment report and (ii) the monitoring methodology for the cooling sector. The monitoring methodology is the "guidance" in annex 02a ("Pilot for Space Cooling") provided by the AEF as part of this funding proposal.
- (ii) The baseline study for each host country referred to in item (1) above shall estimate, take into account and set out: (i) ongoing trends of cooling sector energy demand; (ii) expected future trends of cooling sector energy demand; (iii) correlation between the heat index and energy consumption trends for space cooling; (iv) impact of COVID-19 on cooling sector energy demand; and (v) trends of energy consumption by cooling sector and related trends of GHGs where statistics are available;
- (iii) A monitoring methodology to be used in the host countries for monitoring the mitigation of energy consumption and reduction of GHGs from the cooling sector, which shall be based on a comparison with baseline situation. The monitoring methodology should consider: (i) monitoring of potential leakage and related mitigation measures; and (ii)

Cooling Facility

## Accredited entity:
World Bank

Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, Panama, São Tomé and Príncipe, Somalia, and Sri Lanka

Large

### Impact potential
Scale: We consider the rating of "medium to low" (especially "low") as under-estimating the Facility's potential impact. The World Bank As All is presenting an innovative, multi-sector and multi-country (covering 9 countries) programmatic financing facility to help demonstrate how to scale up cooling solutions in a cost-effective manner while minimizing negative climate impacts.

We would like to highlight ITAP’s observation that the approach to estimating impact in the FP is “conservative.”

#### Corrections/Clarifications:
- o ITAP notes that "only four countries are considered eligible for adaptation assistance" and does not clarify why each of the 9 countries in the Facility are counted against mitigation-related indicators. While each of the countries covered by the Facility has the need to adapt to a warming world as they are exposed to risks of extreme heat, droughts, and other climate hazards, only four countries are considered "high risk" of extreme heat hazard (the other 5 countries/“medium risk”) are counted against adaptation assistance.
- o In all projects, EE would be the Host Country ("the Borrower") acting through the relevant ministry(ies) or delegated Project Implementing Entities (PIEs).
- The policy framework for the Facility is based on the guidance in releases 1 and 2. This model will be implemented by the EE, or the PIE as designated by the AE (NOT the TA) and as such already corresponds with ITAP’s recommendations.
- Rather than representing examples of projects, Annex 22 provides, for each country, a description of the cooling solutions that are being considered under the Cooling Facility. If the Facility is approved by the GCF Board and in line with the terms of reference of a specific project program are early development stage, the AE, in consultation with each Host Country, will determine which specific projects and/or activities considering the agreed parameters.
- The AE will provide guidance on each country’s project. For each country, “base case” (i.e., baseline) assumptions were provided in Annex 23 (GIG Calculation of the Funding Proposal).
- o We agree that TA is support implementation of credit lines is important. TA is envisaged for every project supported by the Facility.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

- As noted above, there should be no doubt that the intention of the AE is that each country will have access to funding support under the Cooling Facility. This is a fundamental aspect of the Cooling Facility. Annex IT presents the amount sought for each country. Given the variability in climate conditions, the diversity of the varying absorption capacity and needs across countries, the cap of a maximum of $10 million per country is considered an appropriate level of additional measure to ensure that all countries can be supported through the Facility.
- The Cooling Facility aims to address the barriers that have so far limited the deployment of clean cooling technologies and also to put into place the support for a broader range of climate-friendly cooling solutions. It will provide support with a range of countries from different parts of the developing world to supply the necessary resources to scale up their cooling capacity. The Facility seeks to scale and mainstream sustainable cooling applications in a number of key sectors, including agriculture, industry, and buildings, thereby opening the potential for across-the-board deployment of climate-friendly solutions.
- The Facility adopts a pragmatic approach to deal with the nature of cooling and also takes into account the need to address the issue of energy security.
- ITAP expressed doubts regarding the "added value" of the Facility approach for cooling (compared to individual country projects). We would note that it was not our intention to create an additional facility. The Cooling Facility, especially the smaller countries would not be in a position to access critical GCF financing and therefore would not benefit from this facility.
- ITAP also expressed concerns regarding the fact that inclusion of a programme could have a negative impact on the programme's overall efficiency and effectiveness as well as all other programmes within the GCF. We respectfully disagree. The alternative to the support provided by the Cooling Facility is limited opportunities to enhance access to climate-friendly cooling. As just noted a learning-by-doing approach would be more appropriate for this range of technological applications, business models and implementation approaches for certain countries.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

We thank ITAP for its assessment and its recognition of the urgent need to support developing countries' moves towards climate-friendly cooling. We appreciate ITAP's favourable recommendation that the Cooling Facility be approved as a stand-alone facility by the GCF Board.


---
**Page 153**
---

We hope that the clarifications provided above help better understand the Facility, its functions and its aims.

We have also communicated our concern with ITAP’s recommended conditions and shared our suggested edits.

In addition to other points we raised and as we clarified in writing during ITAP's assessment period, on a subsequent call with ITAP (09/18/2021), and our comments to the ITAP's assessment, each Project must not be assimilated to a national programme. The proposed Projects are not necessarily aligned with the GCF’s country programmatic approach for Least Developed Countries' national programs but the scope of these Projects is more narrowly defined. In this respect, we would like to clarify that the scope of the projects includes the preparation of individual national/sectoral baseline studies and monitoring plans that go beyond the scope of individual Project activities. The preparation of these studies and plans involves a significant amount of time, conducting the assessments, and producing additional documents to meet all the conditions - representing an even higher transition cost associated with working with the GCF without considering the costs (in particular for GCF) related to the execution of the FAAT to conduct the additional studies and plans for each of the 9 countries that are part of this Facility. We would like to draw your attention to the significant challenges and delays involved in collecting the requested information, especially during the current pandemic situation which has also affected the collection of data, including those COVID-19 emergency projects, including one in a fragile and conflict affected situation.

We regret to note that our comments have not been reflected in the final version of ITAP's assessment and in the conditions ITAP recommends that they are met prior to the execution of the FAA.

# Introduction

The Cooling Facility is proposed as an innovative, multi-sector and multi-country programmatic financing mechanism with the goal of transforming the cooling sector. The program will increase energy efficiency, reduce or avoid GHG emissions , and accelerate access to cooling for economic growth and human capital development.

To achieve this, the facility will:

- **Support investments in efficient, affordable, and sustainable cooling:** This includes supporting investments in efficient, affordable, and sustainable cooling.
- **Catalog technology and business model innovations:** Cataloging technology and business model innovations that can be scaled up or replicated.
- **Build an enabling environment for sustained, long-term systemic change:** Building an enabling environment for sustained, long-term systemic change to ensure the sector is inclusive of women, youth, and marginalized groups in health, agriculture , as well as (iv) support the integration of sustainable cooling into climate-resilient development.

The present Gender Assessment and Action Plan provides an overview of issues affecting men and women related to the Facility's implementation in the nine countries: Bangladesh, Ethiopia, India, Indonesia, Kenya, Malawi, Nepal, Tanzania, Uganda, Somalia, Panama, and Sri Lanka. It details the strategic approach that will be adopted under the facility to address gender equality and inclusion across all sectors of the cooling value chain at different dimensions, for instance through employment in the cooling-related sectors; ensuring women's and men's livelihoods are not negatively impacted by policies; and supporting COVID-19 vaccination efforts. Planners should note that the present Gender Action Plan (GAP) is aligned with the GCF third policy objective to "contribute to reducing the gender gap of 20% in access to financial services, credit, land ownership, and other assets and exclusions through GCF climate investments that mainstream gender equality issues."

The Cooling Facility's gender activities will focus on strengthening women's roles as employers, employees, and entrepreneurs within the cooling sector. The work under the facility will tangibly assist relevant stakeholders (such as ministries, utilities and businesses) in designing and implementing policies, programs, projects, and renewable energy, agriculture, health etc., to support the designing and implementation of innovative solutions that can be scaled up or replicated.

The Gender Action Plan is designed to provide a framework for the facility to collect data and generate crucial knowledge of actions and design interventions needed to help close gender gaps in access to cooling. The plan will also serve as a guide to support the World Bank Group 2016-2023 Gender Strategy and its four pillars of action.

The broad experience and track-record of the ESMAP Gender and Energy Program - which is funding the Cooling Facility - combined with the lessons learned from implementation of the Strategy, combined with the ESMAP Efficient Coolin g Program (which is providing TA support to countries in the cooling sector) and the World Bank Group's 2016-2023 Gender Strategy and its four pillars of action. For example, ensuring key public services such as clinics or hospitals have access to cooling (in medical facilities and for etc), different geographies (both in terms of countries and in terms of urban versus rural contexts) and different sectors (such as health, agriculture, energy etc), and different geographies (both in terms of countries and in terms of urban versus rural contexts) and different sectors (such as health, agriculture, energy etc).

Appropriate gender entry points for the Facility's cooling-informed operations will be case studies on how to address gender gaps in access to cooling (in medical facilities and etc), different geographies (both in terms of countries and in terms of urban versus rural contexts) and different sectors (such as health, agriculture, energy etc), and different geographies (both in terms of countries and in terms of urban versus rural contexts) and different sectors (such as health, agriculture, energy etc).

The WBG Gender Strategy has 4 objectives: i) improving Human Endowments ii) Removing Constraints to Women's Economic Empowerment iii) Strengthening the Governance of Assets and iv) Enhancing Women's Voice and Agency and Engaging Men and Boys

### Gender Gaps That Matter

While more data and analysis are needed to better understand the gender report of access to sustainable cooling and energy efficiency, gender gaps are evident (SEforALL, 2019). Women and girls are often disproportionately impacted by limited access to sustainable cooling and energy. This is because they have fewer opportunities for entrepreneurship and enhanced livelihoods. There is very little evidence that shows women and girls benefit more from the use of cooling technologies than men.

This document will extrapolate approaches that are proved to work from other sectors in particular those that directly apply (after a gender assessment).

Gaps that may impact projects under the Facility are outlined briefly below:

- Jobs and Skills Development
- Entrepreneurship

Alignment with the SGP's Gender Strategy and achieving gender tag: The Bank implements systematically a mechanism to identify operations that aim to close gender gaps in human endowments, more and better jobs, ownership and control of assets, and income. To be considered for funding under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) as a GCF funding project to be "gender tagged", it must meet two criteria: (1) explicitly address one or more dimensions of the Gender Strategy; and (2) include a vertical logic with (a) an analysis of the specific gender issues, (b) actions that seek to address the issues; and (c) indicators that show the advancement towards closing the gender gap.

## 3. Gender Gap Assessment

### 3.1 Overarching Gender Gaps
When designing cooling projects and programs with a gender equality focus, there are many opportunities to consider issues around access to finance, entrepreneurship, employment and consumer engagement. These may include, for example, working toward government policies that support women's economic empowerment in the sector; increasing access through increasing access to climate-friendly cooling; or including sex-disaggregated information on the impacts of cooling technologies, or making sure that both men and women take part in related consultation processes.

Growing evidence points to significant differences in the needs and wants of women versus men. These differences are often not well understood by policymakers who need to know how they use energy on a daily basis, how much they are willing and able to adopt cooling technology and measures e.g. in agriculture sector, and how they respond to campaigns marketing new technologies. This is particularly true for developing countries where context-specific behaviors of men and women can be very different. For example, women may have lower literacy rates or receive less education than men; they may also have more limited access to information (e.g., through radio, television, newspapers, etc.), or participate in fewer decision-making processes such as village meetings or town meetings etc. This points to a need for communications strategies in these countries to look beyond the traditional focus on household heads and instead consider gender-specific consumption patterns, and so forth.

The agriculture sector in a number of developing countries employs a larger proportion of economically active women than is the case for men. Despite their critical role in ensuring food security, women (in particular rural) still face constraints from entering fully and in the same condition as men into this sector. Women are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming; have less access to productive resources, technologies (for harvesting and preservation), and markets. These factors inhibit their potential across the sector, and also limit their economic empowerment and decrease their health, resulting in significant gender gaps that trickle down to families and communities.


---
**Page 159**
---

Other gender gaps, such as in access to finance or affordability constraints (e.g., women have equal access to financial services in only 40% of the world’s countries), may make it relatively difficult for female-headed households or female business owners or farmers to adopt energy-efficient technologies. To achieve policy objectives, it is important to consider these dynamics in the design of relevant programs.

Man and women tend to have different metabolisms, and thus experience air temperature differently. A study by Bhattacharya et al. (2015) found that women were more sensitive than men to thermal comfort in offices in India; female workers were found to accept a wider range of temperatures than males, and require less mechanical cooling (Indrani, Oka, and Rajeev 2018). Research by Huang et al. (2017) has shown that women are more sensitive to heat on certain cognitive tests at higher temperatures than at lower ones, and that overexposed heat can lead to a decline in performance.

Research has demonstrated that men and women respond differently to information and marketing campaigns. Surveys across countries and different economic conditions show that men tend to be more energy-efficient consumers (Huang et al. 2017). Women are more sensitive relative to women, though women exhibit a more positive attitude and behavior toward saving energy than men in some contexts (Chen et al. 2018; Huang et al. 2017).

It is likely that there are opportunities to improve the outcomes of efficiency programs by understanding these differences, and through the targeting of approaches to achieve desired results.

An “energy clinic” conducted by the Energy Management Centre in Kanpur, India demonstrated that women can be key agents of change in utilizing more efficient appliances. Women organized demonstration classes on energy conservation in their own villages, and attracted other women from nearby villages to attend these classes (local administrators [Unnikrishnan 2018]. Some energy companies e.g., Tata Power Delhi Distribution Ltd. have also used women as “brand ambassadors” or “energy champions” or “brand ambassadors” to raise awareness about the benefits of legal connections, help facilitate new connections and promote energy efficiency in their communities (Shrivastava 2017; World Bank 2017).

Other opportunities to tackle gender equality issues include looking at how coping institutions can tackle gender gaps apparent in labor force participation rates and during women’s recruitment into the workforce. Women are more likely than men to be discouraged from entering an industry or adhering to certain roles, limited transport and insufficient workplace infrastructure may also prevent women from accessing jobs. In addition, a regressive organizational culture in male-dominated fields; gender pay gaps resulting from occupational segregation and unequal pay for equal work, and a lack of care services linked to working hours are all barriers that can be addressed through the use of ELSI (with support from the World Bank’s India Energy Efficiency Scale-up Program [IIESP] 1602849) and the ESMAP Gender Equality in Energy Initiative (GEIEI).

School-based education programs promoting energy efficiency can be an effective way to drive behavior change among households, as children can learn to integrate energy and sustainability into their daily lives. In addition, there are opportunities for increasing women in high schools to increase their interest in the science and engineering fields. An example is the Girls of Energy initiative conducted by the U.S. Department of Energy’s Office

1 The Global Gender Gap Report, published by the World Economic Forum, benchmarks 144 countries as part of its annual report on gender parity in areas such as education, health status, economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. It has been published annually since 2007.

6


---
**Page 160**

Of economic Impact and Diversity, whose activities include creating opportunities for more girls to explore the research and technology being developed at the department’s National Laboratories. The program also covers energy efficiency, among other topics, in an interactive e-learning course. The program is designed to be integrated into existing curricula focused on promoting women's participation in research and technological innovation.

An enabling environment is crucial to improve women's empowerment and reduce gender gaps. An enabling environment is critical to improve women’s empowerment and reduce gender gaps. Despite the fact that many countries are improving gender disparities, such as through laws and policies, women continue to be underrepresented in leadership positions. Women also remain concentrated into decent jobs in women are still overrepresented in low-productivity and informal jobs. A lot of the constraints faced by women are related to their lack of access to education, early marriage, labor segregation, time poverty, violence and gender roles. The Cooling Facility will support fostering an enabling environment for women’s empowerment.

A robust legal framework and regulation protecting women rights and their access to services are also key for the implementation of activities that aim to close gender gaps. The following section presents a summary of the findings from the World Bank’s 2021 report, Women, Business and the Laws (World Bank, 2021) assessment. This assessment is an annual global study on how laws and regulations affect women's access to economic opportunities. For instance, the COVID-19 has affected women’s social and economic capabilities, as well as their ability to participate in the labor market.

The importance of legal equality is not limited to disaster preparedness or mitigation of the effects, but it is associated with better development outcomes such as lower rates of vulnerable employment, higher female labor force participation, and greater gender equality. The gender activities will be developed with each Executing Entity to support local institutions having the tools and skills to achieve the proposed objectives. Activities will be in line with each country’s context and priorities. The activities will include a series of capacity-building assessments to identify the main barriers and constraints that inhibit gender equality. It is important for countries to have an enabling environment for women's empowerment effectively. A gender action plan per country is not proposed, but rather the integration of gender considerations into existing plans. Activities should be designed in such way as they are gender-disaggregated and, when relevant, that vaccine and health outreach campaigns also be targeted at women. The activities will be implemented in a participatory manner, considering all relevant stakeholders and include their concerns and suggestions. Finally, during the implementation of these activities, countries should monitor progress against established targets, track progress, make adjustments as needed and learn from the implementation of gender activities.

Linkages to climate change: gender inequality is one of the inhibitors for development, and the impacts of climate change perpetuate it. As previously mentioned, women have less economic, political, and legal power than men resulting in a more considerable inability to cope with the adverse effects of climate change. The linkages according to vulnerabilities such

as poverty, job quality, quality of housing, among others, make women more susceptible to climate change, causing them frequently to be displaced, stop working in productive activities, and spend more time on non-paid tasks. Women's empowerment is key to addressing climate change impacts and building resilience. It can also help women access new opportunities for productive uses such as in farming or cooking activities (such as fuel collection). Where pertinent, the project will support women’s access to income-generating livelihood programs (such as offering financial services that allow women to be more efficient, adopt better technology, and reduce food waste), provide sustainable cooling solutions for buildings that provide services to women, and improve women's access to water.

### 3.2 General Country-Specific Gender Gaps

A preliminary gender gap assessment was conducted across the countries to be supported by the Fund. The general gender gap identified is outlined below, along with the preliminary identification of potential gender entry points for intervention (at the project level), which will be explored during project design.

#### 3.2.1 Bangladesh

Bangladesh obtained a score of 0.726 in the 2020 WEF Global Gender Report Index, which puts the country in the 50th position. This suggests a substantial improvement in comparison to its 2014 score (0.589) and the global average (0.63). Bangladesh is ranked 7th out of 144 countries.

The Constitution of Bangladesh states that women and men have equal rights in all spheres of public life, including marriage, divorce, inheritance, and religious personal laws, which position women in an unequal situation and worse than men. The country has been investing and working on the issue of gender equality since independence (1972). In 1983, Bangladesh was among the first countries to adopt a National Women Development Policy led by the Ministry of Women and Children Affairs (created in 1972 and now known as the Ministry of Women and Children’s Machinery). The following are some of the laws and regulations for women in the country:

- **Legal framework:** Bangladesh has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law 2019 shows that Bangladesh ranks 84th out of 160 countries (score: 4.4 out of 10). It means the country is lower than the regional average achieved in South Asia (53rd) but higher than the global average for women’s rights.

- **Women's participation:** Women are not allowed to work in some industrial jobs in the same way as men (including mining, water, and factoring [labor]). Women are also prevented from working in some jobs that are considered hazardous. Women are also prevented from working in jobs that are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Bangladesh women do not have a law that ensures equal pay for work of equal value.

- **Maternity Rights:** Laws regarding maternity rights are broadly absent. The country lacks parental leave laws and does not have the option or right to enjoy a paid leave. Also, the government does not administer 100% of the maternity leave benefits (Labor Act No. 37).
