# Assets

The Muslim Personal Law (Shariat) Application Act, Art. 2 concedes women to have equal ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal administrative authority over assets during marriage. However, the same law states that widowed men do not have equal rights to real estate from their parents, and widowed women also do not have the same rights to inherit their spouses' assets as men.

## Access to credit: Bangladesh does not have a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit based on gender.

### Marriage:
Women cannot obtain a judgment of divorce in the same way as men and also women do not have the same rights to remarry as men.

### Income:
According to the UNDP's estimates, the female Gross National Income (GNI) per capita is $1,053 (2011 PPP $), which corresponds to 42% of the male GNI per capita ($2,701 in 2011 PPP $).

Gender gaps associated with the current project in Bangladesh

### Cooling-related activities or sectors targeted: efficient use of energy and resources in buildings and appliances

#### Preliminary overview of relevant gender gaps:
- **Women are underrepresented in the energy sector**: A recent study by the World Bank (2018) found that out of 3,765 total board members in public sector organizations, only 16 out of 276 (6%) were female. Bangladesh Power Development Board has the highest number of women board members at 49% (10/21), while the Electricity Division has 8% (3) and 217 out of 2,453 staff (9%). Data on the energy efficiency sector are not readily available.
- **Women in rural areas do not have adequate information**: The gender power relations in the household can influence decision-making behavior, change, or adoption of new technologies such as solar home systems and energy-efficient appliances.
- **The role of women is an important role to foster women's economic empowerment**: It may thus be important to partner with national and local nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) that have experience in implementing paid livelihood programs. Activities that aim to train on use of labor and energy-saving appliances, as well as other finance and business management, credit, market access, marketing strategies.
- **Women face constraints in decision-making at home**: Women are less likely than men to be the primary decision-makers for household use and buildings and appliances. However, women are less likely to be legal owners or occupiers of their homes, and housing is often seen as a male domain. This limits agency in terms of decision making and also ability to gain access to finance due to collateral requirements.

#### Suggested gender entry points and activities include:
(i) develop (or revise/enhance) gender assessment;
(ii) carry out consultations, workshops, gender assessment's presentation, and validation;
(iii) develop a gender action plan that includes cooling and gender; (iv) capacity building/awareness about gender equality; (v) develop gender action plan that includes responsibilities, activities and budgets; (vi) develop and implement communication/outreach campaign.

### 3.2.2 El Salvador

El Salvador obtained a score of 0.706 in the 2020 WEF Global Gender Report Index, which puts the country in the 80th position. This suggests a substantial improvement in comparison to the 2014 score (0.593) and the 2018 score (0.658). The country has the sixth lowest score in its region, Latin America and the Caribbean.

The Constitution of El Salvador states that women and men have equal rights at all levels. The country has a National Policy for Women, which includes gender equality, including designing and implementing the National Policy for Women led by The Salvadoran Institute for Women (ISJ). The law also provides for the protection of women's rights (right to machinery). The following are some of the laws and rules applicable for women in the country.


---
**Page 165**
---

## Legal framework: El Salvador has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights.

The publication Women Business and the Law 2021 (World Bank, 2021) indicates that El Salvador scores 88.6 out of 100. It means the country is in the top third of countries with respect to gender equality.

### Employment and payment: The country does not have a law that restricts women to work in the same fields as men (and can work at night in the same way as men). Also, the country has laws against sexual harassment in employment (Código Penal, Art. 165; Ley General de Prevención de Háogos en Lugares de Trabajo, Art. 3) and sexual harassment at work is consider a crime under the penal code. The country does not have specific laws that prohibit discrimination in employment based on gender (Código del Trabajo, Art. 301(2); Código Penal, Art. 248). Women can be employed in all sectors of the economy including mining and quarrying; manufacturing; water and factoring; construction; agriculture, energy, transportation, and other services.

In regards to pay, Salvadoran women do not have a law that mandates equal remuneration for work of equal value.

### Maternity Rights: Mothers in El Salvador have 112 days of paid time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Código del Trabajo, Art. 309). The country lacks parental leave laws. The country's social security administration administers 100% of the maternity leave benefits (Ley del Seguro Social, Arts. 1 y 12) it is also prohibits the dismissal of pregnant worker (Código del Trabajo, Art. 113).

### Assets and Family: Women in El Salvador have equal legal rights to immovable property and grants them equal administrative authority over assets during marriage. Código Civil, Arts. 980 y 988(1) states that sons and daughters have the same rights as regards inheritance. Código Civil, Art. 275(3) states that women have the same rights to inherit their spouses assets as men.

### Access to credit and entrepreneurship: El Salvador does not have a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit based on gender. Women also have no restrictions when registering a new business, open a bank account or signing a contract.

### Marriage: The Salvador does not have a legal provision that requires a married woman to obey her husband (Código de Familia, Art. 36(1)); women can be called the head of household and can initiate divorce proceedings in cases of domestic violence (Ley Contra la Violencia Intrafamiliar; Ley Especial Integral para una Vida Libre de Violencia, Art. 20). Women and men can obtain a judgment of divorce in the same way as men but they do not have the same rights to remarry as men (Código de Familia, Arts. 17, 23(5) y 115(1)).

### Income: According to the UNDP's estimates, the female Gross National Income (GNI) per capita in El Salvador amounts to $4,671 in 2017 PPP $, which corresponds to 62% of the male GNI per capita (10,501 2017 PPP $).

# Labor Markets

Labor participation in El Salvador is much lower for women than men; women represent only about 40% of the labor force. The WEF (2017) indicates that women's rate in the labor force participation is 50%, while it is 83%. Women are less represented as professionals and leaders, with a female representation of 67.6% compared to men at 94.5%. Women are overrepresented in sectors such as services (commerce, hotels, restaurants — 45.5% or women vs. 20.1% for men), agriculture (38.5% vs. 10%), and domestic/care work (10.9% vs 0.8% of men) and education (4.3% vs. 1.0% of men) (Table [7]).

Women tend to be included more often in temporary jobs as only 37.3% of them are considered full-time workers, a number much lower than the 45.1% registered for men.

## Agency

Only 31% members of Salvadoran's parliament are women. The share of women in ministerial positions is slightly higher (33%).

### Gender Based Violence

According to the United Nations Development Report, 14.3% of women have experienced physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner violence. Other sources suggest that 24.2% of women have suffered of physical partner violence at some point of their lives and 50% of Salvadoran women have been victims of domestic violence.

El Salvador has one of the highest female homicide rates in region, per year, 3.31 per 100,000 die by gender-based violence.

### Gender gaps associated with the current project in El Salvador

#### Cooling-related activities or sectors targeted: health-oriented projects

**Preliminary overview of relevant gender gaps:**

- **Women in El Salvador face some constraints when accessing maternal health services:** Women who have given birth and after giving birth, women do not visit health professionals. Complications and risks are not being identified on time resulting in the reduction of life expectancy at birth.
- **The risk of infection is higher for pregnant women than men:** 40% of women who are pregnant are likely to be infected with SARS-CoV-2. Most of women who have been infected by COVID-19, they have not received any treatment and most of them can infect their newborns.
- **Health authorities recommend that pregnant women to receive the vaccine against COVID-19:** Women live longer than men; women also represent a larger proportion of the population in the age group 65 years old or more. Women over the age of 65 add up to 4.64%, only account for 3.37%.


---
**Page 168**
---

* Women face high levels of gender-based violence: On average, 26.3% of women report physical and/or sexual abuse by partners at some point in their lives. Victims are usually unemployed, who live in urban areas and also mothers of multiple children.
    * The country has a low rate of reporting cases to the police (10%). Most of the health facilities that are dedicated to examining and serving victims of gender-based violence are not adequate or insufficient. Women who work in domestic task face more often sexual harassment and abuse by their employers at home.
    * When entering an emergency service at clinics or hospitals, victims of gender-based violence are often subjected to further trauma and humiliation.

* Reverse gender gap is found in COVID-19 cases in the country: By January 2020, 58% of all confirmed COVID-19 cases were women. In February 2020, 63% of the cases correspond to male patients. 49% of the cases are being detected in women.

Suggested gender entry points and activities include: (i) developing a gender assessment (identify gaps and opportunities); (ii) ensuring that all data collected and shared are disaggregated data and information about better access to vaccines; (iii) stakeholder consultations/presentation; (iv) develop and implement a communications/outreach campaign.

### 3.2.3 Kenya

Kenya obtained a score of 0.671 in the 2020 WEP Global Gender Report Index, which puts the country in the 109th position. This suggests a minor improvement in comparison to the 2008 edition (score: 0.543) and the 2018 edition of the report (0.671) and ranked 73rd. The country ranks 20th among 34 countries in its region, Sub-Saharan Africa.

The Constitution of Kenya states that women and men have the right to equal treatment, including the right to equal opportunities in political, economic, cultural and social spheres. The country has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law 2021 (World Bank Group) shows that Kenya ranks 58th out of 149 countries. This means the country is higher than the regional average achieved in Sub-Saharan Africa (71%).

* Employment and payment: The country does not have a law that restricts women to work only in certain jobs or places, but it has laws that protect women against discrimination. Also, women at workplaces are protected by a legislation that prevents sexual harassment and assault at work (Kenya Constitution Act 2010, Article 43; Employment Act, 2007, Sec. 6) and sexual harassment at work is considered a crime (Sexual Offences Act, 2006, Sec. 2). Women are also protected against discrimination based on gender (Employment Act, Art. 5(3)(a)). Women are able to work in all industrial jobs in the same way as men (including mining, water and farming, construction, agriculture, energy and transport) except for those that are considered hazardous or dangerous jobs that are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Kenyan women are protected by a law that mandates equal remuneration for equal value (Employment Act, Art. 5(3)).

* Many countries were added between the two vintages; thus, the drop in the ranking does not necessarily signals the performance of the country.

# Maternity Rights: Mothers in Kenya have 90 days of paid time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Employment Act, Sec. 29(1)). The country lacks parental leave, but fathers enjoy a 14-day paid leave after labor (Employment Act, Art. 28(5)). Women are entitled to maternity benefits for up to six months of their absence from work (Employment Act, Art. 29). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Employment Act, Art. 29).

# Assets: The National Property Act, Sec. 4(a) and 12 respectively concede women her half share in ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal administrative authority over assets during marriage. Law of Succession Act, Art. 36(5) states that a woman has the same right as a man to inherit movable or immovable properties; but widowed women do not have the same rights to inherit their spouses assets as men (Law of Succession Act, Art. 41).

# Access to credit and entrepreneurship: Kenya does not have a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit based on gender. Women also have no restrictions open to them when it comes to starting or running businesses (Employment Act, Art. 30). However, women experience barriers to register a business as they cannot do it in the same way as men under the Companies and Allied Matters Act, 2015; and Women's Protection Against Domestic Violence Act, 2015); and women can obtain a judgment of divorce in the same way as men, but they do not have the same rights to remarry as men.

# Income: According to the UNDP’s estimates, the female Gross National Income (GNI) per capita in Kenya amounts to 2.619 in 2011 PPP $, which corresponds to 75% of the male GNI per capita (3.490 2011 PPP $).

## Financial inclusion.

In Kenya present higher levels of financial inclusion than women when comparing the ownership of an account at a financial institution (47 versus 65%), a mobile money account (39 versus 52%) and a savings account (30 versus 48%). In contrast, the share of women aged 15 or above in the country with either an account at financial institutions, a mobile money account or a savings account is lower than that for women in developing countries (58.3%), and more than double the share in South-Saharan Africa (36%).

## Education.

Women in Kenya are more likely to be enrolled in primary education than men (81% of girls versus 74% of boys). However, fewer women complete secondary or tertiary education. Consequently, men report a higher average number of schooling years (12 versus 6). In addition, women amount to less than one-third of STEM graduates (31%) [Table 14].

## Health.

The maternal mortality rate in Kenya is 508 deaths per 100,000 births, one of the highest in the world, ranking 164th among 182 countries included in the Human Development Report. This figure is more than twice the average in developing countries (231) but close with its region. The life expectancy at birth for women in Kenya is 59 years compared to men who live longer by as much as 4 years higher than the same figure for males (68.7 versus 64.0 years). [Table 15]

Gender gaps associated with the current project in Kenya:

Cooling systems: The project aims to provide energy efficient and sustainable cooling appliances to both off-grid and on-grid consumers, targeting household end-users, small businesses and manufacturing supply chains. Expansion of credit facility for providers of energy efficient cooling systems.

Preliminary overview of relevant gender gaps:
- Female-headed households are less likely to have access to energy, for all grid and off-grid technologies (83% vs 54%). Female-headed households are more deprived from electricity than their male counterparts: 96.3% and 50.4% of female headed households in urban areas were without electricity compared with 72.1% and 40.6% for male-headed households, respectively.
- Financing options may be close to the access gap for rural female-headed households (83%) as compared to their male counterparts (96%). Financial decision makers are generally less willing (or able) to pay for off-grid solar solutions up front but were more willing to purchase a solar solution if the repayment period was over a longer period of time.
- Women’s income is limited. For instance, when offered an off-grid solar home system on credit at 10% interest rate and with no down payment, women paid full price up front, compared to 31% of male-headed households. However, when a premium was added for the same product, only 24% of female headed households (64.7%) versus 78.6%) borrowed money (61.7% versus 67.4%). Moreover, women tend to rely more on informal sources of credit: 13.5% of adult women borrowed from a formal financial institution compared with 20.9% for men; and vice versa.
- Meanwhile, 24.2% of adult women borrowed through a savings club compared to 14.6% of male-headed households.

Suggested entry points and activities include: (i) develop (or review/reassess) gender assessment; (ii) carry out consultations, workshops, gender assessment’s presentations; (iii) engage in policy dialogues to raise awareness about the linkages between cooling and gender; (iv) capacity building/awareness about gender equality; (v) develop agenda action plan that includes responsible authorities and budgets; (vi) impart informational sessions for technical firms; (vii) assessments on financial barriers female

### Malawi

Malawi obtained a score of **0.604** in the 2020 WEF Global Gender Gap Report Index, which puts the country in the **117th position**. This is the same score the country received in the 2008 edition of the report. In the 2006 edition of the report, Malawi ranked **91st** in the 2006 vintage. The country ranks **23rd** in its region, South-Saharan Africa.

The Constitution of Malawi states that women have the right to fully participate in all spheres of Malawian society and that their rights are protected by law. Women's rights and freedoms are protected by the implementation of the principles of nondiscrimination. The country has been investing in gender equality through the establishment of a Ministry for Gender Equality, and the gender equality division of the Ministry of Gender, Children, Disability and Social Welfare (referred to as the Gender Division hereinafter). The following are some of the laws and rules applicable for women in the country.

#### Legal framework

Malawi has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law 2021 (World Bank, 2021) indicates that Malawi scores **75.3 out of 100**. Thus, the country is higher than the regional average achieved in Sub-Saharan Africa (11%).

- **Employment**: The Employment Act prohibits a law that restricts women to work in the same fields as men and can work at night in the same way as men. Also, women are not prohibited by a law that provides for sexual or physical harassment in employment and that considers harassment at work is treated as crime (Gender Equality Act 2013, Sects. 6(2) & 7(2)). A Malawian law prohibits discrimination on the basis of gender in employment and prohibits women from being able to work in all industrial jobs in the same way as men (including mining, water and factories). Women are also not prohibited by a law that provides for equal pay and is not prevented from working in jobs that are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Malawi has a law that mandates equal remuneration for equal value (Employment Act, Sect. 6).

- **Maternity Rights**: Mothers in Malawi have 56 days of paid time during a period between the birth and return to work after childbirth. Women are entitled to parental leave, and fathers cannot enjoy a paid leave after labor. The government does not enforce this law (Employment Act, Sects. 43 & 47). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Employment Act, Sect. 49)

- **Assets**: The Married Women's Property Act, Sects. 1, 2 and 5 respectively concede women to have equal ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal rights in movable property. The Family Law (Marriage, Divorce, Custody, Inheritance and Protection) Act, Sect. 17 states that sons and daughters have equal rights of inheritance. Women are also entitled to the same rights as men to inherit their spouses' assets as men.

- **Access to credit and entrepreneurship**: Malawi does not have a law that prohibits discrimination on the basis of gender in access to credit. Women's organizations open a bank account or signing a contract (Constitution of Malawi, Art. 24(1)(a)(i)).

### 3.2.5 North Macedonia
North Macedonia obtained a score of 0.711 in the 2020 WEF Global Gender Report Index, which puts the country in the 70th position. This suggests a small improvement in comparison to the 2006 edition of the report, where the country obtained a score of 0.698 and ranked 74th. The country is still below the regional average for Europe and Central Asia.

The Law on Equal Opportunities for Women and Men establishes that in North Macedonia women and men have equal rights at all levels. The country has been investing and working in gender equality issues and regulations, including designing and implementing the National Strategy for Gender Equality (2016-2030) and the National Action Plan for Gender Equality (2017-2021). The Law on Equal Opportunities for Women and Men also establishes that women have equal rights to access education and training in all areas, including higher education. The Law on Equal Opportunities for Women and Men also establishes that women have equal rights to access employment and the right to work in any field of activity (Article 32).

### Legal framework: North Macedonia has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights.
- Publication Business, Women and the Law (2018) shows that the country achieved a score of 75.6 on the Gender Equality Index (GEI), which means the country is higher than the regional average achieved in Europe and Central Asia (83.4).

### Employment and payment: The country does not have a law that restricts women to work in the same fields as men (and can work at night in the same way as men). Also, women at workplaces are protected by a legislation that prevents sexual harassment and discrimination against women. Women are also protected under the Law on Protection Against Harassment at the Workplace, Arts. 1 and 7; Law on Equal Opportunities for Women and Men, Art. 38; Law on Prevention of Employment Discrimination, Art. 41; Law on Protection Against Harassment at the Workplace, Art. 36; Labor Relations Act, Art. 59; and Law on Equal Opportunities for Women and Men, Art. 20. The country also has laws that prohibit discrimination based on gender (Labor Relations Act, Arts. 6 and 7). However, women are not able to work in all jobs as men do because of the nature of some jobs. For example, bars and factories are not allowed (Labor Relations Act, Arts. 160 and 131) while water, agriculture, energy, transportation, and others are allowed). Women are not prevented from working in jobs that are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, N.

# Maternity Rights: Mothers in North Macedonia have 270 days of paid time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Labor Relations Act, Art. 165). The country lacks specific legislation on parental leave for fathers (Labor Relations Act, Art. 146; General Collective Bargaining Agreement for the Private Sector, Art. 38). Maternity leave is not covered by social security benefits (Law on maternity leave benefits [Law on Health Insurance, Arts. 14 and 17]). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Labor Relations Act, Art. 101)

## Assets: Constitution of the Republic of North Macedonia, Art. 5 and Law on Ownership Rights, Art. 26, state that men and women have equal ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal administrative authority over movable property. The law also states in Arts. 3, 9, 10, 14, 17, 18, 19, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, 30, and 31 that sons and daughters have equal rights to inherit assets from their parents. Widowed women have the same rights to inherit their spouses' assets as men.

## Access to credit: The Constitution of North Macedonia guarantees its citizens in access to credit based on gender equality (Constitution Art. 11). Women also have no restrictions open a bank account, signing a contract, and register a business in the same way as men.

## Marriage: The law does not require that requires a married woman to obey her husband; women can be called the head of household in the same way as men. The country has specific legislation for addressing domestic violence (Law on Protection against Domestic Violence, Art. 122(2)); and women can obtain a judgment of divorce and have the same rights to remarry as men.

Close to 40% of parliament members in North Macedonia are women. However, the share of women in leadership positions is still low.

### Gender Based Violence
According to the United Nations Development Report, 10% of women in North Macedonia aged 15 or older experience gender-based violence by their partners.

### Gender gaps associated with the current project in North Macedonia
- **Cooling activities or sector targets:** energy efficiency investments in public buildings (central government and municipal schools, health care facilities, universities, etc.) to lower and avoid cooling costs. The project aims at the identification and implementation of a sustainable financing mechanism.
- **Preliminary overview of relevant gender gaps**
  - **Violence against women:** Gender-based violence represents a priority public health issue: the country has established dedicated facilities administered by the Ministry of Health that address and provide services to victims of gender-based violence, including those with physical and mental injuries and traumas. Also, evidence shows that women who experience violence are more likely to be infected with HIV and to acquire HIV compared to women who do not experience this type of violence.
  - **Vocational education:** Women represent only 20% of students in North Macedonia's secondary schools. From all female students, only 1% choose mechanical engineering as their field of study; from male students, the percentage is 10%. Of male students, those who choose mechanical engineering and 20% choose electrical engineering.
  - **Women are underrepresented in STEM careers:** Overall, 10% of all female students graduate with a degree in science, technology, engineering or mathematics. Women in North Macedonia account to only 17.5% of all researchers in science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) fields.

### The labor market is also clustered by sex
- In North Macedonia, women represent less than 20% of the total number of workers compared to 28% of male workers. Moreover, the male labor force is bigger due to higher participation rates in the construction sector, which accounts for 91% of the employees in electricity; gas; steam and air conditioning supply.

### Panama

Panama obtained a score of **0.730** on the 2009 WEF Global Gender Gap Index, which puts the country in the **46th position**. This suggests a substantial improvement in comparison to the 2008 edition where it was ranked at **51st place**, and the 2007 edition where it was ranked at **73rd**. The country ranks **12th among 25 countries in its region, Latin America and the Caribbean**.

The Constitution of Panama establishes that Panamanian women and men have equal rights as to laws and regulations, including the creation of the National Women's Institute (created in 2008 and acting as the women's rights machinery). The following are some of the laws and rules applicable for women in the country:

#### Legal framework: Panama has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law 2013 shows that the number of laws and regulations protecting women is lower than the regional average achieved in Latin America and the Caribbean (85.1).

- **Employment and payment:** The country does not have a law that restricts women to work only in certain jobs or industries, nor does it have a law that prohibits women from employment based on gender (Ley Num. 82, Arts. 4(20) y 45). Women are able to work in all sectors of the economy (construction, factories, water, agriculture, energy, transportation and others). Women are not prevented from working in jobs that are considered hazardous (Código de Trabajo, Art. 104), nor is there a law that prohibits women from holding public office or that mandates equal remuneration for equal work.

- **Maternity Rights:** Mothers in Panama have 96 days of paid time during a period between the 35th and 37th weeks of pregnancy (Código de Trabajo, Art. 107). The country lacks parental leave, but fathers can enjoy a paid leave after labor (3 days) (Ley Num. 27, Arts. 4(20) y 45), and the law does not provide for paternity leave (Código de Trabajo, Art. 107). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Código de Trabajo).

- **Assets:** Código de Familia, Arts. 103 y 104 concrates women to have equal ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal administrative authority over assets. Women are entitled to receive a share in the inheritance from their parents and daughters have equal rights to inherit assets from their parents; widowed women have the same right to inherit their spouse's assets as men.

- **Access to credit and financial services:** Panama has a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit based on gender, but women do not have restrictions open a bank account, signing a contract, and register a business in the same way as men.

- **Marriage:** Panama does not have a legal provision that requires a married woman to obey her husband; women can be called the head of household in the same way as men. The country has specific legislation for addressing domestic violence (Ley Num.

### 3.2.7 Sao Tome and Principe

The Human Development Report 2020 and the Gender Development Index (GDI) define a ratio of the female to male Human Development Index (HDI). The GDI shows gender inequalities in education, health, and economic opportunities. In 2019, the HDI and GDI for Sao Tome e Principe was 0.906, identifying gender gaps against women.

The Constitution of Sao Tome and Principe guarantees equal rights and obligations to men and women. The country has laws and regulations that address gender equality issues and regulations, including the design and implementation of the National Strategy for Gender Equality (2018-2030). The government also established the National Council on Gender Equity (created in 2009 and acting as the women's rights machinery). The following are some of the laws that protect women against discrimination.

#### Legal Framework: Sao Tome and Principe has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law (World Bank, 2021) ranks Sao Tome and Principe scores 88.3 out of 100. It means the country is higher than the regional average achieved in Sub-Saharan Africa (71).

- **Employment and pay equality:** The law has a provision that allows women to get a job in the same conditions as men (Law No. 27, Art. 28). Also, laws on employment are protected by a legislation that prevents sexual harassment in employment and sexual harassment is prohibited under the law (Law No. 16/2004, Art. 35; Law No. 16/2022, Art. 16(2)). A Samantha law prohibits discrimination in employment based on gender (Law No. 18/2007). The law also provides that women have the right to work in jobs the same as men (including mining, construction, factories, water, agriculture, and other activities). Women are not allowed to work in jobs considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Samantha women workers receive equal pay for equal work.

- **Maternity Rights:** Mothers in Sao Tome and Principe have 60 days of paid time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Labor Law Art. 145(1)). The country lacks parental leave policies. However, the Ministry of Labor and Social Security administers 100% of the maternity leave benefits (Presidential Decree No. 3/2004, Art.


---
**Page 183**
---

### Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal Annex I | Page 30 of 54

#### Decree Law No. 20/2014 (Art. 96(1)(a)), Law No. 6/2018, Art. 269). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Law No. 6/2018, Art. 262).

- **Assets**: Law Nos. 277, Arts. 35 & 36, and 382) concedes women to have equal ownership rights to immovable property and grants spouses equal administrative authority over movable property.
- **Access to credit and entrepreneurship**: Sao Tome and Principe does not have a law that requires a married woman's consent for her husband to obtain credit. However, women do not have restrictions on opening a bank account, signing contracts, or registering as business in the same way as men.

#### Marriage: Sao Tome and Principe does not have a legal provision that requires a man to take his wife's surname upon marriage; women can be called the head of household in the same way as men. The country has specific legislation for addressing domestic violence and sexual harassment, including provisions on the protection order, statement of divorce and have the same rights to remarry (Law 19/2018, Art. 25) as men.

#### Income: Sao Tome & Principe has low Gross National Income (GNI) per capita and it is estimated for US$46 for women and US$438 for men. Also, 37.9% of households are headed by women and 62.1% by men. On average, the monthly household expenditure is 10% lower in female-headed households than male-headed households.

#### Labor Markets: Labor participation in Sao Tome & Principe is much lower for women than men; women's rate in the labor force participation is 43% while men's is 76%. Also, only 44.5% of women who belong to the labor force are considered as economically active compared with 80.9% of men belonging to the labor force. Only 12.5% of women who belong to the labor force are considered as vulnerable employment. Only 3% of women work in the industry sector (vs. 25% of men) and women are overrepresented in agriculture, fishing, and forestry.

#### Education: Women in Sao Tome e Principe are about the same to be enrolled in primary education compared with men; however, girls are slightly more likely to be enrolled in secondary education levels than men (68.7% vs. 61.9%).

#### Health: The maternal mortality ratio in Sao Tome & Principe is 130 per 100,000 live births. This figure is higher compared with the average for Sub-Saharan Africa and the least developed countries of Sub-Saharan Africa (547 region). Also, it is more than 10 times the maternal mortality ratio in OECD (14) or very high human development countries (10) according to the UNDP Human Development Index. The life expectancy at birth in Sao Tome & Principe is a little over four years higher than the same figures for males (72.8 versus 68 years).

#### Agency: Only 18% members of Sao Tome e Principe's parliament are women. The share of women among ministers and senior civil servants is 30%.

#### Gender-Based Violence: About 1 in 3 women have experienced some form of GBV at the hands of their husbands or partners.

### 3.2.8 Somalia

Data and information regarding gender gaps in Somalia are limited. This situation represents a constraint to tackle issues that inhibit gender equality in the country. In the Gender Inequality Index (GII), which measures gender inequality across three dimensions (denoting complete inequality), placing Somalia at the fourth highest in gender inequality globally, with women in the country being affected more by conflict and violence, inhibiting their potential to exit poverty.

The Constitution of Somalia provides that all citizens have equal rights and obligations regardless of sex, and that the state must not discriminate against any person on the basis of gender. The Ministry of Women Affairs (MoWA) is responsible for women's affairs in government, including the design and implementation of the National Gender Policy of Somalia led by the Ministry of Women Affairs. The MoWA also works closely with other government bodies (the women's rights machinery). The following are some of the laws and rules applicable for women in the country.

### Legal framework: Somalia has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and the Law 2019 shows that while there is progress on gender equality in Somalia, it means the country is lower than the average regional achieved in Sub-Saharan Africa (71).

- **Employment and payment**: The country has a law that does not restrict women to work only in certain jobs. However, women are not protected by legislation that prevents sexual harassment in employment. A Somali woman can be employed as a driver or in any other job, except for those prohibited under the Labor Code (Arts. 3 and 70) but women are not able to work in all industrial jobs in the same way as men (including mining, construction, transport, water, agriculture, energy, transportation). The Somali Labor Code (Art. 68) prohibits women from working in jobs that are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Somalian women have equal rights with men and they can be paid equally for work done under the same conditions (Labor Code, Art. 70).

- **Maternity Rights**: Mothers in Somalia have 95 days off past time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Labor Code, Art. 81(2)). The country lacks parental leave, and fathers cannot enjoy a paid leave after labor. The government does not adopt any policy to encourage men's participation in childcare or domestic work (Art. 81(2)). It is also prohibited the dismissal of pregnant worker (Labor Code, Art. 91)

### Restrictions on Women's Rights

- **Access to credit and entrepreneurship:** Somalia does not have a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit or entrepreneurship. Women do not have the restrictions open a bank account, signing a contract, and register a business in the same way as men.
  
- **Marriage:** Somalia does not have a legal provision that requires a married woman to obey her husband; however, women cannot be called the head of household in the same way as men. There is no specific legislation for addressing domestic violence; and women cannot obtain a judgment of divorce and do not have the same rights to remarry.

### Income

- Somalia has one of the lowest Gross National Income (GNI) per capita in the world, and it is estimated at US$400, however there is not recent data that shows the differences between men and women. The World Bank estimates that 52% of male-headed households are affected by poverty compared to 69% of female-headed households; a poor number slightly higher than the 54% of male-headed households.

### Labor Markets

- Women's labor participation in Somalia is much lower for women than men: the Gender Inequality Index (2012) indicates that women's rate in the labor force participation is 19.1%, while men are at 73%. The share of women employed as a self-employed, a higher number compared to men (70.2%). Also, the share of females employed in agriculture was 91.9% when compared to 84.6% of men. Somalia is predominantly a pastoralist society where women are responsible for managing livestock and the property of men; women, on the other hand, manage the sale and exchange of livestock products.

### Education

- Women in Somalia are less likely to be enrolled in any education level than men: For every 10 boys enrolled in primary school there are only 7 girls and for every 10 young men enrolled in secondary school there are only 6 women. The UNDP Human Development Report shows parity show partly in Somalia, the gender disparity in rural Somalia is higher than urban areas.

### Health

- The maternal mortality ratio number in Somalia is 732 per 100,000 live births, one of the highest in the world; this number is above the average in developing countries (231) and above the Sub-Saharan Africa (547) region. Moreover, the figure is more than 52 times the maternal mortality ratio for developed regions (14 per 100,000 live births) according to the UNDP Human Development Report. In addition, female life expectancy at birth in Somalia is 3 years higher than the same figure for males (58 versus 55 years) (Table [31]).

### Sri Lanka

Sri Lanka obtained a score of 0.658 in the 2020 WEF Global Gender Gap Index, which puts the country in the 167th position. This suggests a substantial drop compared to the 2006 edition of the report, where the country obtained a score of 0.72 and ranked 139th. The country has made significant progress since then.

The Constitution of Sri Lanka states that all persons are equal before the law and are entitled to the equal protection of the law and no citizen shall be discriminated against on the grounds of race, caste, creed or sex. The country has been investing and working in gender equality issues and regulations, including the establishment of a National Commission for Women (NCW) in 1987; the creation of a National Plan of Action for Women led by the Ministry of Women and Child Affairs (created initially in 1979 and consolidated in 2015 and acting as the women’s rights machinery). The following are some of the laws and rules applicable for women in the country.

Legal framework: Sri Lanka has laws and regulations that protect women against discrimination and violence and recognize their rights. The publication Women, Business and

* **Employment and payment:** The country has a law that does not restrict women to work in in the same fields as men. Women at workplaces are protected by a legislation prohibiting sexual harassment. Sexual harassment includes unwanted touching, verbal or visual harassment at work is considered a crime (Penal Code Sect., 345). Sri Lanka lacks a law requiring equal pay for equal work. Women who have been pregnant and unable to work in some industrial jobs in the same way as men (including mining (Miners and Mineral Act No. 33, Sec. 50), Employment of Females in Mines Ordinance No. 13, Sec. 2) are not allowed; however, women can work in other industries where they are considered hazardous. In regards of payment, Sri Lankan women are not protected by a law requiring equal pay for equal work.

* **Maternity Rights:** Mothers in Sri Lanka have Nil days of paid time during a period between pregnancy and after labor (Shop and Office Employees Act. Sec. 18B; Shop and Office Employees (Regulation of Employment and Remuneration) (Amendment) Act, No. 14 of 2018). The country lacks parental leave, and fathers cannot enjoy a paid leave for the care of their children. Women are not entitled to any maternity leave benefits (Shop and Office Employees Act., Sec. 18B; Shop and Office Employees (Regulation of Employment and Remuneration) (Amendment) Act, No. 14 of 2018)

* **Assets:** The Married Women's Property Ordinance, sec. 5 confers to women have equal rights with men in the acquisition, enjoyment, management, and disposition over assets during marriage. The Matrimonial Rights and Inheritance Ordinance (No. 36 of 1970) provides that married women are entitled to inherit assets from their parents; and widowed women have the same rights to inherit their children's property.

* **Access to credit and entrepreneurship:** Sri Lanka does not have a law that prohibits discrimination in access to credit based-on gender, but women do not have restrictions open a bank account, signing a contract, and register a business the same way as men.

* **Marriage:** Sri Lanka does not have a legal provision that requires a married woman to obey her husband. Women can marry at any age; however, they cannot marry earlier than men. The country has a specific legislation for addressing domestic violence (Prevention of Domestic Violence Act., No. 34 of 2001) and women can obtain a judgment of divorce and have the same rights to remarry as men.

Income: According to the UNDP's estimates, the female Gross National Income (GNI) per capita in Sri Lanka was $586 in 2011 PPP $, which corresponds to 40% of the male GNI per capita at $1,452 in 2011 PPP $.

**Preliminary overview of relevant gender gaps**

- Despite significant progress in achieving gender equity in health and education, brought about through giving women their right to vote, free education, and access to public services, there is still a lack of women's engagement in labor, their economic empowerment, and their involvement in government decision-making.
- Women are accessing fewer basic services than men. While there is a high level of consciousness among women regarding their rights, there remains the interaction of a vast majority of women in community decision making and in commerce.
- For example, labor force participation rates are low; they are paid less than men, a difference not explained by their productive capacity and holds true for all sectors except agriculture.

# Potential Gender Entry Points by Theme

The following is a list of examples of possible interventions that could be proposed, designed, implemented or monitored to address gender gaps found in the countries to be included in individual gender action plans as relevant.

## A. Foster Women's Economic Empowerment

- Implement employment initiatives for women in the sector to provide labor opportunities and reduce unemployment.
- Create awareness campaigns on women's economic empowerment and perceptions on gender employment issues and opportunities which could engage with employers.
- Create a database of women-owned enterprises and promote their policies, hiring women for consumer or public-facing marketing or advocacy services.
- Incentivize data collection on male versus female participation in firm ownership and leadership positions.
- Explore ways to best incorporate the needs of women and youth in project activities as a way to increase their economic empowerment.
- Increased representation of women in the engineering/technical professions with a focus on renewable energy technologies.
- Track data on female staff hired under service contracts for technical and design work undertaken by the Fund.
- Provide targeted training programs for women and increasing awareness about employment opportunities under the project investments.
- Create awareness campaigns to promote women's participation in the cooling appliance sector.
- Provide opportunities in most productive sectors to reduce the incidence of GBV.

## B. Increase Women's Access to services

- Design and implement targeted approaches to reach female-headed households or families with limited access to energy, particularly those with multiple energy access needs.
- Offer financial products and services that address women's needs. Approaches might include microfinance institutions (MFIs) providing credit for small-scale enterprises; loan funds, and flexible repayment plans. Use of more concessional forms of financing or more intensive technical assistance to enhance gender inclusivity in financial services and programs.
- Foster the financial inclusion of women through the support provided to local banks. Encourage MFIs to provide financial products and services that are tailored for them and that provide services to women and address their needs.
- Productive uses of energy in households and communities there is to closing gender gaps across access to services, education, financial products and other services for women.
- Produce assessments of needs of women and men around cooling e.g. around transportation storage may differ and the cost of cooling and income could be enhanced especially for women who are not capturing profits from their businesses or value additions through cooling.
- Produce an assessment of extent to which reduced waste and loss impacts the national food security, particularly for women and children.
- Gather information on existing policies and institutional arrangements for adopting cooling technologies and barriers to accessing finance to purchase products.
- Foster awareness campaigns to promote the use of cooling appliances and services to mitigate the effects of the COVID-19 global pandemic and the access to its vaccine).

**Page 192**

# Improve leadership and decision-making by women

* Include a baseline survey that looks at gender differences in energy user behaviors, perceptions of energy efficiency, and attitudes on energy efficiency among employees and users of buildings.
* Design communication campaigns to identify any potential gender gaps in the area of energy efficiency, such as conducting awareness raising campaigns on energy efficiency, considering the engagement of both men and women and tailor messages based on their different needs and roles in promoting energy efficiency in buildings.
* Assess barriers and understand different motivations for adopting cooling technologies and barriers to accessing finance to purchase products
* Design communication campaign to change consumer patterns and behaviors.

## Strategic Approach

### 5.1 Facility Level:

The Facility will support activities that contribute to closing gender gaps across sub-projects e.g., data on women’s access to finance and sales development coaching, as a priority at every step of the project cycle, the Gender and Energy program of the World Bank Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP) will be available to provide technical assistance and training.

The Facility will encourage the recruitment of a balanced female-to-male employment ratio within the Project Management Unit (PMU) team to the extent possible. The standard staffing model for each project will include at least one female staff member in each country, responsible for implementing and monitoring each project. If needed, PMU staff members may be recruited from the World Bank’s Gender and Energy Program or ESMAP Specialists may receive capacity-building trainings. For each project supported under the Cooling Technologies Sub-Project, the Facility will provide a gender specialist. In addition, the ESMAP Gender and Energy Program will provide complementary support to the Facility projects in the form of technical advice for the Bank’s implementation/supervision of Facility projects.

This will provide support to consider identified gaps (at all gender dimensions) throughout the project cycle, including at the design stage, to ensure that gender equality is effectively addressed. The Facility will also engage with clients and stakeholders through dedicated meetings, to build awareness and sensitivity about the benefits of promoting women’s economic empowerment throughout interventions that foster their employment and their entrepreneurship.

- Clearly align proposed activities with each country's national priorities and gender legal framework, such as community development or female employment,


---
**Page 193**
---

- Identify an institutional gender-focal point to serve as interlocutor during project implementation, if appropriate.
- Offer technical support to help investing in workforce diversification, building on existing internal gender strategies when available. This support may take the form of workshops, training sessions with external experts, engagement with relevant stakeholders, or a review of training plans to better integrate female employees.
- Promote the access of the COVID-19 vaccine in health facilities that address women's needs.

During project design, the private sector can be involved in financing interventions to (i) create income-generating opportunities for women; ii) implement mechanisms to prevent and respond to associated incidents or risks; iii) increase raising awareness rates among female-headed households, for instance through capacity-building workshop, communication or social media campaigns.


### 5.2 Project Level:

Considering the diverse pre-identified projects to be supported by the Facility, as well as the fact that they are at different stages of maturity, there is no single model for all projects.

Data collection to document the baseline situation and identify gaps. Baseline data will be collected to set targets and measure progress during implementation. It will also serve as a reference point against which project results can be evaluated. Research will build upon existing country gender diagnostics and action plan when available, to support the understanding and consideration of national priorities. To support project design and implementation, stakeholders such as women and female participants, to understand men and women differentiated needs. In addition, initial consultations with relevant stakeholders (such as representatives from men and women, through the involvement of gender and energy experts (Environmental and Social Management Unit) will be conducted in order to identify the most appropriate and feasible interventions. This will include a focus on promoting productive uses of energy; enhanced knowledge and skills of small and micro-businesses; and improved access to finance.

- Identifying and implementing relevant actions: Based on the initial diagnosis and inclusive stakeholders' consultations, targeted activities will be identified to address key gender gaps. These may include (i) actions to promote female employment (including through capacity-building for women in energy-related occupations), in the sector such as support for school-to-work transitions; (ii) financing local communities' initiatives that are led by women and/or benefit from women (such as activities generating revenues); (iii) implementation of prevention measures to mitigate gender-based violence, including through awareness-raising campaigns; and (iv) facilitating energy access for female-headed households (including capacity-building for women in energy-related occupations).

- M&E and Knowledge management: In 2017, the WBG started implementing the "gender-lag" mechanism to identify projects that effectively promote equality between men and women and are aligned with the WBG gender strategy. The Cooling Facility will also adopt this approach to ensure that the project is on track in achieving its results. Results will be monitored throughout the project and be used for real-time adjustments during implementation, including through the use of the gender-lag mechanism.


---
**Page 194**

learned from prior projects as well as regional and country base knowledge production - and exchange learning opportunities.

- Engaging stakeholders with specific needs and risks of gender inequality in mainstreaming gender equality in project design and implementation across throughout the project life (as set out in the Stakeholder Engagement Plan). The CoP will ensure that all project activities are designed to promote women's inclusion and voice during consultations. Consultations and public meetings with stakeholders will be conducted throughout the project cycle in a participatory manner and with a special focus on engaging women, youth, people living with HIV/AIDS, gender-related cultural concerns when relevant and including women from minority ethnic groups and other socially vulnerable groups.

### Expected Gender Co-Benefits Resulting from projects:

The Facility includes projects in different countries that are at different stages of development. Three of the projects will be implemented under a World Bank-approved parent project (Kenya, Parana, and Sri Lanka), while the other cooling-related projects supported by the Facility will build on a World Bank-approved parent project (and be governed as subprojects within the parent project), which already includes provisions for addressing gender*^1^. These are expected to generate significant expected gender co-benefits of the early stage projects will be defined during project preparation, along with preliminary gender action plans. The gender co-benefits and gender action plans will be included in the respective Project Appraisal Document.

#### Kenya:

The parent project intends to contribute to closing the relevant gender gaps in Kenya, particularly by (a) increasing women's access to solar products and cleaner and more efficient cookstoves; (b) increasing women's awareness about solar energy use and its benefits; (c) providing women with technical skills for decision-making around the installation and management of the solar systems; (d) providing women with employment opportunities in the solar sector, including through microfinance; and (e) mitigating potential risks such as displacement or gender-based violence (GBV) that women might face due to project implementation. In addition, the project M&E system will adopt and integrate a gender lens to ensure that the project's gender co-benefits are captured during gender-related activities and the project benefits for women and men.

#### Malawi:

The parent project is expected to generate significant benefits for Malawi’s economy. The proposed project aims to increase tax revenues by increasing the tax base, and government revenues through the registration of new businesses and the creation of new jobs. This will be achieved in part by reducing the fiscal burden associated with government-funded SME financing programs. A critical element of this project is the focus on supporting women-owned businesses. Hence, firms will be selected into Stage 1 of the program based on experimental designs that are stratified by age and gender. In addition, training programs will be customized to meet the needs informed by the (gender) diversity of the women-and-women-led firms, with an emphasis on personal initiative skills training. Some of the other topics that will be covered in the training include: business planning; development of socioemotional skills training and informational related to gender-based violence (GBV).

^1 In the exception it is Suba where the inclusion of gender elements into the design of interventions will be done in the context of the Additional Financing project, including meeting the Bank’s Gender- Corporate commitment (an exception was granted for the Parent project given its emergency nature).


---
**Page 195**
---

returns to education, job readiness, financial literacy, and cross-cutting topics, such as nutrition and civic engagement.

North Macedonia: The parent project will address an employment gap in the energy sector through providing women a targeted training program and increasing awareness about employment opportunities in this sector. The parent project will also engage with industry associations and technical universities to increase awareness and information about the project's offerings, in particular employment opportunities created under technical and design work stations. The project will provide training for women on skills development and entrepreneurship, which will be based on skill requirements needed for technical and design work under the project. The project will also provide a range of services including: (i) provision of training to female women attained skills training activities and certification obtained and (ii) percentage of female women who are employed in the energy sector.

Sao Tome and Principe: In the context of COVID-19, the parent project will continue to support communication campaigns, including messages regarding appropriate care for sick family members, guidance on how to prevent the spread of COVID-19, information about the serious impacts of COVID-19 on caregivers and to disseminate targeted community messages on GBV. The project will ensure that guidelines for pandemic preparedness, response, and planning are in place at all levels of the health system, including clear delineation of shared roles and responsibilities. As such, the project will ensure capture of vaccine coverage data disaggregated by gender and age. In addition, a separate budget line item will be established to be paid to capturing information on the population of female health workers and volunteers vaccinated against COVID-19. The outputs of the training (e.g., knowledge, skills, epidemiology and laboratory diagnostics) will be gender sensitive and the outputs of such trainings will be disseminated in a gender-sensitive manner.

Somalia: The parent project emphasizes addressing persistent gender challenges and monitoring the progress in the gender aspects of the project. First, given high gender disparities in health services as previously noted, the Project will implement a package of GBV services including case identification, counseling, and management, with service providers to track the status of GBV service utilization by women and any other relevant data on GBV. Second, the number of women who have been tracked through the Project's intermediate results indicator (IRI) focused on tracking the number of women who received GBV services will be disaggregated by gender. Third, the project monitors another IRI on the number of outpatient department visit, which is disaggregated to track the number of female visits and the number of male visits. Fourth, the Project contributes to an increase in women's service utilization. The Project Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) will be gender sensitive and will provide a safe space for sexual abuse, harassment, exploitation, and disproportionate use of force among females.

### Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan proposed below is indicative at this stage and may be further amended and adapted according to knowledge gathered during the project inception phase, to reflect local and project context. This gender action plan will cover all activities within the facility and are intended to close gender gaps women face at different levels. Some of the areas on which the facility may focus (but will not be limited to) are:

* **Economic Empowerment:** Women entrepreneurs often lack information about access to finance options in the country. In addition, development of enterprises requires


---
**Page 196**
---

technical assistance support tailored to women’s needs. Partnering with business networks, such as female exporters or associations under chambers of commerce, is key to creating awareness about credit lines among women entrepreneurs. From a consumer perspective, it will be important for the Facility to identify and promote existing technology products and services and the sectors they operate in (e.g., agriculture or transportation).

- **Financial services**—not only accounts, but payments, savings, and credit—and give women some basic tools they need to enhance their livelihoods and economic status. Understanding how women use financial services, what barriers exist for them in adopting technologies, and strategies need to be developed to facilitate women’s participation to the greatest extent possible. The Facility will also seek to identify ways that further reduce access barriers for women. Affordability is a key part of this focus.

**Job creation and skills development**: A significant portion of the financial services will be mapped out as part as job creation and skills development aspects of the Facility. For instance, if women are not able to access credit or savings accounts, they may be in terms of jobs or looking at labor force participation gaps between women and men in the region (e.g., agriculture, construction).

**Consumer/stakeholder Engagement**: Gaining evidence points to significant differences in the needs and wants of women versus men e.g. consumers and entrepreneurs. These differences can be observed across a range of dimensions: how they use energy on a daily basis, how much they are willing and able to adopt cooling technologies, how they access information about products and services, how they respond to campaigns marketing products and services. Differences have also been observed in the online behavior of women versus men. These differences need to be taken into account essential to tailor any efforts toward consumers specific behaviors of men and women.

This project will seek to identify these differences through focus groups, surveys, and written materials as they transmit information related to benefits of cooling-related services, consumption patterns, and so forth. It could also be advisable to leverage women’s networks.

**COVID-19 response and health measures**: Women often lack empowerment and autonomy in decision-making at the household level. This is especially true for women in rural areas. Women must not be excluded from accessing services, as they can act as agents of change within their households. The Facility will seek to identify ways that it can support women to counter the negative effects of the global pandemic.

**Data and gender analysis**: The Facility will collect and analyze collected data and gender-specific information help to assess the project’s results and impacts on gender equality and also help to scale-up refine activities for future interventions.


Available Funding: Funding under Component 1 of the Facility will be dedicated to support gender-informed cooling projects in developing countries. This funding is aligned with the context and needs of each country/project and in line with the Facility’s Gender Action Plan.

This funding is US$20 million per country (i.e., USD 40 million for all four facilities) to support the development of a general framework that will identify relevant gender gaps in countries where barriers women have to access to services associated with cooling. This funding will also allow the Facility to conduct research and provide the opportunity to identify priorities to develop specific activities that will contribute to close gender gaps.

**Beneficiaries**: Men and women are the Facility’s beneficiaries; all activities are gender informed and created in coordination with the IBRD/IDA parent project (where applicable), which will ensure that the projects are designed, implemented, monitored, and evaluated. This funding complements WB-funding dedicated to gender-related activities under each project.

projects, the parent projects are already aligned with the gender-lag WB’s corporate commitments (see footnote 2). WB’s projects that are investing in gender equality issues are focusing in three dimensions: human endowments (health and education); economic opportunities (income generation, access to productive assets); and voice and agency (freedom from violence, political participation etc.). The World Bank Gender Strategy (WBGS) is a comprehensive framework for the WB’s projects addressing socially determined gender inequalities, which are rooted in behaviors, institutions, and market forces. Women and men are heterogeneous groups (that are also stratified by race, ethnicity, disability, age, religion, sexual orientation, geographic location, migratory status, etc.). The WB understands that most inequalities based on gender have political roots and therefore the WB’s projects targeting these inequalities and closing gender gaps are planning to target and improve women's status by working with relevant stakeholders to empower women.

# Activities Indicators and Targets Timeline Responsibilities

## Impact Statement:
The Cooling Facility will follow a systematic approach and provide support to individual projects interventions to contribute to closing gender gaps with a focus on data collection, policy dialogue, capacity building and training and skills development activities. The expected impact includes enhanced female economic participation in cooling-related sectors, increased women's access to services (energy, cooling, financial), and enhanced productivity and income for female-led businesses.

## Outcome Statement:
The Facility will focus on 4 key areas: a) generating sector specific country-level data collection that looks at gaps between women and men; b) policy dialogue activities in the sector and gender-based issues to close gender gaps; c) project and program support to promote gender equality in the projects and programs supported by the facility and d. exchange and learning opportunities.

## Implementation of the Facility:
Number of direct beneficiaries: Total 50 million Number of women: 4.1 million (81%)

Executing Entities with the support of the WB Gender Specialist will be responsible to implement and report on the activities

## Output(s) Statement:
Output 1: Gender Analysis Sector specific country-level data collection that looks at gaps between women and men
Men and women have different motivations and priorities about the use of energy services and adoption of energy technologies and products and make decisions based on their own needs. The gender analysis will be conducted to identify these differences. In addition, information-gathering activities can provide useful input to policy and program design. In addition, a focus on gender gaps with regards to access to finance, entrepreneurship and market opportunities.

To contribute to close a knowledge gender gap for the cooling-related sectors in each country by conducting a gender-sensitive gender assessment that develops an understanding of the differences between women and men in terms of access to cooling-related services/products, access to finance and employment, health services, including those associated directly or indirectly with cooling. The results from these gender assessments will be presented and discussed at country-level multi-stakeholder events (e.g., consultations), and will help develop recommendations on

Output 3: Project support to promote gender equality in the projects that are supported by the Facility

At the project level, gender support will be provided (in coordination with/led by the World Bank staff and/or carried out by consultants) at the design and implementation phases. The proposed general approach that will be followed is outlined below:

To assist projects to meet the WB's corporate commitments of the gender equality agenda, the following are some indicators that aim to close gender gaps in the cooling-related sectors and are aligned with vertical logic of the project:
- To assess barriers and constraints: by identifying barriers that need to be addressed in order to promote gender equality. This includes policies and programs that directly address female labour issues in the cooling-related sectors, down to main level (e.g., ministry, utility, institutional) and sub-national level (e.g., district, municipality, regional bank) HR processes.
- To engage with key stakeholders: through organizing events/consultation activities. Relevant stakeholders (e.g. policy makers, private/government institutions, civil society organizations, etc.) will be engaged (including in the COVID-19 vaccination process and in reduction of GBV).
- To identify and address gender gaps: Possible actions include e.g. enhance the productive uses of energy by women; increase access to finance for women; increasing the knowledge and skills of small and micro-business, households and farmers on how to use newfound access for profitable enterprise or addressing barriers to female labour participation in the cooling sector (including the health sector and its cold chain for vaccines and medicines).

Summary

This knowledge brief is the first such analysis of the gender-based impacts of a lack of access to cooling. It examines the gender-related challenges that should be considered, analyzed and addressed to ensure cooling technologies are designed in ways that promote equitable access.

The purpose of this guide is to provide practitioners in the field of energy efficiency with an overview of the opportunities available to integrate behavior change strategies in demand-side energy efficiency programs. The guide provides a framework for integrating behavior change principles into energy efficiency programs, considering both technical and behavioral considerations. While behavior change principles can be applied to energy efficiency programs in various sectors (public, commercial, industrial, residential, and transportation), this guide centers particularly on the residential sector.

Approximately 1.2 billion people will need to gain access to electricity to achieve universal access by 2030. The only way to achieve this is through a combination of the main grid extension, mini grids, and off-grid solar. This report shows that about half a billion people can be cost-effectively provided with electricity through mini grids. The report provides an overview of the market for mini grids in developing countries and identifies how governments have made modern mini grids a scalable option to complement grid extension and solar home systems. The report is the first comprehensive analysis of the mini grid market, providing insights into the current state of play. Industry analyses in detail the solar-hybrid mini grid costs and technological innovations, shows the importance of introducing enabling measures and mechanisms during the scaling process; and provides policy makers, investors and developers with insights on how mini grids can be scaled up.

# Gender Equality in the Geothermal Energy Sector-Road to Sustainability (ESMAP 2019)

The report is a primer on advancing gender equality in the geothermal energy sector. Based on good practices and lessons learned, it introduces ways that geothermal projects can mitigate risks and pursue opportunities to advance gender equality. These include: (i) changes to access to land and natural resource use; (ii) changes to employment and economic patterns; and (iii) changes to the environment and health.

# Key Points for Gender Equality in Energy Access, Energy Policy, Renewable Energy Generation, Transmission and Distribution (World Bank 2019c)

Getting to Gender Equity in Energy Infrastructure: A Guide for Renewable Energy Generation, Transmission, and Distribution Projects (ESMAP 2018)

Short one-pagers provide hands-on, practical guidance to help task teams close gender gaps in energy operations. They address four energy subsectors—energy access, energy policy, renewable energy, and transmission and distribution—and provide entry points for achieving objectives of the Gender Strategy.

# Integrating Gender Considerations into Energy Operations (World Bank 2013)

Gender Equality in Agriculture: A Guidebook (World Bank, FAO, IFAD 2009)

The Cost of the Gender Gap in Agricultural Productivity—in Malawi, Tanzania and Uganda (World Bank, UN Women, UNEP, UNDP 2015)

The report examines the social and gender footprint of large-scale electricity generation, transmission, and distribution projects. It provides a guide for research and replication of good practices can be built. The main impact pathways analyses are access to land and labor markets, along with social sustainability considerations, including health and education.

Provides project teams with basic tools for integrating gender considerations into energy sector activities with country examples and a focus on monitoring and evaluation.

Gender Equality in Agriculture: A Guidebook (World Bank, FAO, IFAD 2009) provides practical operational guidance on how to design agriculture-for-development strategies that capitalize effectively on the unique properties of agricultural growth and rural development involving women and as a high-growth sector. The guidebook is based on lessons learned from the field and draws upon the associated principles have the potential to make a difference in the lives of hundreds of millions of rural poor.

The report provides a unique quantification of the costs in terms of lost growth opportunities and an estimate of the economic benefits that could be achieved by closing the gender gap. The findings are striking and send a strong signal to policy makers in Africa as well as development partners that closing the gender gap is smart economics.

## Gender in fisheries and aquaculture - Module 13 (World Bank - https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/GENAGRL/VSUBOOK/Resources#issue13.pdf)

This Module details investments that address livelihood problems arising from the ongoing changes in production systems, marketing, and technology in the fisheries and aquaculture sector and examines investments that reflect the gender dimensions of these changes. It also explores how changing roles and responsibilities within households, the value attached to work done by women, and women's limited access to essential resources such as ponds, new technologies, and markets, affect their ability to participate fully in the development process. The Module discusses the factors of inequality and femininity that determine who can and should do what and are visible in local communities, in institutions serving these communities, and in the way many national and international organizations operate.

## Gender Data Portal (World Bank - https://data.worldbank.org/gender)

The Gender Data Portal is the World Bank Group's comprehensive source for the latest sex-disaggregated data on gender equality. It provides access to a wide range of indicators that measure women’s participation in economic, social, and public life and decision-making, and agency.
